## A Corner in Lines / 垄断罚抄生意

===Original===
Of all the useless and irritating things in this world, lines are probably the most useless and the most irritating. In fact, I only know of two people who ever got any good out of them. Dunstable, of Day's, was one, Linton, of Seymour's, the other. For a portion of one winter term they flourished on lines. The more there were set, the better they liked it. They would have been disappointed if masters had given up the habit of doling them out.

Dunstable was a youth of ideas. He saw far more possibilities in the routine of life at Locksley than did the majority of his contemporaries, and every now and then he made use of these possibilities in a way that caused a considerable sensation in the school.

In the ordinary way of school work, however, he was not particularly brilliant, and suffered in consequence. His chief foe was his form-master, Mr. Langridge. The feud between them had begun on Dunstable's arrival in the form two terms before, and had continued ever since. The balance of points lay with the master. The staff has ways of scoring which the school has not. This story really begins with the last day but one of the summer term. It happened that Dunstable's people were going to make their annual migration to Scotland on that day, and the Headmaster, approached on the subject both by letter and in person, saw no reason why﻿—the examinations being over﻿—Dunstable should not leave Locksley a day before the end of term.

===Chinese===
世间无用又恼人的东西林林总总，罚抄（lines）多半要算其中之最——既最无用，也最恼人。说实在的，我只知道曾有两个人从罚抄里得着过好处：一个是戴家（Day's）的邓斯特布尔（Dunstable），另一个是西摩家（Seymour's）的林顿（Linton）。某年冬季学期里有一段日子，他们俩就靠着罚抄欣欣向荣。布置得越多，他们越开心。哪位老师若是戒掉了批发罚抄的习惯，他们准要大失所望。

邓斯特布尔是个满脑子主意的少年。在洛克斯利（Locksley）按部就班的校园生活里，他比大多数同龄人看出了多得多的可乘之机，并且隔三差五开发利用一回，回回都在全校引起不小的轰动。

然而在寻常功课上，他并不见得怎么出色，也因此吃了苦头。他的头号对头是班主任兰格里奇先生（Mr. Langridge）。两人的积怨始于两个学期前邓斯特布尔初入此班之时，此后绵延不绝。论比分，优势在老师一边——教职员自有学生学不来的一套得分手段。这个故事真正开场，是在夏季学期结束前的倒数第二天。碰巧那天邓斯特布尔家里要照例北迁苏格兰，校长先收着信、后又见着人，两番恳求之下，觉得既然考试已毕，实在没有理由不准邓斯特布尔提前一天离开洛克斯利。

===Original===
He called Dunstable to his study one night after preparation.

“Your father has written to me, Dunstable,” he said, “to ask that you may be allowed to go home on Wednesday instead of Thursday. I think that, under the special circumstances, there will be no objection to this. You had better see that the matron packs your boxes.”

“Yes, sir,” said Dunstable. “Good business,” he added to himself, as he left the room.

When he got back to his own den, he began to ponder over the matter, to see if something could not be made out of it. That was Dunstable's way. He never let anything drop until he had made certain that he had exhausted all its possibilities.

===Chinese===
一天晚上，晚自习过后，校长把邓斯特布尔叫到了书房。

「你父亲给我来信了，邓斯特布尔，」他说，「问我能否准你周三就回家，不用捱到周四。我想，情势特殊，此事不会有什么妨碍。你最好知会舍监太太一声，让她替你收拾行李。」

「好的，先生。」邓斯特布尔说。「妙极。」离开房间时，他心里给自己补了一句。

回到自己的小窝，他便开始盘算这件事，看看能不能从中变出点什么名堂。邓斯特布尔为人向来如此：不把一件事的油水榨到最后一滴，他绝不撒手。

===Original===
Just before he went to bed he had evolved a neat little scheme for scoring off Mr. Langridge. The knowledge of his plans was confined to himself and the Headmaster. His dorm-master would imagine that he was going to stay on till the last day of term. Therefore, if he misbehaved himself in form, Mr. Langridge would set him lines in blissful ignorance of the fact that he would not be there next day to show them up. At the beginning of the following term, moreover, he would not be in Mr. Langridge's form, for he was certain of his move up.

He acted accordingly.

He spent the earlier part of Wednesday morning in breaches of the peace. Mr. Langridge, instead of pulling him up, put him on to translate; Dunstable went on to translate. As he had not prepared the lesson and was not an adept at construing unseen, his performance was poor.

===Chinese===
上床前一刻，他已酝酿出一套回敬兰格里奇先生的漂亮小计。知情的只有他和校长两人。宿舍导师只会当他要待到学期最后一天。因此，只消他在班上作乱，兰格里奇先生便会全然无知无觉地罚他抄行，浑然不知第二天他根本不在场、无从交卷。再者，下学期开学他也不在兰格里奇班上了——升级十拿九稳。

他当即付诸行动。

周三上午的前半晌，他都用来扰乱课堂治安。兰格里奇先生没有当场纠弹，反而叫他起来翻译；邓斯特布尔便翻译。功课没有预习过，即席解读陌生拉丁文又非其所长，他的表演惨淡得很。

===Original===
After a minute and a half, the form-master wearied.

“Have you looked at this, Dunstable?” he asked.

There was a time-honoured answer to this question.

“Yes, sir,” he said.

===Chinese===
一分半钟后，班主任倦了。

「这一课你看过了吗，邓斯特布尔？」他问。

对这个问题，有一个世代相传的标准答案。

「看过了，先生。」他答。

===Original===
Public-school ethics do not demand that you should reply truthfully to the spirit of a question. The letter of it is all that requires attention. Dunstable had *looked* at the lesson. He was looking at it then. Masters should practise exactness of speech. A certain form at Harrow were in the habit of walking across a copy of a Latin author before morning-school. They could then say with truth that they “had been over it.” This is not an isolated case.

“Go on,” said Mr. Langridge.

Dunstable smiled as he did so.

Mr. Langridge was annoyed.

“What are you laughing at? What do you mean by it? Stand up. You will write out the lesson in Latin and English, and show it up to me by four this afternoon. I know what you are thinking. You imagine that because this is the end of the term you can do as you please, but you will find yourself mistaken. Mind﻿—by four o'clock.”

At four o'clock Dunstable was enjoying an excellent tea in Green Street, Park Lane, and telling his mother that he had had a most enjoyable term, marred by no unpleasantness whatever. His holidays were sweetened by the thought of Mr. Langridge's baffled wrath on discovering the true inwardness of the recent episode.

===Chinese===
公学的伦理并不要求你顺着问题的精神实质据实作答，须要注意的只是字面。邓斯特布尔确实「看」过这一课——此刻就正看着。老师们说话理应讲求精确。哈罗公学（Harrow）有一个班，素有晨课前从一册拉丁作家的书上踩过去的习惯，如此便可如实宣称他们「过」了这一课。此非孤例。

「接着翻。」兰格里奇先生说。

邓斯特布尔一边翻，一边绽出微笑。

兰格里奇先生恼了。

「你笑什么？这是什么意思？站起来。你把这一课用拉丁文和英文抄写一遍，今天下午四点前交来。我知道你在打什么主意。你当学期一到尾巴上就能随心所欲了，很快你就会发现自己想错了。记住——四点整。」

到了四点钟，邓斯特布尔正在公园巷（Park Lane）绿街的家中享用一顿精美茶点，并向母亲汇报：本学期过得愉快之极，未受任何不快之累。一想到兰格里奇先生识破近日这桩事变的真正内幕之后那无处发泄的雷霆之怒，他的假期便又甜上几分。

---

===Original===
When he returned to Locksley at the beginning of the winter term, he was at once made aware that that episode was not to be considered closed. On the first evening, Mr. Day, his housemaster, sent for him.

“Well, Dunstable,” he said, “where is that imposition?”

Dunstable affected ignorance.

===Chinese===
冬季学期开学回到洛克斯利，他立刻便醒悟：那桩事变并没有被视为了结。开学头一晚，舍监戴先生（Mr. Day）便遣人叫他去。

「我说，邓斯特布尔，」他开口，「罚课（imposition）呢？」

邓斯特布尔装糊涂。

===Original===
“Please, sir, you set me no imposition.”

“No, Dunstable, no.” Mr. Day peered at him gravely through his spectacles. “*I* set you no imposition; but Mr. Langridge did.”

Dunstable imitated that eminent tactician, Br'er Rabbit. He “lay low and said nuffin.”

“Surely,” continued Mr. Day, in tones of mild reproach, “you did not think that you could take Mr. Langridge in?”

===Chinese===
「回先生，您没有罚过我课呀。」

「是啊，邓斯特布尔，是没有。」戴先生隔着眼镜严肃地端详他。「*我*是没罚你的课；罚你课的是兰格里奇先生。」

邓斯特布尔效法那位杰出的战术家兔大哥（Br'er Rabbit），「趴得低低的，啥也不说」。

「你总不至于以为，」戴先生用温和责备的口气接着说，「你能把兰格里奇先生蒙混过去吧？」

===Original===
Dunstable rather thought he *had* taken Mr. Langridge in; but he made no reply.

“Well,” said Mr. Day. “I must set you some punishment. I shall give the butler instructions to hand you a note from me at three o'clock tomorrow.” (The next day was a half-holiday.) “In that note you will find indicated what I wish you to write out.”

Why this comic-opera secret-society business, Dunstable wondered. Then it dawned upon him. Mr. Day wished to break up his half-holiday thoroughly.

That afternoon Dunstable retired in disgust to his study to brood over his wrongs; to him entered Charles, his friend, one C. J. Linton, to wit, of Seymour's, a very hearty sportsman.

===Chinese===
邓斯特布尔倒觉得自己*已经*把兰格里奇先生蒙混过去了；但他一言不发。

「好吧，」戴先生说，「总得给你一点惩处。我会吩咐管家，明天三点钟交你一张我的条子。」（次日是个半日假。）「条子上会写明，我要你抄写什么。」

搞这套轻歌剧式的秘密结社排场，所为何来？邓斯特布尔暗自纳闷。随即他恍然大悟：戴先生是要把他的半日假搅个粉碎。

当天下午，邓斯特布尔满怀厌恶地退守书房，细数自己的冤屈；他的朋友查尔斯翩然而入——此君全名C. J. 林顿，西摩家的人士，一位极其热忱的运动健将。

===Original===
“Good,” said Linton. “Didn't think I should find you in. Thought you might have gone off somewhere as it's such a ripping day. Tell you what we'll do. Scull a mile or two up the river and have tea somewhere.”

“I should like to awfully,” said Dunstable, “but I'm afraid I can't.”

And he explained Mr. Day's ingenious scheme for preventing him from straying that afternoon.

“Rot, isn't it,” he said.

===Chinese===
「巧了，」林顿说，「还当扑个空呢。今儿天气这么棒，只当你溜到哪儿去了。这么着，咱们划船往上游荡个一两英里，寻个地方喝茶去。」

「我一千个想去，」邓斯特布尔说，「可惜去不成。」

接着，他把戴先生那套防止他当天下午远走高飞的妙计解释了一番。

「可恶，是不是。」他说。

===Original===
“Beastly. Wouldn't have thought old Day had it in him. But I'll tell you what,” he said. “Do the impot now, and then you'll be able to start at three sharp, and we shall get in a good time on the river. Day always sets the same thing. I've known scores of chaps get impots from him, and they all had to do the Greek numerals. He's mad on the Greek numerals. Never does anything else. You'll be as safe as anything if you do them. Buck up, I'll help.”

They accordingly sat down there and then. By three o'clock an imposing array of sheets of foolscap covered with badly-written Greek lay on the study table.

“That ought to be enough,” said Linton, laying down his pen. “He can't set you more than we've done, I should think.”

“Rummy how alike our writing looks,” said Dunstable, collecting the sheets and examining them. “You can hardly tell which is which even when you know. Well, there goes three. My watch is slow, as it always is. I'll go and get that note.”

===Chinese===
「恶心透了。真想不到老戴还有这份心机。不过我有个主意，」他说，「现在就把罚课（impot）做掉，三点一敲你就能动身，咱们在河上还能痛痛快快玩一场。老戴罚来罚去就那一套。经他罚过课的家伙我见得多了，个个都是抄希腊数字。他对希腊数字痴得发狂，别的从不罚。你抄那个保管万无一失。麻利点，我帮你。」

两人当即坐下开工。到三点钟，书房桌上已经摊开壮观的一大叠大页书写纸，上头涂满歪歪扭扭的希腊文。

「该够了吧，」林顿搁下笔，「他总不能罚得比咱们写的还多，我想。」

「怪了，咱俩的字长得真像，」邓斯特布尔把纸页拢起来端详，「心里明明白白，也难分哪张是谁的。好，三点到了。我的表照例偏慢。我去取条子。」

===Original===
Two minutes later he returned, full of abusive references to Mr. Day. The crafty pedagogue appeared to have foreseen Dunstable's attempt to circumvent him by doing the Greek numerals on the chance of his setting them. The imposition he had set in his note was ten pages of irregular verbs, and they were to be shown up in his study before five o'clock. Linton's programme for the afternoon was out of the question now. But he loyally gave up any other plans which he might have formed in order to help Dunstable with his irregular verbs. Dunstable was too disgusted with fate to be properly grateful.

“And the worst of it is,” he said, as they adjourned for tea at half-past four, having deposited the verbs on Mr. Day's table, “that all those numerals will be wasted now.”

“I should keep them, though,” said Linton. “They may come in useful. You never know.”

===Chinese===
两分钟后他回来了，满口对戴先生的恶语相向。这位老谋深算的教育家，似乎早料到邓斯特布尔会赌他照旧罚希腊数字、从而抢先把希腊数字备好以逸待劳。条子上定的罚课是十页不规则动词，五点前须送交他的书房。林顿下午的节目这下全盘告吹。但他还是忠心耿耿地撇开自己可能有的种种安排，留下来帮邓斯特布尔对付不规则动词。邓斯特布尔对命运怨气冲天，连像样的道谢都没顾上。

「最惨的是，」四点半，两人把动词交上戴先生的桌子后转去喝茶，他说道，「那一堆数字如今全白写了。」

「要我说，留着吧，」林顿说，「兴许日后用得上。世事难料。」

---

===Original===
Towards the end of the second week of term Fate, by way of compensation, allowed Dunstable a distinct stroke of luck. Mr. Forman, the master of his new form, set him a hundred lines of Virgil, and told him to show them up next day. To Dunstable's delight, the next day passed without mention of them; and when the day after that went by, and still nothing was said, he came to the conclusion that Mr. Forman had forgotten all about them.

Which was indeed the case. Mr. Forman was engaged in editing a new edition of the “Bacchae,” and was apt to be absentminded in consequence. So Dunstable, with a glad smile, hove the lines into a cupboard in his study to keep company with the Greek numerals which he had done for Mr. Day, and went out to play fives with Linton.

Linton, curiously enough, had also had a stroke of luck in a rather similar way. He told Dunstable about it as they strolled back to the houses after their game.

“Bit of luck this afternoon,” he said. “You remember Appleby setting me a hundred-and-fifty the day before yesterday? Well, I showed them up today, and he looked through them and chucked them into the wastepaper basket under his desk. I thought at the time I hadn't seen him muck them up at all with his pencil, which is his usual game, so after he had gone at the end of school I nipped to the basket and fished them out. They were as good as new, so I saved them up in case I get any more.”

===Chinese===
开学第二周将尽，命运之神大概是想补偿补偿，赐给邓斯特布尔一注明显的鸿运。新班级的班主任福曼先生（Mr. Forman）罚他抄一百行维吉尔（Virgil），命他次日呈交。令邓斯特布尔大喜的是，第二天过去，无人提起；又一天过去，仍无下文。他于是断定，福曼先生早把这事忘了个精光。

事实果真如此。福曼先生正忙于编订新版《酒神的伴侣》（Bacchae），心不在焉属于职业病。于是邓斯特布尔喜笑颜开，把那沓行数抛进书房的小柜，让它们与替戴先生备下的希腊数字作伴，然后出门找林顿打墙手球（fives）去了。

说来也巧，林顿也交了一注路数颇为相似的鸿运。打完球往各宿舍遛达回去的路上，他把这事讲给邓斯特布尔听。

「今儿下午走了运，」他说，「前天阿普尔比先生（Appleby）罚我一百五十行，记得吧？喏，今天我交了上去，他从头翻过一遍，随手就丢进了他桌底下的字纸篓。我当时就留意到，他压根没用铅笔在上头乱画——那是他的老把戏——于是放学他走后，我猫到篓子边上，把那批行数又钓了出来。完好如新，我就存了起来，以备日后再领罚抄。」

===Original===
Dunstable hastened to tell of his own good fortune. Linton was impressed by the coincidence.

“I tell you what,” he said, “we score either way. Because if we never get any more lines﻿—”

Dunstable laughed.

“Yes, I know,” Linton went on, “we're bound to. But even supposing we don't, what we've got in stock needn't be wasted.”

===Chinese===
邓斯特布尔连忙把自己的好事也说了一遍。两桩巧事撞在一处，林顿深受触动。

「我说啊，」他说，「咱们横竖都稳赚。因为就算往后再也领不着一行罚抄——」

邓斯特布尔笑了。

「是，我知道，」林顿接着说，「反正早晚还会有的。可就算真没有，咱们囤下的这些货也犯不着糟蹋。」

===Original===
“I don't see that,” said Dunstable. “Going to have 'em bound in cloth and published? Or were you thinking of framing them?”

“Why, don't you see? Sell them, of course. There are dozens of chaps in the school who would be glad of a few hundred lines cheap.”

“It wouldn't work. They'd be spotted.”

“Rot. It's been done before, and nobody said anything. A chap in Seymour's who left last Easter sold all his stock lines by auction on the last day of term. They were Virgil mostly and Greek numerals. They sold like hot cakes. There were about five hundred of them altogether. And I happen to know that every word of them has been given up and passed all right.”

===Chinese===
「这话我就看不明白了，」邓斯特布尔说，「打算拿去布面精装出版？还是想装裱起来挂墙？」

「嗨，还不明白？当然是卖呀。学校里巴不得廉价进几百行罚抄的家伙，一抓一大把。」

「行不通。会被人瞧出来。」

「胡扯。早有人干过，谁也没说半个字。西摩家一个上复活节离校的家伙，学期最后一天把全部存货罚抄来了场拍卖。大多是维吉尔和希腊数字，抢购一空，总共约莫五百行。而我碰巧知道，那批货一字不落全都交上去过了关。」

===Original===
“Well, I shall keep mine,” said Dunstable. “I am sure to want all the lines in stock that I can get. I used to think Langridge was fairly bad in the way of impots, but Forman takes the biscuit easily. It seems to be a sort of hobby of his. You can't stop him.”

But it was not until the middle of preparation that the great idea flashed upon Dunstable's mind.

It was the simplicity of the thing that took his breath away. That and its possibilities. This was the idea. Why not start a Lines Trust in the school? An agency for supplying lines at moderate rates to all who desired them? There did not seem to be a single flaw in the scheme. He and Linton between them could turn out enough material in a week to give the Trust a good working capital. And as for the risk of detection when customers came to show up the goods supplied to them, that was very slight. As has been pointed out before, there was practically one handwriting common to the whole school when it came to writing lines. It resembled the movements of a fly that had fallen into an inkpot, and subsequently taken a little brisk exercise on a sheet of foolscap by way of restoring the circulation. Then, again, the attitude of the master to whom the lines were shown was not likely to be critical. So that everything seemed in favour of Dunstable's scheme.

===Chinese===
「那好，我的归我留着，」邓斯特布尔说，「存货自然多多益善，这我有数。从前我当兰格里奇罚课已算够狠，谁知福曼轻松摘魁。罚课简直是他的一大癖好，拦都拦不住。」

不过，直到晚自习过半，那个伟大的念头才在邓斯特布尔脑海中电光石火般一闪。

让他屏息的是这主意的简单——简单，加上前景无量。主意是这样的：何不在学校里创办一个罚抄托拉斯？一家以公道价格向凡有此需求之士供应罚抄的代办行？这方案简直挑不出一丝破绽。他跟林顿一个礼拜的产量，就够给托拉斯攒下一笔可观的流动资本。至于客户拿货交差时被识破的风险，微乎其微。前文已经指出，一写到罚抄，全校实际上通行着同一种笔迹——其状酷似一只失足落进墨水瓶的苍蝇，随后爬上一张大页书写纸，做了一阵舒筋活血的小快步运动。再者，收罚抄的老师也未必摆得出批评的眼光。如此看来，万事皆有利于邓斯特布尔的宏图。

===Original===
Linton, to whom he confided it, was inclined to scoff at first, but when he had had the beauties of the idea explained to him at length, became an enthusiastic supporter of the scheme.

“But,” he objected, “it'll take up all our time. Is it worth it? We can't spend every afternoon sweating away at impots for other people.”

“It's all right,” said Dunstable, “I've thought of that. We shall need to pitch in pretty hard for about a week or ten days. That will give us a good big stock, and after that if we turn out a hundred each every day it will be all right. A hundred's not much fag if you spread them over a day.”

Linton admitted that this was sound, and the Locksley Lines Supplying Trust, Ltd., set to work in earnest.

===Chinese===
林顿听他吐露机密，起初嗤之以鼻，待那主意的种种妙处被掰开揉碎讲了个通透，便一跃成为方案的热忱拥趸。

「不过，」他提出异议，「这得把咱们的时间全搭进去。值当吗？总不能天天下午替别人挥汗赶罚课吧。」

「不碍事，」邓斯特布尔说，「这个我想过了。只需发狠苦干个七八上十天，攒下老大一笔存货；往后每人每天产一百行就成。一百行摊在一整天里，算不得苦役。」

林顿承认此论在理，于是「洛克斯利罚抄供应托拉斯有限公司」（Locksley Lines Supplying Trust, Ltd.）郑重开张。

===Original===
It must not be supposed that the Agency left a great deal to chance. The writing of lines in advance may seem a very speculative business; but both Dunstable and Linton had had a wide experience of Locksley masters, and the methods of the same when roused, and they were thus enabled to reduce the element of chance to a minimum. They knew, for example, that Mr. Day's favourite imposition was the Greek numerals, and that in nine cases out of ten that would be what the youth who had dealings with him would need to ask for from the Lines Trust. Mr. Appleby, on the other hand, invariably set Virgil. The oldest inhabitant had never known him to depart from this custom. For the French masters extracts from the works of Victor Hugo would probably pass muster.

A week from the date of the above conversation, everyone in the school, with the exception of the prefects and the sixth form, found in his desk on arriving at his form-room a printed slip of paper. (Spiking, the stationer in the High Street, had printed it.) It was nothing less than the prospectus of the new Trust. It set forth in glowing terms the advantages offered by the agency. Dunstable had written it﻿—he had a certain amount of skill with his pen﻿—and Linton had suggested subtle and captivating additions. The whole presented rather a striking appearance.

The document was headed with the name of the Trust in large letters. Under this came a number of “scare headlines” such as:

> See what you save!
> No more worry!
> Peace, perfect peace! why do lines when we do them for you?

Then came the real prospectus:

> The Locksley Lines Supplying Trust, Ltd. has been instituted to meet the growing demand for lines and other impositions. While there are masters at our public schools there will always be lines. At Locksley the crop of masters has always flourished﻿—and still flourishes﻿—very rankly, and the demand for lines has greatly taxed the powers of those to whom has been assigned the task of supplying them. It is for the purpose of affording relief to these that the Lines Trust has been formed. It is proposed that all orders for lines shall be supplied out of our vast stock. Our charges are moderate, and vary between threepence and sixpence per hundred lines. The higher charge is made for Greek impositions, which, for obvious reasons, entail a greater degree of labour on our large and efficient staff of writers. All orders, which will be promptly executed, should be forwarded to Mr. P. A. Dunstable, 6 College Grounds, Locksley, or to Mr. C. J. Linton, 10 College Grounds, Locksley. *Payment must be enclosed with order, or the latter will not be executed.* Under no conditions will notes of hand or cheques be accepted as legal tender. There is no trust about us except the name. Come in your thousands. We have lines for all. If the Trust's stock of lines were to be placed end to end it would reach part of the way to London. “You pay the threepence. We do the rest.”

===Chinese===
可别以为这家代办行把成败押给运气。预写罚抄看似一桩极投机的买卖，但邓斯特布尔和林顿对洛克斯利诸位老师的脾性——以及他们被惹毛之后的手段——阅历极深，足以把偶然因素压到最低。譬如他们知道，戴先生最偏爱的罚课是希腊数字，与他打过交道的少年，十之八九要向罚抄托拉斯订购的正是此项。阿普尔比先生那边，则一律罚维吉尔。连校中资历最老的老人，也未见他破过此例。至于法语老师，维克多·雨果（Victor Hugo）著作选段大约便可蒙混过关。

上述谈话一周之后，全校上下——级长与六年级诸君除外——人人走进教室，都在书桌里发现一张印刷的单页。（高街那位文具商斯派金（Spiking）承印。）不偏不倚，正是新托拉斯的招股章程（prospectus）。它以热烈绚烂的辞藻，历数了本代办行提供的种种便利。文章出自邓斯特布尔手笔——他那支笔颇有些功力——林顿又贡献了几处机敏动人的增补。整体观之，气象相当夺目。

文件顶端，是托拉斯的大号名头。名头之下是若干条「惊悚标题」，例如：

> 瞧瞧您省下的工夫！
> 再无烦恼缠身！
> 平安，完全的平安！罚抄何必亲力亲为——有我们替您代劳！

接着才是章程正文：

> 洛克斯利罚抄供应托拉斯有限公司，为满足与日俱增的罚抄及其他罚课需求而设立。只要公学里还有老师，罚抄便永不绝迹。在洛克斯利，老师这一作物素来长势——且至今长势——极其繁茂，罚抄之需求，早已令奉命供应此物的诸君不堪重负。罚抄托拉斯之成立，正是为解此辈于倒悬。凡订购罚抄，均自我方浩瀚存货拨付。收费公道，每百行三便士至六便士不等。希腊文罚课收取较高一档，盖因显而易见之故，此项须令我方规模宏大、效率卓著的抄写团队多付几分辛劳。所有订单均将火速办理，请径寄洛克斯利学院坪（College Grounds）6 号P. A. 邓斯特布尔先生，或洛克斯利学院坪 10 号C. J. 林顿先生。*订货务须随函附款，否则恕不办理。*本公司在任何情况下均不承兑期票与支票。本公司除了名号里那个「托拉斯」（trust），概不讲「信任」。诸君请成千上万地涌来。罚抄现货，供应大众。若把本托拉斯的存货首尾相接，能一路排到去伦敦的中途。「您付三便士，余下的我们来。」

===Original===
Then a blank space, after which came a few “unsolicited testimonials”:

> “Lower Fifth” writes: “I was set two hundred lines of Virgil on Saturday last at one o'clock. Having laid in a supply from your agency I was enabled to show them up at five minutes past one. The master who gave me the commission was unable to restrain his admiration at the rapidity and neatness of my work. You may make what use of this you please.”
> “Dexter's House” writes: “Please send me one hundred (100) lines from *Aeneid, Book Two*. Mr. Dexter was so delighted with the last I showed him that he has asked me to do some more.”
> “Enthusiast” writes: “Thank you for your Greek numerals. Day took them without blinking. So beautifully were they executed that I can hardly believe even now that I did not write them myself.”

===Chinese===
再往下是一段空白，其后是几则「不请自来的顾客感言」：

> 「下五班」（Lower Fifth）来函：「上周六一点整，我被罚维吉尔两百行。幸而从贵行购得现货，一点零五分即行呈交。颁下此项差事的老师，对本人作业之神速与整洁叹赏不能自已。此函任凭随意使用。」
> 「德克斯特宿舍」（Dexter's House）来函：「请寄《埃涅阿斯纪》（*Aeneid*）第二卷的罚抄一百（100）行。德克斯特先生对我上次呈交的那批大为心折，已嘱我再做若干。」
> 「热心人」（Enthusiast）来函：「承蒙惠寄希腊数字，谢谢。戴先生收下时眼皮都没眨一下。那活儿做得实在漂亮，害我至今难信不是我亲笔写的。」

---

===Original===
There could be no doubt about the popularity of the Trust. It caught on instantly.

Nothing else was discussed in the form-rooms at the quarter to eleven interval, and in the houses after lunch it was the sole topic of conversation. Dunstable and Linton were bombarded with questions and witticisms of the near personal sort. To the latter they replied with directness, to the former evasively.

“What's it all *about*?” someone would ask, fluttering the leaflet before Dunstable's unmoved face.

“You should read it carefully,” Dunstable would reply. “It's all there.”

===Chinese===
托拉斯之走红，毋庸置疑——一夜之间风行全校。

十点三刻的课间，各教室里别无他话；午饭后回到宿舍，它是唯一的话题。邓斯特布尔和林顿被连番轰炸，问题与近于人身攻击的俏皮话齐飞。对俏皮话，他们直球回敬；对问题，则王顾左右而言他。

「这到底是*干什么*的？」总有人把传单抖在邓斯特布尔那张纹丝不动的脸前。

「你该细细研读，」邓斯特布尔便答道，「全写在那上头了。」

===Original===
“But what are you playing at?”

“We tried to make it clear to the meanest intelligence. Sorry you can't understand it.”

While at the same time Linton, in his form-room, would be explaining to excited inquirers that he was sorry, but it was impossible to reply to their query as to who was running the Trust. He was not at liberty to reveal business secrets. Suffice it that there the lines were, waiting to be bought, and he was there to sell them. So that if anybody cared to lay in a stock, large or small, according to taste, would he kindly walk up and deposit the necessary coin?

But here the public showed an unaccountable disinclination to deal. It was gratifying to have acquaintances coming up and saying admiringly: “You are an ass, you know,” as if they were paying the highest of compliments﻿—as, indeed, they probably imagined that they were. All this was magnificent, but it was not business. Dunstable and Linton felt that the whole attitude of the public towards the new enterprise was wrong. Locksley seemed to regard the Trust as a huge joke, and its prospectus as a literary *jeu d'esprit*.

In fact, it looked very much as if﻿—from a purely commercial point of view﻿—the great Lines Supplying Trust was going to be what is known in theatrical circles as a frost.

For two whole days the public refused to bite, and Dunstable and Linton, turning over the stacks of lines in their studies, thought gloomily that this world is no place for original enterprise.

===Chinese===
「可你们到底在玩什么把戏？」

「我们已力求让智力最不济者也能看懂。阁下看不懂，深表遗憾。」

与此同时，林顿在自己教室里也正向激动的问询者们解释：他很抱歉，关于托拉斯由何人运营一节，恕难奉复。商业机密，不便泄露。只需知道：罚抄就在那里，待价而沽；他也在那里，专职售卖。诸位如有意囤货——多少不拘，各随口味——可否劳驾移步近前，缴付必要的硬币？

然而此时，公众却表现出一种莫名其妙的交易冷淡。相熟的同学凑上前来，赞慕道：「你是头蠢驴，知道吧。」仿佛在致以最高规格的恭维——他们八成也自认为确乎如此，这自然令人快慰。这一切蔚为壮观，可惜不是生意。邓斯特布尔和林顿感到，公众对这家新企业的整体态度全然不对。洛克斯利似乎把托拉斯当成天大的笑话，把它的章程当成一篇文学性的谐趣妙笔（jeu d'esprit）。

事实上，纯从商业角度观之，伟大的罚抄供应托拉斯眼看就要沦为戏剧圈行话里所谓的「冷场货」了。

整整两天，公众连钩都不肯咬。邓斯特布尔和林顿翻检着书房里成堆的罚抄存货，阴郁地想：这个世界，实在容不下独创的买卖。

===Original===
Then things began to move.

It was quite an accident that started them. Jackson, of Dexter's, was teaing with Linton, and, as was his habit, was giving him a condensed history of his life since he last saw him. In the course of this he touched on a small encounter with M. Gaudinois which had occurred that afternoon.

“So I got two pages of *Quatre-Vingt Treize* to write,” he concluded, “for doing practically nothing.”

All Jackson's impositions, according to him, were given him for doing practically nothing. Now and then he got them for doing literally nothing﻿—when he ought to have been doing form-work.

“Done 'em?” asked Linton.

“Not yet; no,” replied Jackson. “More tea, please.”

===Chinese===
随后，局面动了起来。

点火的纯属偶然。德克斯特家的杰克逊（Jackson）正在林顿处喝茶，且照老习惯，向主人扼要汇报别后以来的人生简史。其间提到当天下午他与戈迪努瓦先生（M. Gaudinois）的一场小型遭遇战。

「于是我就领了两页《九三年》（*Quatre-Vingt Treize*）要抄，」他总结道，「罪名嘛，基本是啥也没干。」

照杰克逊的说法，他领的每回罚课，罪名基本都是啥也没干。偶尔也有一回，罪名是字面意义上的啥也没干——在本该做功课的时候。

「抄完了？」林顿问。

「还没呢。」杰克逊答道，「劳驾，再添点茶。」

===Original===
“What you want to do, then,” said Linton, “is to apply to the Locksley Lines Supplying Trust. That's what you must do.”

“You needn't rot a chap on a painful subject,” protested Jackson.

“I wasn't rotting,” said Linton. “Why don't you apply to the Lines Trust?”

“Then do you mean to say that there really is such a thing?” Jackson said incredulously. “Why I thought it was all a rag.”

“I know you did. It's the rotten sort of thing you would think. Rag, by Jove! Look at this. Now do you understand that this is a genuine concern?”

He got up and went to the cupboard which filled the space between the stove and the bookshelf. From this resting-place he extracted a great pile of manuscript and dumped it down on the table with a bang which caused a good deal of Jackson's tea to spring from its native cup on to its owner's trousers.

===Chinese===
「那么你要做的，」林顿说，「就是向洛克斯利罚抄供应托拉斯申请供货。非此不可。」

「哪壶不开提哪壶，犯不着这么消遣人。」杰克逊抗议。

「我没消遣你，」林顿说，「你怎么就不向罚抄托拉斯申请呢？」

「这么说，那玩意儿竟是真有其事？」杰克逊将信将疑，「我还当纯属一场胡闹呢。」

「我就知道你会这么想。你这种人脑子里想出来的净是这种烂念头。胡闹，天晓得！你瞧瞧这个。现在你总该明白，这是一家如假包换的正经买卖了吧？」

他起身走向那只塞在炉子与书架之间的柜子，从这处安身之所抽出一大摞手稿，砰地墩上桌面，震得杰克逊杯中的茶水纷纷跃出故土，大半飞抵其物主的裤腿。

===Original===
“When you've finished,” protested Jackson, mopping himself with a handkerchief that had seen better days.

“Sorry. But look at these. What did you say your impot was? Oh, I remember. Here you are. Two pages of *Quatre-Vingt Treize*. I don't know which two pages, but I suppose any will do.”

Jackson was amazed.

“Great Scott! what a wad of stuff! When did you do it all?”

“Oh, at odd times. Dunstable's got just as much over at Day's. So you see the Trust is a jolly big show. Here are your two pages. That looks just like your scrawl, doesn't it? These would be fourpence in the ordinary way, but you can have 'em for nothing this time.”

“Oh, I say,” said Jackson gratefully, “that's awfully good of you.”

===Chinese===
「你倒是有完没完。」杰克逊抗议着，用一块早已家道中落的手帕擦拭自身。

「抱歉抱歉。可你瞧瞧这些。你说你的罚课是什么来着？哦，想起来了。给你——《九三年》两页。不知道是哪两页，不过我想哪两页都使得。」

杰克逊惊呆了。

「我的老天！好大一沓！这都是什么时候写的？」

「哦，零零碎碎的工夫呗。邓斯特布尔在戴家那边存着一模一样的一大摞。所以你明白了，咱们托拉斯是了不得的大买卖。喏，你的两页。瞧着跟你那笔鬼画符一模一样，是不是？照规矩这个该收四便士，这回白送。」

「哎呀，我说，」杰克逊感激不尽，「你真够意思。」

===Original===
After that the Locksley Lines Supplying Trust, Ltd. went ahead with a rush. The brilliant success which attended its first specimen﻿—M. Gaudinois took Jackson's imposition without a murmur﻿—promoted confidence in the public, and they rushed to buy. Orders poured in from all the houses, and by the middle of the term the organisers of the scheme were able to divide a substantial sum.

“How are you getting on round your way?” asked Linton of Dunstable at the end of the sixth week of term.

“Ripping. Selling like hot cakes.”

“So are mine,” said Linton. “I've almost come to the end of my stock. I ought to have written some more, but I've been a bit slack lately.”

“Yes, buck up. We must keep a lot in hand.”

“I say, did you hear that about Merrett in our house?” asked Linton.

===Chinese===
打那以后，洛克斯利罚抄供应托拉斯有限公司一路狂飙。头件样品大获全胜——戈迪努瓦先生收下杰克逊的罚课，一声没吭——公众信心大增，蜂拥抢购。订单自各宿舍倾泻而来，到学期过半，方案的两位创办人已能分掉一笔可观的钱款。

「你那一头生意如何？」第六周周末，林顿问邓斯特布尔。

「红火得很。热饼出炉，一抢而空。」

「我这边也一样，」林顿说，「存货快见底了。本该再多写些，近来有点犯懒。」

「那可得加把劲了。手头务必囤足。」

「我说，咱宿舍梅里特（Merrett）那档子事你听说了吗？」林顿问。

===Original===
“What about him?”

“Why, he tried to start a rival show. Wrote a prospectus and everything. But it didn't catch on a bit. The only chap who bought any of his lines was young Shoeblossom. He wanted a couple of hundred for Appleby. Appleby was on to them like bricks. Spotted Shoeblossom hadn't written them, and asked who had. He wouldn't say, so he got them doubled. Everyone in the house is jolly sick with Merrett. They think he ought to have owned up.”

“Did that smash up Merrett's show? Is he going to turn out any more?”

“Rather not. Who'd buy 'em?”

It would have been better for the Lines Supplying Trust if Merrett had not received this crushing blow and had been allowed to carry on a rival business on legitimate lines. Locksley was conservative in its habits, and would probably have continued to support the old firm.

As it was, the baffled Merrett, a youth of vindictive nature, brooded over his defeat, and presently hit upon a scheme whereby things might be levelled up.

===Chinese===
「他怎么了？」

「嗨，他想另立山头，办个对台买卖。章程都写好了，一应俱全。可惜半点没人捧场。买他罚抄的，只有小舒布洛瑟姆（Shoeblossom）一个，要两百行，是交阿普尔比的差。阿普尔比盯得死紧，一眼看穿不是舒布洛瑟姆亲笔，追问出自谁手。他死不开口，罚数便翻了一倍。全宿舍都对梅里特憋着一肚子火，觉得他本该挺身认账。」

「这一下砸了梅里特的台子？他还出不出货？」

「出才怪。谁还买他的？」

倘若梅里特未曾挨这毁灭性的一击、得以循正当路子经营他的竞争买卖，对罚抄供应托拉斯反倒是件好事。洛克斯利习性保守，多半会继续捧老字号的场。

然而事已至此，败下阵来的梅里特——此少年天性记仇——对着自己的失利郁郁盘桓，不久便悟出一条扳回局面的计策。

===Original===
One afternoon, shortly before lockup, Dunstable was surprised by the advent of Linton to his study in a bruised and dishevelled condition. One of his expressive eyes was closed and blackened. He also wore what is known in ring circles as a thick ear.

“What on earth's up?” inquired Dunstable, amazed at these phenomena. “Have you been scrapping?”

“Yes﻿—Merrett﻿—I won. What are you up to﻿—writing lines? You may as well save yourself the trouble. They won't be any good.” Dunstable stared.

“The Trust's bust,” said Linton.

He never wasted words in moments of emotion.

“What!”

===Chinese===
一天下午，锁门熄灯前不久，林顿光临邓斯特布尔的书房，其状令主人大吃一惊：青一块紫一块，衣衫不整。他那双富于表情的眼睛，一只已闭拢且泛出乌青，另佩戴拳击圈内所谓的「肿耳朵」一枚。

「这究竟是怎么了？」邓斯特布尔目睹如此奇观，不禁动问，「跟人干架了？」

「嗯——梅里特——我赢了。你在忙什么呢——抄行呢？趁早省了这份力气吧，抄了也白抄。」邓斯特布尔瞪目结舌。

「托拉斯砸锅了。」林顿说。

他在情绪激动的时刻从不浪费言辞。

「什么！」

===Original===
“﻿‘Bust’ was what I said. That beast Merrett gave the show away.”

“What did he do? Surely he didn't tell a master?”

“Well, he did the next thing to it. He hauled out that prospectus, and started reading it in form. I watched him do it. He kept it under the desk and made a foul row, laughing over it. Appleby couldn't help spotting him. Of course, he told him to bring him what he was reading. Up went Merrett with the prospectus.”

“Was Appleby sick?”

“I don't believe he was, really. At least, he laughed when he read the thing. But he hauled me up after school and gave me a long jaw, and made me take all the lines I'd got to his house. He burnt them. I had it out with Merrett just now. He swears he didn't mean to get the thing spotted, but I knew he did.”

“Where did you scrag him!”

===Chinese===
「『砸锅』，我就是这么说的。梅里特那畜生把咱们的台子给卖了。」

「他干了什么？总不见得跑去向老师告密了吧？」

「那倒没有，就差一步。他把那张章程掏了出来，上课时读开了。我亲眼瞧着他干的。他把章程压在课桌底下，读得咯咯怪笑，闹出好一阵乌烟瘴气。阿普尔比想不发现他都难。当然，便命令他把正在读的东西呈上去。梅里特就捧着章程上了讲台。」

「阿普尔比火了吗？」

「我看倒真没有。起码他读那玩意儿的时候笑了。可放学后他把我提了去，好一通长篇训诫，又命我把手头全部罚抄送到他家。他把它们烧了。方才我跟梅里特把这笔账算清了。他赌咒发誓，说不是故意让事情败露的，可我心里明镜似的：就是故意的。」

「你在哪儿修理的他！」

===Original===
“In the dormitory. He chucked it after the third round.”

There was a knock at the door.

“Come in,” shouted Dunstable.

Buxton appeared, a member of Appleby's house.

“Oh, Dunstable, Appleby wants to see you.”

“All right,” said Dunstable wearily.

===Chinese===
「宿舍里。打到第三回合，他就撂挑子了。」

敲门声响起。

「进来。」邓斯特布尔吼道。

来者巴克斯顿（Buxton），阿普尔比宿舍的一员。

「喂，邓斯特布尔，阿普尔比找你。」

「好吧。」邓斯特布尔疲惫地说。

===Original===
Mr. Appleby was in facetious mood. He chaffed Dunstable genially about his prospectus, and admitted that it had amused him. Dunstable smiled without enjoyment. It was a good thing, perhaps, that Mr. Appleby saw the humorous rather than the lawless side of the Trust; but all the quips in the world could not save that institution from ruin.

Presently Mr. Appleby's manner changed. “I am a funny dog, I know,” he seemed to say; “but duty is duty, and must be done.”

“How many lines have you at your house, Dunstable?” he asked.

“About eight hundred, sir.”

“Then you had better write me eight hundred lines, and show them up to me in this room at﻿—shall we say at ten minutes to five? It is now a quarter to, so that you will have plenty of time.”

Dunstable went, and returned five minutes later, bearing an armful of manuscript.

===Chinese===
阿普尔比先生兴致颇诙谐。他和颜悦色地拿那份章程打趣邓斯特布尔，并承认它把自己逗乐了。邓斯特布尔报以微笑，毫无愉悦可言。阿普尔比先生赏识的是托拉斯滑稽的一面而非无法无天的一面，这或许算件好事；然而普天之下的俏皮话加在一起，也救不了这家机构免于倾覆。

少顷，阿普尔比先生神色一变。「我是个逗趣的角色，这我知道，」他仿佛在说，「但职责终归是职责，非尽不可。」

「你宿舍里存着多少行，邓斯特布尔？」他问。

「八百行上下，先生。」

「那么你最好给我写上八百行，送交到这间屋里来——就定在，唔，四点五十，如何？现在四点四十五，你的时间绰绰有余。」

邓斯特布尔去了，五分钟后抱着一怀手稿回来。

===Original===
“I don't think I shall need to count them,” said Mr. Appleby. “Kindly take them in batches of ten sheets, and tear them in half, Dunstable.”

“Yes, sir.”

The last sheet fluttered in two sections into the surfeited wastepaper basket.

“It's an awful waste, sir,” said Dunstable regretfully.

Mr. Appleby beamed.

===Chinese===
「想必不必清点了，」阿普尔比先生说，「劳驾，十张一沓，把它们撕作两半，邓斯特布尔。」

「好的，先生。」

最后一张纸分作两截，飘飘摇摇落进了早已吃撑的字纸篓。

「这浪费得骇人啊，先生。」邓斯特布尔惋惜地说。

阿普尔比先生满面春风。

===Original===
“We must, however,” he said, “always endeavour to look on the bright side, Dunstable. The writing of these eight hundred lines will have given you a fine grip of the rhythm of Virgil, the splendid prose of Victor Hugo, and the unstudied majesty of the Greek Numerals. Good night, Dunstable.”

“Good night, sir,” said the President of the Locksley Lines Supplying Trust, Ltd.

===Chinese===
「不过，」他说，「我们永远应当勉力看到光明的一面，邓斯特布尔。这八百行抄下来，你已把维吉尔的韵律、雨果的锦绣散文、希腊数字那不假雕饰的庄严，牢牢握进了掌心。晚安，邓斯特布尔。」

「晚安，先生。」洛克斯利罚抄供应托拉斯有限公司总裁应道。

## A Shocking Affair / 一桩骇人事件

===Original===

The Bradshaw who appears in the following tale is the same youth who figures as the hero﻿—or villain, label him as you like﻿—of the preceding equally veracious narrative. I mention this because I should not care for you to go away with the idea that a waistcoat marked with the name of Bradshaw must of necessity cover a scheming heart. It may, however, be noticed that a good many members of the Bradshaw family possess a keen and rather sinister sense of the humorous, inherited doubtless from their great ancestor, the dry wag who wrote that monument of quiet drollery, *Bradshaw’s Railway Guide*. So with the hero of my story.

Frederick Wackerbath Bradshaw was, as I have pointed out, my contemporary at St. Austin’s. We were in the same House, and together we sported on the green﻿—and elsewhere﻿—and did our best to turn the majority of the staff of masters into confirmed pessimists, they in the meantime endeavouring to do the same by us with every weapon that lay to their hand. And the worst of these weapons were the end-of-term examination papers. Mellish was our form-master, and once a term a demon entered into Mellish. He brooded silently apart from the madding crowd. He wandered through dry places seeking rest, and at intervals he would smile evilly, and jot down a note on the back of an envelope. These notes, collected and printed closely on the vilest paper, made up the examination questions.

Our form read two authors a term, one Latin and one Greek. It was the Greek that we feared most. Mellish had a sort of genius for picking out absolutely untranslatable passages, and desiring us (in print) to render the same with full notes. This term the book had been Thucydides, Book II, with regard to which I may echo the words of a certain critic when called upon to give his candid opinion of a friend’s first novel, “I dare not say what I think about that book.”

===Chinese===

下面这则故事里出场的布拉德肖（Bradshaw），与前一篇同样信实可靠的故事里担纲主角——或者反派，随你怎么给他贴标签——的，正是同一位少年。我特意交代这一句，因为我不愿诸位从此带上一条成见，以为凡背心上标着「布拉德肖」之名者，胸前必裹着一颗工于算计的心。不过倒也不妨留意：布拉德肖家族的许多成员，都具备一种敏锐而颇为阴损的幽默感，想必得自他们那位伟大祖先的遗传——正是那位冷面滑稽家，写下了《布拉德肖铁路指南》（Bradshaw's Railway Guide）这座静默谐趣的不朽丰碑。本篇的主角，亦复如是。

前文交代过，弗雷德里克·瓦克巴思·布拉德肖（Frederick Wackerbath Bradshaw）是我在圣奥斯汀（St. Austin's）的同期生。我们同属一所宿舍，一同在草坪上——以及其他种种所在——纵情嬉戏，竭尽所能把教职员的大多数改造为死心塌地的悲观主义者；与此同时，他们也调动手头一切兵器，力争在我们身上取得同样战果。这些兵器里最毒的一件，便是学期末考卷。梅利什（Mellish）是我们的班主任，而每学期总有那么一回，梅利什体内要闯进一个魔鬼。他会远离喧嚣的人群，默然郁郁地出神；会行经干旱之地寻求安息；隔三差五，还要阴恻恻一笑，往信封背面记上一笔。这些零星札记收集拢来，用最恶劣的纸张密密排印，便构成了考题。

我们班每学期攻读两位作家，一位拉丁文，一位希腊文。最叫我们胆寒的是希腊文。专挑绝对译不出来的段落，再（以铅字）嘱我们连同详尽注释一并译出——在这上头，梅利什颇有几分天才。这学期的书目是修昔底德（Thucydides）第二卷。关于此书，恕我借用某位批评家的原话——他被人请去就朋友的第一部小说发表坦率见解时，如此作答：「那本书，我是不敢说出自己的想法的。」

===Original===

About a week before the commencement of the examinations, the ordinary night-work used to cease, and we were supposed, during that week, to be steadily going over the old ground and arming ourselves for the approaching struggle. There were, I suppose, people who actually did do this, but for my own part I always used to regard those seven days as a blessed period of rest, set apart specially to enable me to keep abreast of the light fiction of the day. And most of the form, so far as I know, thought the same. It was only on the night before the examination that one began to revise in real earnest. One’s methods on that night resolved themselves into sitting in a chair and wondering where to begin. Just as one came to a decision, it was bedtime.

“Bradshaw,” I said, as I reached page 103 without having read a line, “do you know any likely bits?”

===Chinese===

考试开锣前约莫一周，寻常晚自习照例停摆。按理，那一周我们应当稳扎稳打地温习旧课，为迫近的战斗披挂甲胄。想必世上确有照此办理的人；但就我本人而论，那七天向来被我视为一段蒙福的安息期，专为让我跟上层出不穷的通俗小说而设。据我所知，班里大多数人的想法也与我不谋而合。真要动真格温书，总得等到考试前夜。那一晚的温书方法，归结起来就是坐进椅子，琢磨从哪儿下手。刚琢磨出个所以然，就到上床时间了。

「布拉德肖，」我一面翻到第 103 页、一面一个字也没读地问道，「你知道有哪几段有戏吗？」

===Original===

Bradshaw looked up from his book. He was attempting to get a general idea of Thucydides’ style by reading Pickwick.

“What?” he said.

I obliged with a repetition of my remark.

“Likely bits? Oh, you mean for the Thucydides. I don’t know. Mellish never sets the bits any decent ordinary individual would set. I should take my chance if I were you.”

“What are you going to do?”

===Chinese===

布拉德肖从书上抬起眼来。他正试图通过阅读《匹克威克外传》（Pickwick）来把握修昔底德的文风大势。

「什么？」他说。

我殷勤地把话重复了一遍。

「有戏的段落？哦，你是说修昔底德。不知道。梅利什出的段落，从来不是任何体面的正常人会出的那些。我要是你，就听天由命。」

「那你打算怎么办？」

---

===Original===

“I’m going to read Pickwick. Thicksides doesn’t come within a mile of it.”

I thought so too.

“But how about tomorrow?”

“Oh, I shan’t be there,” he said, as if it were the most ordinary of statements.

“Not there! Why, have you been sacked?”

===Chinese===

「我打算接着读《匹克威克外传》。『皮厚底斯』差着它十万八千里呢。」

我也是这个看法。

「可明天怎么办？」

「哦，明天我不在场。」他说，那口气仿佛在陈述天下最寻常不过的一件事。

「不在场！怎么，你被开除了？」

===Original===

This really seemed the only possible explanation. Such an event would not have come as a surprise. It was always a matter for wonder to me *why* the authorities never sacked Bradshaw, or at the least requested him to leave. Possibly it was another case of the ass and the bundles of hay. They could not make up their minds which special misdemeanour of his to attack first.

“No, I’ve not been sacked,” said Bradshaw.

A light dawned upon me.

“Oh,” I said, “you’re going to slumber in.” For the benefit of the uninitiated, I may mention that to slumber in is to stay in the House during school on a pretence of illness.

===Chinese===

这似乎真是唯一讲得通的解释。真出了这样的事，谁也不会吃惊。校方竟从未开除过布拉德肖，至少也从没请他卷铺盖走人，个中缘由始终令我纳罕。说不定又是一桩「驴子与草垛」的公案——他们拿不定主意，该先拿他哪一桩具体罪行开刀。

「不，我没被开除。」布拉德肖说。

我心里透进一线灵光。

「哦，」我说，「你是打算装病卧床。」为照顾不明就里的读者，容我补一句：所谓装病卧床（slumber in），就是托病不上课，赖在宿舍里。

===Original===

“That,” replied the man of mystery, with considerable asperity, “is exactly the silly rotten kid’s idea that would come naturally to a complete idiot like you.”

As a rule, I resent being called a complete idiot, but this was not the time for asserting one’s personal dignity. I had to know what Bradshaw’s scheme for evading the examination was. Perhaps there might be room for two in it; in which case I should have been exceedingly glad to have lent my moral support to it. I pressed for an explanation.

“You may jaw,” said Bradshaw at last, “as much as you jolly well please, but I’m not going to give this away. All you’re going to know is that I shan’t be there tomorrow.”

“I bet you are, and I bet you do a jolly rank paper too,” I said, remembering that the sceptic is sometimes vouchsafed revelations to which the most devout believer may not aspire. It is, for instance, always the young man who scoffs at ghosts that the family spectre chooses as his audience. But it required more than a mere sneer or an empty gibe to pump information out of Bradshaw. He took me up at once.

===Chinese===

「那个主意，」这位神秘人物答道，语气相当严厉，「恰恰是只有你这号彻头彻尾的白痴，才会自然而然冒出来的幼稚烂点子。」

按常理，被人唤作彻头彻尾的白痴，我总要动气；但此刻不是伸张个人尊严的时候。我必须打听清楚，布拉德肖逃避考试的锦囊妙计究竟是什么。兴许里头还容得下两个人；若果真如此，我定会欣然为它提供道义上的声援。我加紧追问。

「你爱磨嘴皮子，」布拉德肖末了说，「只管磨个痛快，但这事我绝不外泄。你所能知道的只有一条：明天我不在场。」

「我赌你到场，还赌你交一份糟得可怜的卷子。」我说。我想起一条至理：怀疑论者有时反蒙天启，得窥最虔诚的信徒也无缘一见的机密。譬如说，家宅的幽灵择定听众，专挑那位嗤笑鬼神的年轻人。不过，想从布拉德肖嘴里套出情报，单靠一声冷笑、一句空泛的奚落是不够的。他当即应战。

===Original===

“What’ll you bet?” he said.

Now I was prepared to wager imaginary sums to any extent he might have cared to name, but as my actual worldly wealth at that moment consisted of one penny, and my expectations were limited to the shilling pocket-money which I should receive on the following Saturday﻿—half of which was already mortgaged﻿—it behoved me to avoid doing anything rash with my ready money. But, since a refusal would have meant the downfall of my arguments, I was obliged to name a figure. I named an even sixpence. After all, I felt, I must win. By what means, other than illness, could Bradshaw possibly avoid putting in an appearance at the Thucydides examination?

“All right,” said Bradshaw, “an even sixpence. You’ll lose.”

“Slumbering in barred.”

===Chinese===

「你赌什么？」他说。

虚拟的赌注，他开多大我都奉陪得起；无奈我此刻的实际身家只有一便士，未来的进项也无非是下周六才能领到的一先令零花钱——其中一半早已抵押出去——因此我务必避免拿现钱轻举妄动。然而一口回绝，便等于我的整套论证全线崩塌，我只得报出一个数。我报了对赌六便士。毕竟，我自觉稳操胜券：除了称病，布拉德肖还有什么法子不在修昔底德考试上露面？

「成交，」布拉德肖说，「对赌六便士。你输定了。」

「装病卧床不算数。」

===Original===

“Of course.”

“Real illness barred too,” I said. Bradshaw is a man of resource, and has been known to make himself genuinely ill in similar emergencies.

“Right you are. Slumbering in and real illness both barred. Anything else you’d like to bar?”

I thought.

===Chinese===

「那自然。」

「真生病也不算数。」我说。布拉德肖足智多谋，类似紧急关头把自己弄成真病号，是有前科的。

「依你依你。装病卧床、真生病，双双不算数。还有要排除的项目吗？」

我想了想。

===Original===

“No. Unless﻿—” an idea struck me﻿—“You’re not going to run away?”

Bradshaw scorned to answer the question.

“Now you’d better buck up with your work,” he said, opening his book again. “You’ve got about as long odds as anyone ever got. But you’ll lose all the same.”

It scarcely seemed possible. And yet﻿—. Bradshaw was generally right. If he said he had a scheme for doing﻿—though it was generally for not doing﻿—something, it rarely failed to come off. I thought of my sixpence, my only sixpence, and felt a distinct pang of remorse. After all, only the other day the chaplain had said how wrong it was to bet. By Jove, so he had. Decent man the chaplain. Pity to do anything he would disapprove of. I was on the point of recalling my wager, when before my mind’s eye rose a vision of Bradshaw rampant and sneering, and myself writhing in my chair a crushed and scored-off wreck. I drew the line at that. I valued my self-respect at more than sixpence. If it had been a shilling now﻿—. So I set my teeth and turned once more to my Thucydides. Bradshaw, having picked up the thread of his story again, emitted hoarse chuckles like minute guns, until I very nearly rose and fell upon him. It is maddening to listen to a person laughing and not to know the joke.

===Chinese===

「没有了。除非——」我灵光一现，「你该不会是要跑路吧？」

布拉德肖不屑一答。

「好了，你还是麻利用功去吧。」他说着重新翻开书，「你这便宜占得，古往今来的赌客里数一数二。但你横竖要输。」

这怎么看都不像有戏。然而——。布拉德肖的判断向来靠得住。只要他宣称对某事——虽然多半是某事的不办——胸有成竹，此事便极少失手。我想起我那枚六便士，仅有的六便士，心头分明掠过一阵悔意的刺痛。毕竟，前两天校牧才讲过打赌多么罪过。天哪，确实讲过。校牧是个正派人。干出叫他皱眉的事，未免可惜。我正要收回赌约，眼前却浮起一幅图景：布拉德肖昂然狞笑，而我瘫在椅中辗转，一具被挫尽锐气的残骸。这个我忍不了。我的自尊，身价在六便士之上。倘若是枚先令嘛——。于是我咬紧牙关，重新扎进修昔底德。布拉德肖接上了故事的线索，像报丧炮似的每隔一阵放出一声沙哑的闷笑，笑得我险些奋起扑杀。听人哈哈大笑而不知笑点何在，最能令人抓狂。

===Original===

“You will be allowed two hours for this paper,” said Mellish on the following afternoon, as he returned to his desk after distributing the Thucydides questions. “At five minutes to four I shall begin to collect your papers, but those who wish may go on till ten past. Write only on one side of the paper, and put your names in the top right-hand corner. Marks will be given for neatness. Any boy whom I see looking at his neighbour’s﻿—*where’s Bradshaw?*”

===Chinese===

次日下午，梅利什发完修昔底德考题，踱回讲台，说道：「本场限时两小时。三点五十五分我开始收卷，愿意的也可以写到四点十分。只许写在纸的单面，姓名写在右上角。卷面整洁者计分。谁要是让我看见在偷看邻座的——*布拉德肖呢？*」

===Original===

It was already five minutes past the hour. The latest of the late always had the decency to appear at least by three minutes past.

“Has anybody seen Bradshaw?” repeated Mellish. “You, what’s-your-name﻿—(I am what’s-your-name, very much at your service)﻿—you are in his House. Have you seen him?”

I could have pointed out with some pleasure at this juncture that if Cain expressed indignation at being asked where his brother was, I, by a simple sum in proportion, might with even greater justice feel annoyed at having to locate a person who was no relative of mine at all. Did Mr. Mellish expect me to keep an eye on every member of my House? Did Mr. Mellish﻿—in short, what did he mean by it?

This was what I thought. I said, “No, sir.”

“This is extraordinary,” said Mellish, “most extraordinary. Why, the boy was in school this morning.”

===Chinese===

此刻已过整点五分钟。迟到界的殿后名家，再怎么拖沓也讲点体面，顶多迟到三分钟，必然露面。

「有人见过布拉德肖吗？」梅利什又问一遍。「那位，那个谁——（在下便是『那个谁』，随时听候差遣）——你跟他同一宿舍。你见过他没有？」

此刻我本可以不无快意地指出：倘若该隐被人问起兄弟下落，尚且有理由表示愤慨，那么按一道简单的比例题换算，要我负责定位一个与我毫无亲缘的人，岂不更有充分的权利动怒？梅利什先生莫非指望我盯着宿舍里的每一个人？梅利什先生究竟——简而言之，他这是什么意思？

以上是我的腹诽。我的口头回答是：「没见过，先生。」

「这可稀奇了，」梅利什说，「稀奇之至。要知道，这孩子今天上午还在学校呢。」

---

===Original===

This was true. The boy had been in school that morning to some purpose, having beaten all records (his own records) in the gentle sport of Mellish-baiting. This evidently occurred to Mellish at the time, for he dropped the subject at once, and told us to begin our papers.

Now I have remarked already that I dare not say what I think of Thucydides, Book II. How then shall I frame my opinion of that examination paper? It was Thucydides, Book II, with the few easy parts left out. It was Thucydides, Book II, with special homemade difficulties added. It was﻿—well, in its way it was a masterpiece. Without going into details﻿—I dislike sensational and realistic writing﻿—I may say that I personally was not one of those who required an extra ten minutes to finish their papers. I finished mine at half-past two, and amused myself for the remaining hour and a half by writing neatly on several sheets of foolscap exactly what I thought of Mr. Mellish, and precisely what I hoped would happen to him some day. It was grateful and comforting.

At intervals I wondered what had become of Bradshaw. I was not surprised at his absence. At first I had feared that he would keep his word in that matter. As time went on I knew that he would. At more frequent intervals I wondered how I should enjoy being a bankrupt.

Four o’clock came round, and found me so engrossed in putting the finishing touches to my excursus of Mr. Mellish’s character, that I stayed on in the form-room till ten past. Two other members of the form stayed too, writing with the despairing energy of those who had five minutes to say what they would like to spread over five hours. At last Mellish collected the papers. He seemed a trifle surprised when I gave up my modest three sheets. Brown and Morrison, who had their eye on the form prize, each gave up reams. Brown told me subsequently that he had only had time to do sixteen sheets, and wanted to know whether I had adopted Rutherford’s emendation in preference to the old reading in Question II. My prolonged stay had made him regard me as a possible rival.

===Chinese===

此话不假。那天上午，这孩子在学校里着实颇有建树，在「戏弄梅利什」这项文雅运动中刷新了全部纪录（他自己保持的纪录）。梅利什显然当场也想起了这一层，旋即撇开话题，吩咐我们动笔。

我说过，对修昔底德第二卷，我不敢说出自己的想法。那么对那份考卷，又教我如何措辞呢？它是修昔底德第二卷，删去了寥寥几处浅易段落；它是修昔底德第二卷，添上了特制的家常难题；它是——唔，以它的路数而论，是一件杰作。细节不必展开——我不喜欢耸人听闻的现实主义笔法——只想说，本人并不在需要宽限十分钟完卷者之列。我两点半便告完卷，余下的一个半钟头用来自娱：在几张大页书写纸上工工整整写下我对梅利什先生的全部看法，以及某年某月的某一天，我热切盼望降临到他头上的种种遭遇。此中滋味，令人感恩，令人宽慰。

我时不时纳闷布拉德肖的下落。他的缺席并不令我意外。起初我还唯恐他当真说到做到；随着时间推移，我知道他必然说到做到。我以更高的频率纳闷的是：破产的滋味，我享用起来究竟如何。

四点钟来而复去，我却还埋头在那篇论梅利什先生人格的专题论文上润饰收尾，收得聚精会神，索性在教室里一直坐到四点十分。班里另有两位也没走，以绝望者的爆发力奋笔疾书——只剩五分钟，却要把恨不能铺陈五个钟头的话一气说完。梅利什终于收卷。我交出谦逊的三张纸时，他似乎微感诧异。而盯上班级奖的布朗（Brown）与莫里森（Morrison），各自交出了浩浩几大卷。布朗事后告诉我，他只来得及写十六张，还问我第二题里是不是舍旧读本而采了拉瑟福德（Rutherford）的校勘。我久久不退场，已使他把我视作了潜在的竞争对手。

===Original===

I dwell upon this part of my story, because it has an important bearing on subsequent events. If I had not waited in the form-room I should not have gone downstairs just behind Mellish. And if I had not gone downstairs just behind Mellish, I should not have been in at the death, that is to say the discovery of Bradshaw, and this story would have been all beginning and middle, and no ending, for I am certain that Bradshaw would never have told me a word. He was a most secretive animal.

I went downstairs, as I say, just behind Mellish. St. Austin’s, you must know, is composed of three blocks of buildings, the senior, the middle, and the junior, joined by cloisters. We left the senior block by the door. To the captious critic this information may seem superfluous, but let me tell him that I have left the block in my time, and entered it, too, though never, it is true, in the company of a master, in other ways. There are windows.

===Chinese===

我在这一节上多费笔墨，是因为它与后文关系重大。倘若我没有在教室里盘桓，就不会恰好跟在梅利什身后下楼；倘若没有恰好跟在梅利什身后下楼，我就无缘亲历终局——亦即布拉德肖被发现的一幕——本篇便将有头有腰而无尾，因为我确信，布拉德肖是半个字也不会向我吐露的。他是头口风奇严的动物。

正如前述，我跟在梅利什身后下了楼。须知圣奥斯汀由三座楼群组成——高年级楼、中年级楼、低年级楼——之间以回廊相连。我们走正门离开了高年级楼。挑剔的批评家或许觉得这一交代纯属多余，但容我奉告：想当年，我离开那座楼、也进入过那座楼，走的都是别的路子——只是的确从未偕同老师一起。毕竟，还有窗户嘛。

===Original===

Our procession of two, Mellish leading by a couple of yards, passed through the cloisters, and came to the middle block, where the Masters’ Common Room is. I had no particular reason for going to that block, but it was all on my way to the House, and I knew that Mellish hated having his footsteps dogged. That Thucydides paper rankled slightly.

In the middle block, at the top of the building, far from the haunts of men, is the Science Museum, containing﻿—so I have heard, I have never been near the place myself﻿—two stuffed rats, a case of mouldering butterflies, and other objects of acute interest. The room has a staircase all to itself, and this was the reason why, directly I heard shouts proceeding from that staircase, I deduced that they came from the Museum. I am like Sherlock Holmes, I don’t mind explaining my methods.

===Chinese===

我们这支两人的行进队伍——梅利什领先两三码——穿过回廊，抵达中年级楼，教师公共休息室所在之地。我往那座楼去本无特殊理由，不过反正顺路都在宿舍方向，何况我知道梅利什最恨有人尾随足迹。那份修昔底德考卷，在我心里多少还隐隐作痛。

中年级楼的顶层，人迹罕至之处，坐落着科学博物馆。馆藏——据说是这样，本人从未亲临近旁——有两只剥制标本鼠、一匣霉斑累累的蝴蝶，以及其他种种趣味隽永的展品。那间屋子独享一部楼梯，因此我一听见喊声从那楼梯上传来，当即推断声源便是博物馆。我与夏洛克·福尔摩斯（Sherlock Holmes）是一路人，不介意解说一下自己的方法。

---

===Original===

“Help!” shouted the voice. “Help!”

The voice was Bradshaw’s.

Mellish was talking to M. Gerard, the French master, at the moment. He had evidently been telling him of Bradshaw’s nonappearance, for at the sound of his voice they both spun round, and stood looking at the staircase like a couple of pointers.

===Chinese===

「救命啊！」那声音喊道，「救命啊！」

声音是布拉德肖的。

此刻梅利什正与法语老师热拉尔先生（M. Gerard）交谈。他方才显然正说到布拉德肖缺席一事，因为一听见这嗓音，两人齐刷刷旋过身，双双直勾勾盯住楼梯，活像一对钉住猎物的指示犬。

===Original===

“Help,” cried the voice again.

Mellish and Gerard bounded up the stairs. I had never seen a French master run before. It was a pleasant sight. I followed. As we reached the door of the Museum, which was shut, renewed shouts filtered through it. Mellish gave tongue.

“Bradshaw!”

===Chinese===

「救命啊。」那声音又叫。

梅利什与热拉尔纵身上楼。法语老师奔跑的雄姿，我此前从未见识，令人心旷神怡。我随后跟上。行至博物馆门前——门关着——新一轮呼救又从门内渗出。梅利什放声一吠。

「布拉德肖！」

===Original===

“Yes, sir,” from within.

“Are you there?” This I thought, and still think, quite a superfluous question.

“Yes, sir,” said Bradshaw.

“What are you doing in there, Bradshaw? Why were you not in school this afternoon? Come out at once.” This in deep and thrilling tones.

“Please, sir,” said Bradshaw complainingly, “I can’t open the door.” Now, the immediate effect of telling a person that you are unable to open a door is to make him try his hand at it. Someone observes that there are three things which everyone thinks he can do better than anyone else, namely poking a fire, writing a novel, and opening a door.

===Chinese===

「是，先生。」门内应道。

「你在里面吗？」这个问题，我当时认为、至今仍认为是纯属多余。

「是，先生。」布拉德肖说。

「你在里面干什么，布拉德肖？今天下午为什么不上学？马上出来。」以上词句，以深沉激越的声调发出。

「回先生，」布拉德肖委屈巴巴地说，「我打不开这扇门。」要知道，告知某人你打不开一扇门，立竿见影的效果，便是他非要亲手一试。有位高人指出：世上有三件事，人人自认做得比谁都好——拨火，写小说，还有开门。

===Original===

Gerard was no exception to the rule.

“Can’t open the door?” he said. “Nonsense, nonsense.” And, swooping at the handle, he grasped it firmly, and turned it.

At this point he made an attempt, a very spirited attempt, to lower the world’s record for the standing high jump. I have spoken above of the pleasure it gave me to see a French master run. But for good, square enjoyment, warranted free from all injurious chemicals, give me a French master jumping.

===Chinese===

热拉尔于此定律概莫能外。

「打不开？」他说，「胡说，胡说。」说着猛地扑向门把手，一把攥牢，拧了下去。

就在这节骨眼上，他做了一次尝试——一次气势如虹的尝试——试图改写原地跳高的世界纪录。上文说过，观看法语老师奔跑，于我已是桩乐事。但若论纯正地道的享受——保真无掺假、不含任何有害化学成分的那种——还得请给我看法语老师跳。

===Original===

“My dear Gerard,” said the amazed Mellish.

“I have received a shock. Dear me, I have received a most terrible shock.”

So had I, only of another kind. I really thought I should have expired in my tracks with the effort of keeping my enjoyment strictly to myself. I saw what had happened. The Museum is lit by electric light. To turn it on one has to shoot the bolt of the door, which, like the handle, is made of metal. It is on the killing two birds with one stone principle. You lock yourself in and light yourself up with one movement. It was plain that the current had gone wrong somehow, run amock, as it were. Mellish meanwhile, instead of being warned by Gerard’s fate, had followed his example, and tried to turn the handle. His jump, though quite a creditable effort, fell short of Gerard’s by some six inches. I began to feel as if some sort of round game were going on. I hoped that they would not want me to take a hand. I also hoped that the thing would continue for a good while longer. The success of the piece certainly warranted the prolongation of its run. But here I was disappointed. The disturbance had attracted another spectator, Blaize, the science and chemistry master. The matter was hastily explained to him in all its bearings. There was Bradshaw entombed within the Museum, with every prospect of death by starvation, unless he could support life for the next few years on the two stuffed rats and the case of butterflies. The authorities did not see their way to adding a human specimen (youth’s size) to the treasures in the Museum, *so*﻿—how was he to be got out?

===Chinese===

「我亲爱的热拉尔。」惊愕的梅利什说。

「我遭了电击。天哪，我遭了一记极其可怕的电击。」

我也遭了一记，只是属于另一种。为把满腔的愉悦严格锁在自己一人身上，我几乎当场力竭殉职。事情的原委，我看得明白。博物馆用电灯照明。要开灯，就得推上门闩，而门闩与把手一样，由金属制成。这是本着「一石二鸟」的原则设计的：一个动作，门锁上了，人也把自己照亮了。显然，电流不知哪里出了岔子，简直可以说是发了狂。与此同时，梅利什非但没从热拉尔的下场吸取教训，反而步其后尘，也去拧了把手。他那一跳虽属可圈可点的佳绩，比热拉尔仍短着六英寸上下。我开始觉得，这里头仿佛正进行着某种轮流上场的集体游戏。我希望他们别来拉我入局，也希望这出好戏再多演上好一阵子——以该剧的卖座程度而论，延长档期实属理所当然。但在这里我失望了。骚动又引来一位看客：理科兼化学老师布莱兹（Blaize）。有人把事情的前因后果匆匆向他交代了一番：布拉德肖被封存在博物馆内，前景是十足的饿毙，除非他能靠那两只标本鼠和那匣蝴蝶维持生命，撑过未来数年。校方看不出有什么路数往博物馆的珍藏里再添一件人类标本（少年尺码），*所以*——该怎么把他弄出来呢？

===Original===

The scientific mind is equal to every emergency.

===Chinese===

科学的头脑，应付任何紧急事态都游刃有余。

===Original===

“Bradshaw,” shouted Blaize through the keyhole.

“Sir?”

“Are you there?”

I should imagine that Bradshaw was growing tired of this question by this time. Besides, it cast aspersions on the veracity of Gerard and Mellish. Bradshaw, with perfect politeness, hastened to inform the gentleman that he was there.

“Have you a piece of paper?”

===Chinese===

「布拉德肖。」布莱兹对着锁孔高喊。

「先生？」

「你在里面吗？」

我料想布拉德肖此时对这个问题已然生腻。何况，它还构成了对热拉尔与梅利什二人诚实品格的暗讽。布拉德肖以十足的礼貌，急忙向这位先生保证：他在。

「你身上有纸吗？」

---

===Original===

“Will an envelope do, sir?”

“Bless the boy, anything will do so long as it is paper.”

Dear me, I thought, is it as bad as all that? Is Blaize, in despair of ever rescuing the unfortunate prisoner, going to ask him to draw up a “last dying words” document, to be pushed under the door and despatched to his sorrowing guardian?

“Put it over your hand, and then shoot back the bolt.”

===Chinese===

「信封行吗，先生？」

「老天爷，这孩子，只要是纸，什么都行。」

哎呀，我心想，竟糟到这步田地了？莫非布莱兹对营救这名不幸的囚徒已然绝望，要请他起草一份「临终遗言」，好从门缝底下塞出来，寄交他悲恸的监护人？

「把它包在手上，再把门闩拉回去。」

===Original===

“But, sir, the electricity.”

“Pooh, boy!”

The scientific mind is always intolerant of lay ignorance.

“Pooh, boy, paper is a nonconductor. You won’t get hurt.”

Bradshaw apparently acted on his instructions. From the other side of the door came the sharp sound of the bolt as it was shot back, and at the same time the light ceased to shine through the keyhole. A moment later the handle turned, and Bradshaw stepped forth﻿—free!

===Chinese===

「可是先生，电……」

「呸，孩子！」

科学的头脑，向来容不得门外汉的无知。

「呸，孩子，纸不导电。伤不着你。」

布拉德肖显然遵嘱办理了。门那一边传来门闩拉回的脆响，与此同时，锁孔里透出的灯光应声熄灭。片刻之后把手一转，布拉德肖迈步而出——自由了！

===Original===

“Dear me,” said Mellish. “Now I never knew that before, Blaize. Remarkable. But this ought to be seen to. In the meantime, I had better ask the Headmaster to give out that the Museum is closed until further notice, I think.”

And closed the Museum has been ever since. That further notice has never been given. And yet nobody seems to feel as if an essential part of their life had ceased to be, so to speak. Curious. Bradshaw, after a short explanation, was allowed to go away without a stain﻿—that is to say, without any additional stain﻿—on his character. We left the authorities discussing the matter, and went downstairs.

“Sixpence isn’t enough,” I said, “take this penny. It’s all I’ve got. You shall have the sixpence on Saturday.”

===Chinese===

「哎呀，」梅利什说，「这个我以前可不知道，布莱兹。了不得。不过这毛病得赶紧整治。我想，眼下我还是去请校长宣布一声：博物馆关闭，另行通知。」

而自那以后，博物馆一直是关闭的。那道「另行通知」，始终未曾发布。然而似乎无人觉得，可以这么说，自己生命中某个不可或缺的部分就此消亡。怪哉。布拉德肖略作了一番解释，便获准离开，品格上未沾污点——也就是说，未添新的污点。我们撇下还在讨论此事的校方诸公，下楼去了。

「六便士不够，」我说，「这枚便士你先拿去。我的家底就这些了。六便士周六补上。」

===Original===

“Thanks,” said Bradshaw. “Was the Thucydides paper pretty warm?”

“Warmish. But, I say, didn’t you get a beastly shock when you locked the door?”

“I did the week before last, the first time I ever went to the place. This time I was more or less prepared for it. Blaize seems to think that paper dodge a special invention of his own. He’ll be taking out a patent for it one of these days. Why, every kid knows that paper doesn’t conduct electricity.”

“I didn’t,” I said honestly.

===Chinese===

「多谢。」布拉德肖说，「修昔底德那份卷子，够烫手吧？」

「烫手得很。不过，我说，你锁门那会儿，没挨一记狠的电击吗？」

「上上礼拜挨过一回了，那是我头一回去那地方。这回多多少少有了防备。布莱兹似乎把那手『纸花招』当作了他的独家发明，早晚要去申个专利。嗐，纸不导电这种事，哪个小孩不知道。」

「我就不知道。」我诚实地坦白。

---

===Original===

“You don’t know much,” said Bradshaw, with equal honesty.

“I don’t,” I replied. “Bradshaw, you’re a great man, but you missed the best part of it all.”

“What, the Thucydides paper?” asked he with a grin.

“No, you missed seeing Gerard jump quite six feet.”

===Chinese===

「你知道的本就不多。」布拉德肖以同等的诚实回敬。

「确实不多。」我答道，「布拉德肖，你是位伟人，可惜全场最精彩的部分，你没看着。」

「什么，修昔底德的卷子？」他咧嘴一笑，问道。

「不是，你错过了看热拉尔足足跳起六英尺。」

===Original===

Bradshaw’s face expressed keen disappointment.

“No, did he really? Oh, I say, I wish I’d seen it.”

The moral of which is that the wicked do not always prosper. If Bradshaw had not been in the Museum, he might have seen Gerard jump six feet, which would have made him happy for weeks. On second thoughts, though, that does not work out quite right, for if Bradshaw had not been in the Museum, Gerard would not have jumped at all. No, better put it this way. I was virtuous, and I had the pleasure of witnessing the sight I have referred to. But then there was the Thucydides paper, which Bradshaw missed but which I did not. No. On consideration, the moral of this story shall be withdrawn and submitted to a committee of experts. Perhaps they will be able to say what it is.

===Chinese===

布拉德肖脸上流露出深切的失望。

「不是吧，他真跳了？哎呀，我说，真希望我也看着了。」

由此可见的教训是：恶人未必总是得志。倘若布拉德肖不在博物馆里，他本可以看热拉尔那一记六英尺的纵跳，足够他快活上好几个星期。然而转念一想，这笔账又算不拢：倘若布拉德肖不在博物馆里，热拉尔根本就跳不成。不，不如这样措辞：我是有德的，于是我有幸目睹了上述盛景；但话又说回来，修昔底德的考卷，布拉德肖躲过了，我却没有。不行。经再三考虑，本篇的教训撤回，送交专家委员会审议。或许他们能说出个所以然来。

## L’Affaire Uncle John (A Story in Letters) / 约翰叔叔事件（书信体小说）

### I / 一

===Original===
From Richard Venables, of St. Austin’s School, to his brother Archibald Venables, of King’s College, Cambridge:

> Dear Archie—I take up my pen to write to you, not as one hoping for an answer, but rather in order that (you notice the Thucydidean construction) I may tell you of an event the most important of those that have gone before. You may or may not have heard far-off echoes of my adventure with Uncle John, who has just come back from the diamond-mines—and looks it. It happened thusly:
> Last Wednesday evening I was going through the cricket field to meet Uncle John, at the station, as per esteemed favour from the governor, telling me to. Just as I got on the scene, to my horror, amazement, and disgust, I saw a middle-aged bounder, in loud checks, who, from his looks, might have been anything from a retired pawnbroker to a secondhand butler, sacked from his last place for stealing the sherry, standing in the middle of the field, on the very wicket the Rugborough match is to be played on next Saturday (tomorrow), and digging—*digging*—I’ll trouble you. Excavating great chunks of our best turf with a walking-stick. I was so unnerved, I nearly fainted. It’s bad enough being captain of a School team under any circs, as far as putting you off your game goes, but when you see the wicket you’ve been rolling by day, and dreaming about by night, being mangled by an utter stranger—well! They say a cow is slightly irritated when her calf is taken away from her, but I don’t suppose the most maternal cow that ever lived came anywhere near the frenzy that surged up in my bosom at that moment. I flew up to him, foaming at the mouth. “My dear sir,” I shrieked, “*are* you aware that you’re spoiling the best wicket that has ever been prepared since cricket began?” He looked at me, in a dazed sort of way, and said, “What?” I said: “How on earth do you think we’re going to play Rugborough on a ploughed field?” “I don’t follow, mister,” he replied. A man who calls you “mister” is beyond the pale. You are justified in being a little rude to him. So I said: “Then you must be either drunk or mad, and I trust it’s the latter.” I believe that’s from some book, though I don’t remember which. This did seem to wake him up a bit, but before he could frame his opinion in words, up came Biffen, the ground-man, to have a last look at his wicket before retiring for the night. When he saw the holes—they were about a foot deep, and scattered promiscuously, just where two balls out of three pitch—he almost had hysterics. I gently explained the situation to him, and left him to settle with my friend of the check suit. Biffen was just settling down to a sort of Philippic when I went, and I knew that I had left the man in competent hands. Then I went to the station. The train I had been told to meet was the 5:30. By the way, of course, I didn’t know in the least what Uncle John was like, not having seen him since I was about one-and-a-half, but I had been told to look out for a tall, rather good-looking man. Well, the 5:30 came in all right, but none of the passengers seemed to answer to the description. The ones who were tall were not good looking, and the only man who was good looking stood five feet nothing in his boots. I did ask him if he was Mr. John Dalgliesh; but, his name happening to be Robinson, he could not oblige. I sat out a couple more trains, and then went back to the field. The man had gone, but Biffen was still there. “Was you expecting anyone today, sir?” he asked, as I came up. “Yes. Why?” I said. “That was ’im,” said Biffen. By skilful questioning, I elicited the whole thing. It seems that the fearsome bargee, in checks, was the governor’s “tall, good-looking man”; in other words, Uncle John himself. He had come by the 4:30, I suppose. Anyway, there he was, and I had insulted him badly. Biffen told me that he had asked who I was, and that he (Biffen) had given the information, while he was thinking of something else to say to him about his digging. By the way, I suppose he dug from force of habit. Thought he’d find diamonds, perhaps. When Biffen told him this, he said in a nasty voice: “Then, when he comes back will you have the goodness to tell him that my name is John Dalgliesh, and that he will hear more of this.” And I’m uncommonly afraid I shall. The governor bars Uncle John awfully, I know, but he wanted me to be particularly civil to him, because he was to get me a place in some beastly firm when I leave. I haven’t heard from home yet, but I expect to soon. Still, I’d like to know how I could stand and watch him ruining the wicket for our spot match of the season. As it is, it won’t be as good as it would have been. The Rugborough slow man will be unplayable if he can find one of these spots. Altogether, it’s a beastly business. Write soon, though I know you won’t.
> —Yours ever,
> Dick

===Chinese===
圣奥斯汀学校（St. Austin’s）的理查德·维纳布尔斯（Richard Venables），致剑桥大学国王学院（King’s College, Cambridge）的兄长阿奇博尔德·维纳布尔斯（Archibald Venables）：

> 亲爱的阿奇——我提笔给你写信，并非指望回音，而是为了（注意，这是修昔底德式的句法）向你通报一件大事——已往诸事之中最为重大的一桩。我同约翰叔叔（Uncle John）的那段历险，远方的风声你或许有所耳闻，或许没有。这位叔叔刚从钻石矿回来——瞧那副尊容便一望而知。事情经过如下：
> 上周三傍晚，我正穿过板球场前往车站迎接约翰叔叔——此行乃奉老爷子隆恩俯准、特降恩谕、钦命我前往。刚一到场，我便惊骇、错愕、且恶心地看见：一个身穿花哨格纹的中年粗汉——瞧那副长相，上可以是退休当铺老板，下可以是因偷雪利酒被上家辞退的二手段管家——正站在场地中央，恰好立在下周六（也就是明天）与拉格伯勒（Rugborough）比赛要用的那条球道（wicket）上，在挖——*挖*——尊驾听清楚了，是在挖。拿一根手杖，把我们最好的草皮大块大块地掘将起来。我五内俱焚，险些当场厥过去。无论何种情形，当校队队长这差事本身就够叫人心神不宁的了，可当你亲眼看见自己白天碾平、夜里梦见的球道，被一个彻头彻尾的陌生外人肆意糟蹋——唉！人们说，母牛丢了牛犊会略感愠怒，但我料想，古往今来最有母性的母牛，其怒火也万万不及我彼时胸中翻腾的狂怒。我口吐白沫地朝他飞扑过去。「亲爱的先生，」我尖叫道，「你*可*知道，你正在毁掉自有板球以来保养得最出色的一条球道？」他一脸茫然地望着我，说：「啥？」我说：「你觉得我们怎么可能在一片犁过的田里跟拉格伯勒比赛？」「俺听不懂，mister。」他答道。一个唤你作「mister」的男人，已自绝于文明社会之外，你对他稍加无礼，纯属合乎道义。于是我说：「那么你要么喝醉了，要么疯了，我祈祷是后者。」我记得这话出自某本书，书名忘了。这话似乎确实让他清醒了一点，但还没等他把他的意见组织成言语，场地管理员比芬（Biffen）来了——他就寝前要最后巡看一眼自己的球道。一看见那些坑——约莫一英尺深，东一个西一个，恰恰散布在三个球里有两个要落点的地方——他几乎当场歇斯底里。我温言向他说明了形势，便把他留给我那位格纹西装的朋友去发落。我走时，比芬正准备开讲一篇德摩斯梯尼式的长篇痛斥（Philippic），我心中笃定：此人已托付给胜任的双手。随后我去了车站。奉命要接的是 5:30 那班车。顺带一提，我自然丝毫不知约翰叔叔长什么模样——上回见他，我才一岁半——只奉到指示，要留心一位高个子、相当英俊的先生。5:30 那班倒是准点进了站，可乘客里没有一位对得上号：高个的都不英俊，唯一一位英俊的，穿上靴子也才五英尺整。我确实问过他是不是约翰·达尔格利什先生（Mr. John Dalgliesh）；怎奈他姓罗宾逊（Robinson），爱莫能助。我又干坐等了两班车，然后回到球场。那人已走了，比芬还在。「今儿个您是在等哪位吧，先生？」我走近时他问。「是啊。怎么？」我说。「方才那位，就是『他』嘞。」比芬说。我施展高超的盘问术，套出了全部原委。原来那位花格纹的、驳船夫模样的可怖恶汉，正是老爷子口中的「高个子、相当英俊的先生」；换言之，正是约翰叔叔本人。想必他是坐 4:30 那班来的。总之，人是到了，而我也已经把他得罪了个透。比芬告诉我，那位先生曾问起我是何人，而他（比芬）已如实奉告——彼时他正满脑子琢磨着，就那通乱挖还有什么别的话好跟那人讲。顺便说一句，我猜他挖坑纯属积习使然。没准儿还当真以为能挖出钻石来呢。比芬把这话转告他时，他用一种阴恻恻的嗓音说：「那么，等他回来，可否劳您大驾告知：我名叫约翰·达尔格利什，此事他还会听到下文。」而这「下文」，我怕是万分躲不掉了。我知道，老爷子极其看不上约翰叔叔，却又非要我对他格外客气周到，因为等我离校，还要仰仗他把我安插进某家讨厌的公司。家里尚未来信，不过料想必快。话虽如此，我倒要请教请教：眼看他毁掉我们本季度头一场重头戏的球道，我如何能袖手立在旁边？事已至此，球道再不能恢复本来的样子了。拉格伯勒那位慢速投手，只要摸着一处那样的坑洼，投出来的球就根本没法打。总而言之，这桩事实在可恶之至。速回信，虽然我知道你不会。
> ——你永远的，
> 迪克

### II / 二

===Original===
Telegram from Major-General Sir Everard Venables, V.C., K.C.M.G., to his son Richard Venables:

> Venables, St. Austin’s. What all this about Uncle John. Says were grossly rude. Write explanation next post—Venables.

===Chinese===
埃弗拉德·维纳布尔斯少将爵士（Major-General Sir Everard Venables，维多利亚十字勋章、圣米迦勒及圣乔治勋章得主）致其子理查德·维纳布尔斯的电报：

> 维纳布尔斯，圣奥斯汀。约翰叔叔这究竟是怎么回事。他说你无礼至极。下一班邮次来信解释。——维纳布尔斯

### III / 三

===Original===
Letter from Mrs. James Anthony (née Miss Dorothy Venables) to her brother Richard Venables:

> Dear Dick—What *have* you been doing to Uncle John? Jim and I are stopping for a fortnight with father, and have just come in for the whole thing. Uncle John—*isn’t* he a horrible man?—says you were grossly insolent to him when he went down to see you. *Do* write and tell me all about it. I have heard no details as yet. Father refuses to give them, and gets simply *furious* when the matter is mentioned. Jim said at dinner last night that a conscientious boy would probably feel bound to be rude to Uncle John. Father said “Conscience be—”; I forget the rest, but it was awful. Jim says if he gets any worse we shall have to sit on his head, and cut the traces. He is getting so dreadfully *horsey*. Do write the very minute you get this. I want to know all about it.
> —Your affectionate sister,
> Dorothy

===Chinese===
詹姆斯·安东尼太太（Mrs. James Anthony，闺名多萝西·维纳布尔斯〔Dorothy Venables〕）致其弟理查德·维纳布尔斯的信：

> 亲爱的迪克——你到底对约翰叔叔干了什么呀？吉姆（Jim）和我正在父亲家小住两周，恰好撞上这整出好戏。约翰叔叔——他是不是个可怕的男人？——说你在他下去看你时对他无礼至极。千万千万写信把原委统统告诉我。细节我至今一无所闻：父亲拒绝透露，而且一提这事儿就*勃然大怒*。昨晚吃饭时吉姆说，一个凭良心办事的男孩，多半会觉得对约翰叔叔无礼乃是义不容辞。父亲说：「让良心见——」后半句我忘了，总之难听得很。吉姆说，他要是再这么恶化下去，我们就只好按住他的脑袋、割断他的缰绳了。他如今浑身的马味儿重得吓人。接信即刻回复，一分钟也别耽搁。我要知道全部内情。
> ——爱你的妹妹，
> 多萝西

### IV / 四

===Original===
Part of letter from Richard Venables, of St. Austin’s, to his father Major-General Sir Everard Venables, V.C., K.C.M.G.:

> … So you see it was really his fault. The Emperor of Germany has no right to come and dig holes in our best wicket. Take a parallel case. Suppose some idiot of a fellow (not that Uncle John’s that, of course, but you know what I mean) came and began rooting up your azaleas. Wouldn’t you want to say something cutting? I will apologize to Uncle John, if you like; but still, I do think he might have gone somewhere else if he really wanted to dig. So you see, etc., etc.

===Chinese===
圣奥斯汀的理查德·维纳布尔斯致父亲埃弗拉德·维纳布尔斯少将爵士的信（节选）：

> ……这么说来，实在是他自己的错。德意志皇帝也无权跑来在我们最好的球道上挖坑。且举一个类似的案例：假设有那么个蠢家伙（当然啦，约翰叔叔并非此类，不过您明白我的意思），跑来动手把您的杜鹃花连根刨起，您难道不想讲两句刻薄话？您若愿意，我可以向约翰叔叔道歉；不过说真的，他要实在想挖，也未尝不能另寻别处。由此可见，云云，等等。

### V / 五

===Original===
Letter from Richard Venables, of St. Austin’s, to his sister Mrs. James Anthony:

> Dear Dolly—Thanks awfully for your letter, and thank Jim for his message. He’s a ripper. I’m awfully glad you married him and not that rotter, Thompson, who used to hang on so. I hope the most marvellous infant on earth is flourishing. And now about Uncle John. Really, I am jolly glad I did say all that to him. We played Rugborough yesterday, and the wicket was simply vile. They won the toss, and made two hundred and ten. Of course, the wicket was all right at one end, and that’s where they made most of their runs. I was wicket-keeping as usual, and I felt awfully ashamed of the beastly pitch when their captain asked me if it was the football-field. Of course, he wouldn’t have said that if he hadn’t been a pal of mine, but it was probably what the rest of the team thought, only they were too polite to say so. When we came to bat it was worse than ever. I went in first with Welch—that’s the fellow who stopped a week at home a few years ago; I don’t know whether you remember him. He got out in the first over, caught off a ball that pitched where Uncle John had been prospecting, and jumped up. It was rotten luck, of course, and worse was to follow, for by half-past five we had eight wickets down for just over the hundred, and only young Scott, who’s simply a slogger, and another fellow to come in. Well, Scott came in. I had made about sixty then, and was fairly well set—and he started simply mopping up the bowling. He gave a chance every over as regular as clockwork, and it was always missed, and then he would make up for it with two or three tremendous whangs—a safe four every time. It wasn’t batting. It was more like golf. Well, this went on for some time, and we began to get hopeful again, having got a hundred and eighty odd. I just kept up my wicket, while Scott hit. Then he got caught, and the last man, a fellow called Moore, came in. I’d put him in the team as a bowler, but he could bat a little, too, on occasions, and luckily this was one of them. There were only eleven to win, and I had the bowling. I was feeling awfully fit, and put their slow man clean over the screen twice running, which left us only three to get. Then it was over, and Moore played the fast man in grand style, though he didn’t score. Well, I got the bowling again, and halfway through the over I carted a half-volley into the Pav, and that gave us the match. Moore hung on for a bit and made about ten, and then got bowled. We made 223 altogether, of which I had managed to get seventy-eight, not out. It pulls my average up a good bit. Rather decent, isn’t it? The fellows rotted about a good deal, and chaired me into the Pav, but it was Scott who won us the match, I think. He made ninety-four. But Uncle John nearly did for us with his beastly walking-stick. On a good wicket we might have made any number. I don’t know how the affair will end. Keep me posted up in the governor’s symptoms, and write again soon.
> —Your affectionate brother,
> Dick
> P.S.—On looking over this letter, I find I have taken it for granted that you know all about the Uncle John affair. Probably you do, but, in case you don’t, it was this way. You see, I was going, etc., etc.

===Chinese===
圣奥斯汀的理查德·维纳布尔斯致姐姐詹姆斯·安东尼太太的信：

> 亲爱的多莉——来信感激不尽，也替我谢谢吉姆的口信。他真是个妙人。你嫁的是他、而不是那个从前老缠着你不放的讨厌鬼汤普森（Thompson），我高兴极了。愿天下最神妙的婴孩茁壮成长。现在说约翰叔叔。说实在的，我把那些话统统说了出口，实在高兴得很。昨天我们对阵拉格伯勒，球道简直恶劣透顶。他们掷币获胜，拿下二百一十分。当然，球道有一端还像样，他们的分多半是在那一端得的。我照例担任三柱门守员（wicket-keeping），他们队长问我这是不是足球场的时候，我对这条可恶的球道羞愧难当。当然，他若不是我的朋友，绝不会说这话；但这多半也是他们全队的心声，只是其余诸位客气，不便出口。轮到我们击球时，情形更是雪上加霜。我和韦尔奇（Welch）率先出场——就是几年前在家里住过一个礼拜的那个家伙，不知你还记不记得他。第一个回合（over）他就出局了：一记球恰好落在约翰叔叔「勘探」过的地界，猛然弹起，被他截杀。这自然是倒霉透顶，而更糟的还在后头：到五点半，我们已失八座三柱门，得分刚过百，能上场的只剩纯粹抡大棒的小斯科特（Scott），外加另一名队友。于是斯科特上场。我那时已得六十分上下，站稳了阵脚——而他随即开始把对方的投球横扫一空。他每个回合准点奉送一次机会，回回无人接住；随后便用两三记惊天动地的猛轰找补回来——回回稳取四分。那不叫击球，倒更像打高尔夫。这般光景持续了些时候，我们已得一百八十几分，重又燃起希望。斯科特轰球时，我只管守住自己的三柱门。后来他被截杀，最后一人——一个姓摩尔（Moore）的——上了场。我把他排进队里，原是当投手（bowler）用的，不过他偶尔也能击两下子，所幸今日正是「偶尔」之一。只差十一分即可获胜，此刻面对投球的是我。我状态好极了，接连两记把对方慢速投手的球干干净净送过围屏，只剩三分便可拿下。那一回合打完，摩尔面对快球手应对得气派十足，虽然没得分。好啦，又轮到我迎击投球，回合中途，我把一记半高球轰进了休息亭（Pav），比赛就此到手。摩尔又支撑了片刻，得了十分上下，随后被投杀出局。我们总共得 223 分，其中我个人奋力拿下七十八分，未出局。这把我的平均分拉高了一大截。挺像样的吧？众人起哄了好一阵，还把我高抬进休息亭，不过依我看，赢下比赛的其实是斯科特——他得了九十四分。但约翰叔叔和他那根可恶的手杖，差一点就要了我们的命。球道若是完好，我们要得多少分有多少分。这事如何收场，我尚不得而知。随时向我通报老爷子的病情动向，并速再回信。
> ——爱你的弟弟，
> 迪克
> 又及——检读此信，我发现我已想当然地认定，你对约翰叔叔事件了如指掌。多半你确实知道；万一不知道，事情是这样的：你瞧，我当时正走着，云云，等等。

### VI / 六

===Original===
From Archibald Venables, of King’s College, Cambridge, to Richard Venables, of St. Austin’s:

> Dear Dick—Just a line to thank you for your letter, and to tell you that since I got it I have had a visit from the great Uncle John, too. He *is* an outsider, if you like. I gave him the best lunch I could in my rooms, and the man started a long lecture on extravagance. He doesn’t seem to understand the difference between the ’Varsity and a private school. He kept on asking leading questions about pocket-money and holidays, and wanted to know if my master allowed me to walk in the streets in that waistcoat—a remark which cut me to the quick, “that waistcoat” being quite the most posh thing of the sort in Cambridge. He then enquired after my studies; and, finally, when I saw him off at the station, said that he had decided not to tip me, because he was afraid that I was inclined to be extravagant. I was quite kind to him, however, in spite of everything; but I was glad you had spoken to him like a father. The recollection of it soothed me, though it seemed to worry him. He talked a good deal about it. Glad you came off against Rugborough.
> —Your ever,
> A. Venables

===Chinese===
剑桥大学国王学院的阿奇博尔德·维纳布尔斯，致圣奥斯汀的理查德·维纳布尔斯：

> 亲爱的迪克——匆此数行，一谢来信，二要奉告：自收信以来，那位大名鼎鼎的约翰叔叔也莅临了敝处。这位呀，可真是个登峰造极的*圈外人*。我在自己房间里款待了他力所能及的最好一顿午餐，此人竟就此开启了一场关于奢侈浪费的长篇训诫。他似乎全然不明白大学（’Varsity）与私立学堂的区别。他连珠炮似的就零用钱与假期提出种种诱导性问题，还想知道我的老师是否准许我穿着那件马甲招摇过市——此言深深刺伤了我：那件马甲，可是全剑桥同类货色之中最气派的一件。随后他又垂询我的学业；最后，我在车站送行时，他表示已决定不给我赏钱，因为担心我有挥霍的倾向。尽管如此，我待他还是十分和气；不过，你能像老父亲一般训他一通，我着实欣慰。对那一幕的回忆抚慰了我，虽然它似乎让他颇为烦恼——他就这桩事絮叨了老半天。听闻你们赢了拉格伯勒，甚慰。
> ——你永远的，
> A. 维纳布尔斯

### VII / 七

===Original===
From Mr. John Dalgliesh to Mr. Philip Mortimer, of Penge:

> Dear Sir—In reply to your letter of the 18th inst., I shall be happy to recommend your son, Reginald, for the vacant post in the firm of Messrs Van Nugget, Diomonde, and Mynes, African merchants. I have written them to that effect, and you will, doubtless, receive a communication from them shortly.
> —I am, my dear sir, yours faithfully,
> J. Dalgliesh

===Chinese===
约翰·达尔格利什先生致彭奇（Penge）的菲利普·莫蒂默先生（Mr. Philip Mortimer）：

> 敬启者——本月 18 日大函敬悉。兹欣然奉复：我乐于举荐令郎雷金纳德（Reginald），出任非洲贸易商「范纳格特、迪奥蒙德与迈因斯公司」（Messrs Van Nugget, Diomonde, and Mynes）的空缺职位。我已就此意致函该公司，想必不日您便会收到他们的信函。
> ——我亲爱的先生，您忠实的，
> J. 达尔格利什

### VIII / 八

===Original===
Letter from Richard Venables, of St. Austin’s, to his father Major-General Sir Everard Venables, V.C., K.C.M.G.:

> Dear Father—Uncle John writes, in answer to my apology, to say that no apologies will meet the case; and that he has given his nomination in that rotten City firm of his to a fellow called Mortimer. But rather a decent thing has happened. There is a chap here I know pretty well, who is the son of Lord Marmaduke Twistleton, and it appears that the dook himself was down watching the Rugborough match, and liked my batting. He came and talked to me after the match, and asked me what I was going to do when I left, and I said I wasn’t certain, and he said that, if I hadn’t anything better on, he could give me a place on his estate up in Scotland, as a sort of land-agent, as he wanted a chap who could play cricket, because he was keen on the game himself, and always had a lot going on in the summer up there. So he says that, if I go up to the ’Varsity for three years, he can guarantee me the place when I come down, with a jolly good screw and a ripping open-air life, with lots of riding, and so on, which is just what I’ve always wanted. So, can I? It’s the sort of opportunity that won’t occur again, and you know you always said the only reason I couldn’t go up to the ’Varsity was, that it would be a waste of time. But in this case, you see, it won’t, because he wants me to go, and guarantees me the place when I come down. It’ll be awfully fine, if I may. I hope you’ll see it.
> —Your affectionate son,
> Dick
> P.S.—I think he’s writing to you. He asked your address. I think Uncle John’s a rotter. I sent him a rattling fine apology, and this is how he treats it. But it’ll be all right if you like this land-agent idea. If you like, you might wire your answer.

===Chinese===
圣奥斯汀的理查德·维纳布尔斯致父亲埃弗拉德·维纳布尔斯少将爵士的信：

> 亲爱的父亲——约翰叔叔回信答复我的道歉，说任何道歉都无济于事；并已把他那家伦敦城破公司里的提名名额，给了一个姓莫蒂默的家伙。不过倒是出了件相当妙的事。这儿有个我相当熟的家伙，是马默杜克·特威斯尔顿勋爵（Lord Marmaduke Twistleton）的公子。据说公爵大人（the dook）亲自到场看了对拉格伯勒的比赛，颇为欣赏我的击球。赛后他过来与我攀谈，问我毕业后有何打算，我说尚不确定；他说，若我没有更好的安排，他可以在他苏格兰的庄园里给我谋个职位，算是地产管家（land-agent）之类，因为他正需要一个会打板球的人——他本人酷爱此道，夏天那边总有一大摊子赛事活动。他还说，只要我上三年大学，毕业时那个职位便包在我身上——一份相当可观的薪水，一种精彩纷呈的户外生活，还有大量的骑马等等——这正是我一向梦寐以求的。所以，我能去吗？这种机会一去便不会再有。您也知道，您常说，不让我上大学的唯一缘由，无非是浪费时间。可如今您瞧，这一回并不浪费：他要我去，还担保我毕业便有职位。若蒙俯允，那真是妙极了。望您明察。
> ——爱您的儿子，
> 迪克
> 又及——我想他正打算给您写信，他问过您的地址。依我看，约翰叔叔就是个无赖。我给他寄去一封漂亮至极的道歉信，他竟这样糟蹋。不过，您若中意这地产管家的主意，一切都会顺顺当当。您若愿意，可拍电报作复。

### IX / 九

===Original===
