「是啊。我本该告诉你，我弟弟这学期要来本校。老头子（指校长）、梅雷维尔，还有其余各位当权者，我统统知会了。想不通怎么单单漏了你。不知怎么就滑出了脑子。不过，你待他倒似乎够公道，领他四处转转什么的。真是难为你了。」

哈里森虚弱地笑了笑。小维纳布尔斯咧嘴一乐。在哈里森看来像个谜的是：这对兄弟何以能错开时辰、分抵学校。谜底其实再简单不过：维纳布尔斯家老大假期最后一周是在麦克阿瑟（MacArthur）家里度过的——此公乃圣奥斯汀十五人队队长，走读生一名，「悬」在离学校一英里之内的居所。

「可我想不通的，」维纳布尔斯不依不饶地继续道，「是这桩家具公案。据我所知，上学期末我走得并不仓促。你若要落实，我可以过问一下以确保无误，不过我料你会发现是你弄错了。八成是想到别人身上去了。总之，我们想着你必定最清楚，便把家具统统拖到了走廊里；如今看来是出了点差错，这些物件压根儿就该待在屋里。所以，麻烦你再搬回去，可好？我们本想搭把手，无奈正要出门去铺子买些茶点。你最好赶在我们回来之前弄妥。多谢之至。」

===Original===
Harrison coughed nervously, and rose to a point of order.

“I was going out to tea, too,” he said.

“I’m sorry, but I think you’ll have to scratch the engagement,” said Venables.

Harrison made a last effort.

===Chinese===
哈里森神经质地咳了一声，起立提出程序问题。

「我本来也打算出去喝茶的。」他说。

「很遗憾，我看那个约会你只好取消了。」维纳布尔斯说。

哈里森作了最后的努力。

===Original===
“I’m fagging for Welch this term,” he protested.

It was the rule at St. Austin’s that every fag had the right to refuse to serve two masters. Otherwise there would have been no peace for that downtrodden race.

“That,” said Venables, “ought to be awfully jolly for Welch, don’t you know, but as a matter of fact term hasn’t begun yet. It doesn’t start till tomorrow. Weigh in.”

Various feelings began to wage war beneath Harrison’s Eton waistcoat. A profound disinclination to undertake the suggested task battled briskly with a feeling that, if he refused the commission, things might﻿—nay, would﻿—happen.

===Chinese===
「这学期我在给韦尔奇（Welch）当听差。」他抗议道。

圣奥斯汀有条规矩：每个听差都有权拒绝伺候两个主人。否则，那个受压迫的种族便永无宁日。

「那件事，」维纳布尔斯说，「想必让韦尔奇乐不可支，你说是吧？不过呢，实际上学期还没开始，明天才开学。开工吧。」

种种情绪在哈里森的伊顿马甲底下开起战来。对这桩提议差事的深切不愿，同另一种感觉激烈交锋：他若拒绝这项委托，事情可能——不，必定——发生。

===Original===
“Harrison,” said Venables gently, but with meaning, as he hesitated, “do you know what it is to wish you had never been born?”

And Harrison, with a thoughtful expression on his face, picked up a photograph from the floor, and hung it neatly in its place over the mantelpiece.

===Chinese===
他迟疑的当口，「哈里森，」维纳布尔斯柔声道，却字字有分量，「你可知道什么叫但愿自己从未出生？」

于是哈里森面带若有所思的神情，从地板上拾起一张相片，端端正正挂回壁炉上方的原处。

## How Payne Bucked Up / 佩恩是怎样振作起来的

### I / 一

===Original===
It was Walkinshaw’s affair from the first. Grey, the captain of the St. Austin’s Fifteen, was in the infirmary nursing a bad knee. To him came Charles Augustus Walkinshaw with a scheme. Walkinshaw was football secretary, and in Grey’s absence acted as captain. Besides these two there were only a couple of last year’s team left—Reade and Barrett, both of Philpott’s House.

“Hullo, Grey, how’s the knee?” said Walkinshaw.

“How’s the team getting on?” he said.

===Chinese===
这件事从一开头就是沃金肖的手笔。圣奥斯汀十五人队（the Fifteen）的队长格雷（Grey），正在医务室里将养一条伤得不轻的膝盖。查尔斯·奥古斯塔斯·沃金肖（Charles Augustus Walkinshaw）带着一个方案来见他。沃金肖是橄榄球干事，格雷缺阵期间由他代理队长。除这二位之外，去年阵容里留下的只剩一对——里德（Reade）和巴雷特（Barrett），同属菲尔波特家（Philpott's House）。

「哈啰，格雷，膝盖怎么样？」沃金肖说。

「球队近来如何？」他问。

===Original===
“Well, as far as I can see,” said Walkinshaw, “we ought to have a rather good season, if you’d only hurry up and come back. We beat a jolly hot lot of All Comers yesterday. Smith was playing for them. The Blue, you know. And lots of others. We got a goal and a try to nil.”

“Good,” said Grey. “Who did anything for us? Who scored?”

“I got in once. Payne got the other.”

“By Jove, did he? What sort of a game is he playing this year?”

===Chinese===
「唔，依我看，」沃金肖说，「这个赛季我们本该相当像样，只等你赶紧养好回来。昨天我们赢了一支火辣辣的『各路来客』队（All Comers）。史密斯（Smith）在他们那边打。就是个Blue（校队荣誉队员），你知道的。此外还有一大批好手。我们以一记破门加一次达阵，让对方吃了零蛋。」

「好，」格雷说，「谁替我们出了力？谁得的分？」

「我进了一次。另一个是佩恩（Payne）拿的。」

「嗬，是吗？他今年打的是什么水准？」

===Original===
The moment had come for Walkinshaw to unburden himself to his scheme. He proceeded to do so.

“Not up to much,” he said. “Look here, Grey, I’ve got rather an idea. It’s my opinion Payne’s not bucking up nearly as much as he might. Do you mind if I leave him out of the next game?”

Grey stared. The idea was revolutionary.

“What! Leave him out? My good man, he’ll be the next chap to get his colours. He’s a cert for his cap.”

“That’s just it. He knows he’s a cert, and he’s slacking on the strength of it. Now, my idea is that if you slung him out for a match or two, he’d buck up extra hard when he came into the team again. Can’t I have a shot at it?”

===Chinese===
沃金肖向自己那套方案倾吐衷肠的时刻到了。他随即付诸行动。

「不怎么样，」他说，「听着，格雷，我有个相当妙的主意。依我看，佩恩的振作劲儿，远不及他本来可以有的程度。你介不介意下一场把他从名单里除名？」

格雷瞪眼了。这主意具有革命性。

「什么！把他除名？我的好伙计，下一个戴上队色（colours）的就是他了。校队帽他十拿九稳。」

「毛病正出在这儿。他知道自己十拿九稳，便有恃无恐地懈怠起来。而我的主意是：你若把他甩出去一两场，等他归队时准会加倍卖力地振作。能不能让我试它一把？」

===Original===
Grey weighed the matter. Walkinshaw pressed home his arguments.

“You see, it isn’t like cricket. At cricket, of course, it might put a chap off awfully to be left out, but I don’t see how it can hurt a man’s play at footer. Besides, he’s beginning to stick on side already.”

“Is he, by Jove?” said Grey. This was the unpardonable sin. “Well, I’ll tell you what you can do if you like. Get up a scratch game, First Fifteen *v.* Second, and make him captain of the Second.”

“Right,” said Walkinshaw, and retired beaming.

===Chinese===
格雷权衡此事。沃金肖步步进逼，把论据钉牢。

「你瞧，这跟板球不一样。打板球，把人晾出去当然可能叫人大失常，可这怎么会伤一个人的橄榄球（footer）发挥，我就看不出了。再说了，他已经开始摆起架子来了。」

「是吗，嗬！」格雷说。这可是不可赦之罪。「好吧，我告诉你一个办法，你要是愿意的话。组一场即席练习赛，一队*对*二队，让他当二队的队长。」

「好嘞。」沃金肖说，满面红光地退了场。

===Original===
Walkinshaw, it may be remarked at once, to prevent mistakes, was a well-meaning idiot. There was no doubt about his being well-meaning. Also, there was no doubt about his being an idiot. He was continually getting insane ideas into his head, and being unable to get them out again. This matter of Payne was a good example of his customary methods. He had put his hand on the one really first-class forward St. Austin’s possessed, and proposed to remove him from the team. And yet through it all he was perfectly well-meaning. The fact that personally he rather disliked Payne had, to do him justice, no weight at all with him. He would have done the same by his bosom friend under like circumstances. This is the only excuse that can be offered for him. It was true that Payne regarded himself as a certainty for his colours, as far as anything can be considered certain in this vale of sorrow. But to accuse him of trading on this, and, to use the vernacular, of putting on side, was unjust to a degree.

On the afternoon following this conversation Payne, who was a member of Dacre’s House, came into his study and banged his books down on the table with much emphasis. This was a sign that he was feeling dissatisfied with the way in which affairs were conducted in the world. Bowden, who was asleep in an armchair—he had been staying in with a cold—woke with a start. Bowden shared Payne’s study. He played centre three-quarter for the Second Fifteen.

===Chinese===
为防差错，宜在此即刻注明：沃金肖是个好心肠的白痴。他好心肠，这一点毋庸置疑；同样，他是白痴，这一点也毋庸置疑。他脑子里不住地有疯癫的念头钻进去，而且再钻不出来。佩恩这桩事，便是他惯用手法的上佳标本。圣奥斯汀仅有的那名货真价实的一流前锋，恰好被他一手拈出，随即提议逐出队外。然而自始至终，他好心得彻头彻尾。平心而论，他个人对佩恩颇有几分看不顺眼，但这一节在他那里丝毫不曾左右天平。换作他的知交密友，同样情形之下他也会照此办理。这是能替他提出的唯一开脱。诚然，佩恩把自己看作队色的十拿九稳人选——在这愁苦尘世，凡事但凡能称得上十拿九稳的，也就到这个地步了。但若指控他借此居奇，并且——套句俗话——摆起了架子，那可不公道到了无以复加的地步。

这场谈话过后的下午，戴克家（Dacre's House）的成员佩恩走进书房，把书本砰砰地重摔在桌上，力道十足。这是一个标志，表明他对世间事务的操办方式心怀不满。鲍登（Bowden）原本正在扶手椅里酣睡——他因感冒一直窝在屋里——此时惊得跳了起来。鲍登与佩恩合用这间书房，在二队出任中路三四分卫（centre three-quarter）。

===Original===
“Hullo!” he said.

Payne grunted. Bowden realized that matters had not been going well with him. He attempted to soothe him with conversation, choosing what he thought would be a congenial topic.

“What’s on on Saturday?” he asked.

“Scratch game. First *v.* Second.”

Bowden groaned.

“I know those First *v.* Second games,” he said. “They turn the Second out to get butchered for thirty-five minutes each way, to improve the First’s combination. It may be fun for the First, but it’s not nearly so rollicking for us. Look here, Payne, if you find me with the pill at any time, you can let me down easy, you know. You needn’t go bringing off any of your beastly gallery tackles.”

===Chinese===
「哈啰！」他说。

佩恩哼了一声。鲍登明白他近来诸事不顺，便试图以交谈加以安抚，挑了个自以为投其所好的话题。

「周六有什么节目？」他问。

「即席练习赛。一队*对*二队。」

鲍登呻吟了一声。

「这种一队*对*二队的比赛，我可领教得多了，」他说，「他们把二队拉出去，上下半场各挨宰三十五分钟，好磨炼一队的配合。对一队或许是桩乐事，对我们可远没有那么欢腾。听着，佩恩，你什么时候要是撞见我抱着那颗『药丸』（the pill，球的谑称），尽可以手下留情、放倒得斯文些，知道吧。你犯不着专门去施展你那套讨厌的、演给看台瞧的擒抱。」

===Original===
“I won’t,” said Payne. “To start with, it would be against rules. We happen to be on the same side.”

“Rot, man; I’m not playing for the First.” This was the only explanation that occurred to him.

“I’m playing for the Second.”

“What! Are you certain?”

“I’ve seen the list. They’re playing Babington instead of me.”

===Chinese===
「不会的，」佩恩说，「头一条，那违反规则。我们恰好同属一边。」

「瞎说，老兄；我又不是替一队打。」这是他所能想到的唯一解释。

「我是替二队打。」

「什么！你拿得准？」

「名单我亲眼见了。他们让巴宾顿（Babington）顶了我的缺。」

===Original===
“But why? Babington’s no good.”

“I think they have a sort of idea I’m slacking or something. At any rate, Walkinshaw told me that if I bucked up I might get tried again.”

“Silly goat,” said Bowden. “What are you going to do?”

“I’m going to take his advice, and buck up.”

===Chinese===
「可这是为什么？巴宾顿不成啊。」

「我想他们多少有点觉得我在懈怠还是怎么的。反正沃金肖跟我说了，只要我振作起来，没准儿还能再让我试试。」

「蠢山羊。」鲍登说，「那你打算怎么办？」

「我打算采纳他的忠告——振作起来。」

### II / 二

===Original===
He did. At the beginning of the game the ropes were lined by some thirty spectators, who had come to derive a languid enjoyment from seeing the First pile up a record score. By halftime their numbers had risen to an excited mob of something over three hundred, and the second half of the game was fought out to the accompaniment of a storm of yells and counter yells such as usually only belonged to school matches. The Second Fifteen, after a poor start, suddenly awoke to the fact that this was not going to be the conventional massacre by any means. The First had scored an unconverted try five minutes after the kickoff, and it was after this that the Second began to get together. The school back bungled the drop out badly, and had to find touch in his own twenty-five, and after that it was anyone’s game. The scrums were a treat to behold. Payne was a monument of strength. Time after time the Second had the ball out to their three-quarters, and just after halftime Bowden slipped through in the corner. The kick failed, and the two teams, with their scores equal now, settled down grimly to fight the thing out to a finish. But though they remained on their opponents’ line for most of the rest of the game, the Second did not add to their score, and the match ended in a draw of three points all.

===Chinese===
他真振作了。开赛之时，围绳边排着三十来名看客，是来懒洋洋地消受一队刷新纪录比分的。到中场，人数已涨成三百出头、兴奋若狂的一大群，而下半场比赛，是在一阵通常唯有校际对抗才配享有的呐喊与反呐喊的暴风之中鏖战到底的。二队开局疲弱，随即猛然醒悟：这无论如何不会是那场照例的屠戮。开球五分钟后，一队得了一次附加射门未中的达阵；正是在此之后，二队开始拧成一股绳。校队那名后锋把落踢开球弄得一塌糊涂，只得在本方二十五码线内把球踢出边线，此后赛况便谁也说不准了。一场场争球（scrum）看去煞是过瘾。佩恩是一座力量的丰碑。二队一次又一次把球传出到三四分卫线上；中场刚过，鲍登便从边角的缝隙里钻了过去。附加射门未进，两队比分既已拉平，便都横下心来，决意鏖战到底。不过，尽管此后大半时间里二队都压在对方阵线跟前，却再未能添分，比赛终以三比三言和。

===Original===
The first intimation Grey received of this came to him late in the evening. He had been reading a novel which, whatever its other merits may have been, was not interesting, and it had sent him to sleep. He awoke to hear a well-known voice observe with some unction: “Ah! M’yes. Leeches and hot fomentations.” This effectually banished sleep. If there were two things in the world that he loathed, they were leeches and hot fomentations, and the School doctor apparently regarded them as a panacea for every kind of bodily ailment, from a fractured skull to a cold in the head. It was this gentleman who had just spoken, but Grey’s alarm vanished as he perceived that the words had no personal application to himself. The object of the remark was a fellow-sufferer in the next bed but one. Now Grey was certain that when he had fallen asleep there had been nobody in that bed. When, therefore, the medical expert had departed on his fell errand, the quest of leeches and hot fomentations, he sat up and gave tongue.

===Chinese===
格雷最早得知这一消息，是在当天夜里很晚的时候。他先前读着一部小说，那小说无论还有什么别的优点，反正毫不有趣，读着读着便把他送进了梦乡。他一觉醒来，只听一个熟悉的嗓音煞有介事地发表意见：「啊！嗯—是的。水蛭，还有热敷。」这一句便把睡意彻底驱散了。世上他若还有两样憎恶的东西，那便是水蛭和热敷；而校医显然把它们看作包治百病的万应灵药，从颅骨碎裂一直管到伤风鼻塞。方才发言的正是此公。不过格雷一察觉这话并非冲他本人而来，惊惶便消散了。这话的承受者，是隔了一张床的那位难友。而格雷记得清清楚楚：他入睡时，那张床上还空无一人。于是，等那位医学专家踏上他那凶残的差事——搜求水蛭与热敷——离去之后，他便坐起身来开了腔。

===Original===
“Who’s that in that bed?” he asked.

“Hullo, Grey,” replied a voice. “Didn’t know you were awake. I’ve come to keep you company.”

“That you, Barrett? What’s up with you?”

“Collarbone. Dislocated it or something. Reade’s over in that corner. He has bust his ankle. Oh, yes, we’ve been having a nice, cheery afternoon,” concluded Barrett bitterly.

“Great Scott! How did it happen?”

“Payne.”

===Chinese===
「那张床上是谁？」他问。

「哈啰，格雷，」一个声音答道，「不知道你醒着。我是来给你作伴的。」

「是你吗，巴雷特？你出了什么事？」

「锁骨。脱了臼还是怎么的。里德在那边的角落里。他摔坏了脚踝。哦是啊，我们过了一个多么惬意欢畅的下午，」巴雷特苦涩地收尾道。

「天哪！怎么闹的？」

「佩恩。」

===Original===
“Where? In your collarbone?”

“Yes. That wasn’t what I meant, though. What I was explaining was that Payne got hold of me in the middle of the field, and threw me into touch. After which he fell on me. That was enough for my simple needs. I’m not grasping.”

“How about Reade?”

“The entire Second scrum collapsed on top of Reade. When we dug him out his ankle was crocked. Mainspring gone, probably. Then they gathered up the pieces and took them gently away. I don’t know how it all ended.”

Just then Walkinshaw burst into the room. He had a large bruise over one eye, his arm was in a sling, and he limped. But he was in excellent spirits.

===Chinese===
「哪儿？在你锁骨上？」

「对。不过那不是我的意思。我要说明的是：佩恩在中场把我逮住，把我扔出了边线。随后他整个人压到我身上。这对我简单的需求而言已经足够。我不是个贪得无厌的人。」

「那里德呢？」

「二队整队争球前锋塌作一堆，压在里德身上。等我们把他挖出来，脚踝已经报废，多半是主发条断了。随后他们把零件归拢起来，轻轻地抬走了。到头来怎样收场，我就不得而知了。」

正当此时，沃金肖闯进屋来。他一只眼睛上顶着一大块乌青，一条胳膊吊着悬带，走路一瘸一拐。可他的兴致却好上加好。

===Original===
“I knew I was right, by Jove,” he observed to Grey. “I knew he could buck up if he liked.”

“I know it now,” said Barrett.

“Who’s this you’re talking about?” said Grey.

“Payne. I’ve never seen anything like the game he played today. He was everywhere. And, by Jove, his *tackling*!”

“Don’t,” said Barrett, wearily.

===Chinese===
「我就知道我是对的，嗬，」他向格雷发表见解，「我就知道他只要愿意，就能振作。」

「如今我知道了。」巴雷特说。

「你们说的是谁？」格雷说。

「佩恩。他今天打的这场球，我平生未见其匹。他无处不在。还有，嗬，他那份*擒抱*！」

「别说了。」巴雷特疲惫地说。

===Original===
“It’s the best match I ever played in,” said Walkinshaw, bubbling over with enthusiasm. “Do you know, the Second had all the best of the game.”

“What was the score?”

“Draw. One try all.”

===Chinese===
「这是我打过的最精彩的一场比赛，」沃金肖说得热忱四溢，「你可知道，占尽上风的全是二队。」

「比分多少？」

「平局。各一次达阵。」

===Original===
“And now I suppose you’re satisfied?” enquired Barrett. The great scheme for the regeneration of Payne had been confided to him by its proud patentee.

“Almost,” said Walkinshaw. “We’ll continue the treatment for one more game, and then we’ll have him simply fizzing for the Windybury match. That’s next Saturday. By the way, I’m afraid you’ll hardly be fit again in time for that, Barrett, will you?”

“I may possibly,” said Barrett, coldly, “be getting about again in time for the Windybury match of the year after next. This year I’m afraid I shall not have the pleasure. And I should strongly advise you, if you don’t want to have to put a team of cripples into the field, to discontinue the treatment, as you call it.”

“Oh, I don’t know,” said Walkinshaw.

On the following Wednesday evening, at five o’clock, something was carried in on a stretcher, and deposited in the bed which lay between Grey and Barrett. Close scrutiny revealed the fact that it was what had once been Charles Augustus Walkinshaw. He was slightly broken up.

===Chinese===
「那么如今你该满意了吧？」巴雷特问。改造佩恩的宏伟方案，已由其志得意满的专利人向他交过底。

「差不离了，」沃金肖说，「这疗程我们再续上一场，到温迪伯里（Windybury）那一战，他准会浑身气泡直冒。那是下周六。对了，恐怕你到时候很难及时痊愈归队了吧，巴雷特？」

「我嘛，」巴雷特冷冷地说，「没准儿能赶在后年温迪伯里之战开打前重新下地走动。今年这一场，恐怕我是无福消受了。而且我正要竭力奉劝你：倘若你不想派一支瘸子队伍上场，就趁早停了你所谓的这套疗法。」

「嗐，那可说不准。」沃金肖说。

接下来那个周三的傍晚五点，有一样东西被担架抬了进来，安放在格雷与巴雷特之间的那张床上。细看之下才认出，那东西从前曾是查尔斯·奥古斯塔斯·沃金肖。他碎得不算太厉害。

===Original===
“Payne?” enquired Grey in chilly tones.

Walkinshaw admitted the impeachment.

Grey took a pencil and a piece of paper from the table at his side. “If you want to know what I’m doing,” he said, “I’m writing out the team for the Windybury match, and I’m going to make Payne captain, as the senior Second Fifteen man. And if we win I’m jolly well going to give him his cap after the match. If we don’t win, it’ll be the fault of a raving lunatic of the name of Walkinshaw, with his beastly Colney Hatch schemes for reforming slack forwards. You utter rotter!”

===Chinese===
「佩恩？」格雷以冷冰冰的声调问。

沃金肖认下了这桩指控。

格雷从手边的桌上取过一枝铅笔和一张纸。「你们若想知道我在做什么，」他说，「我在排温迪伯里一战的出场名单，我打算让佩恩当队长，算他是二队里资历最深的一位。要是赢了，赛后我说什么也要把校队帽发给他。要是赢不了，那就要归罪于一位名叫沃金肖的咆哮疯子，归罪于他改造懈怠前锋的那一套科尔尼哈奇（Colney Hatch，即疯人院）式的馊主意。你这个十足的混蛋！」

===Original===
Fortunately for the future peace of mind of C. A. Walkinshaw, the latter contingency did not occur. The School, in spite of its absentees, contrived to pull the match off by a try to nil. Payne, as was only right and proper, scored the try, making his way through the ranks of the visiting team with the quiet persistence of a steamroller. After the game he came to tea, by request, at the infirmary, and was straightaway invested by Grey with his First Fifteen colours. On his arrival he surveyed the invalids with interest.

“Rough game, footer,” he observed at length.

“Don’t mention it,” said Barrett politely. “Leeches,” he added dreamily. “Leeches and hot fomentations. *Boiling* fomentations. Will somebody kindly murder Walkinshaw!”

“Why?” asked Payne, innocently.

===Chinese===
所幸那后一种情形并未发生，C. A. 沃金肖日后的心境安宁总算得了保全。校队尽管缺员累累，仍设法以一次达阵对零赢下了比赛。那次达阵，正如合乎情理、理所当然，由佩恩亲手取得：他以压路机般沉静的坚韧，从客队的队列中一路碾了过去。赛后他应召来医务室喝茶，格雷当场便将一队的队色授予了他。一到场，他便饶有兴味地巡视起众位病号。

「橄榄球这运动，粗野。」他半晌才评论道。

「不客气。」巴雷特彬彬有礼地说。「水蛭，」他悠悠如梦地补了一句，「水蛭，还有热敷。*滚沸的*热敷。哪位好心人行行好，去把沃金肖谋害了吧！」

「为什么呀？」佩恩一脸无辜地问。

## How Pillingshot Scored / 皮林肖特是怎样得分的

===Original===
Pillingshot was annoyed. He was disgusted, mortified; no other word for it. He had no objection, of course, to Mr. Mellish saying that his work during the term, and especially his Livy, had been disgraceful. A master has the right to say that sort of thing if he likes. It is one of the perquisites of the position. But when he went on to observe, without a touch of shame, that there would be an examination in the Livy as far as they had gone in it on the following Saturday, Pillingshot felt that he exceeded. It was not playing the game. There were the examinations at the end of term. Those were fair enough. You knew exactly when they were coming, and could make your arrangements accordingly. But to spring an examination on you in the middle of the term out of a blue sky, as it were, was underhand and unsportsmanlike, and would not do at all. Pillingshot wished that he could put his foot down. He would have liked to have stalked up to Mr. Mellish’s desk, fixed him with a blazing eye, and remarked, “Sir, withdraw that remark. Cancel that statement instantly, or—!” or words to that effect.

===Chinese===
皮林肖特（Pillingshot）恼火了。他反感，他羞愤难当；除此之外再找不出第二个词。梅利什先生（Mr. Mellish）说他这学期的功课、尤其是李维（Livy）一科糟糕得不成体统，这他自然毫无异议。老师若高兴说这种话，自有说的权利，这是该职位的一项外快。可是当对方竟毫无廉耻地接着宣布，下星期六要就已讲到的进度来一场李维考试，皮林肖特便觉得他越界了。这可不讲规矩。学期末本有大考，那倒公平得很：它们几时驾到，你一清二楚，尽可未雨绸缪、从容布置。可是学期中途、仿佛晴空里一声霹雳似的朝你兜头砸下一场考试，就是阴险，就是不讲运动家精神，万万要不得。皮林肖特但愿自己能挺身力拒。他倒很想昂然踱到梅利什先生的讲桌前，目光灼灼地钉住他，发表声明道：「先生，收回那句话。即刻撤销该声明，否则——！」或诸如此类的言辞。

===Original===
What he did say was: “Oo, si-i-r!!”

“Yes,” said Mr. Mellish, not troubling to conceal his triumph at Pillingshot’s reception of the news, “there will be a Livy examination next Saturday. And—” (he almost intoned this last observation)—“anybody who does not get fifty percent, Pillingshot, fifty percent, will be severely punished. Very severely punished, Pillingshot.”

After which the lesson had proceeded on its course.

===Chinese===
而他实际说出口的是：「噢——，先——生——！！」

「是——的，」梅利什先生说，对皮林肖特闻讯后的反应，他连得胜之情都懒得掩饰，「下星期六将有一场李维考试。而且——」（这后一句他几乎是吟诵出来的）——「凡拿不到五成的，皮林肖特，五成，都将受到严厉惩处。极其严厉的惩处，皮林肖特。」

此后，这堂课便循着常轨进行下去了。

===Original===
“Yes, it is rather low, isn’t it?” said Pillingshot’s friend, Parker, as Pillingshot came to the end of a stirring excursus on the rights of the citizen, with special reference to midterm Livy examinations. “That’s the worst of Mellish. He always has you somehow.”

“But what am I to *do*?” raved Pillingshot.

“I should advise you to swot it up before Saturday,” said Parker.

===Chinese===
「是啊，这手是相当下作，不是吗？」皮林肖特的朋友帕克（Parker）说。这时皮林肖特刚就公民权利发表完一篇慷慨激昂的长篇宏论，并特别针对学期中途的李维考试有所申论。「梅利什的毛病就在这儿。他总有法子算计到你。」

「可我究竟该*怎么办*？」皮林肖特狂乱地喊道。

「依我之见，你最好在星期六之前把书突击啃透，」帕克说。

===Original===
“Oh, don’t be an ass,” said Pillingshot, irritably.

What was the good of friends if they could only make idiotic suggestions like that?

He retired, brooding, to his house.

===Chinese===
「噢，别当蠢驴，」皮林肖特烦躁地说。

朋友若只会提这种白痴建议，要朋友何用？

于是他满怀心事，回宿舍去了。

===Original===
The day was Wednesday. There were only two more days, therefore, in which to prepare a quarter of a book of Livy. It couldn’t be done. The thing was not possible.

In the house he met Smythe.

===Chinese===
这一天是星期三。也就是说，统共只剩两天工夫，来预备李维的四分之一卷。办不到的。此事绝无可能。

在宿舍里他遇见了斯迈思（Smythe）。

===Original===
“What are you going to do about it?” he inquired. Smythe was top of the form, and if he didn’t know how to grapple with a crisis of this sort, who *could* know?

“If you’ll kindly explain,” said Smythe, “what the dickens you are talking about, I might be able to tell you.”

Pillingshot explained, with unwonted politeness, that “it” meant the Livy examination.

“Oh,” said Smythe, airily, “that! I’m just going to skim through it in case I’ve forgotten any of it. Then I shall read up the notes carefully. And then, if I have time, I shall have a look at the history of the period. I should advise you to do that, too.”

===Chinese===
「这桩事你打算怎么料理？」他讨教道。斯迈思是班级第一名，连他都不知道如何降服此类危机，还有*谁*知道？

「承蒙你肯解释，」斯迈思说，「你唠唠叨叨的究竟是个什么鬼，我兴许还能奉告。」

皮林肖特以罕见的客气解释道，「这桩事」指的是那场李维考试。

「哦，」斯迈思轻飘飘地说，「那个呀！我正打算把它浏览一遍，以防哪处记岔了。然后我要把注释仔细温习。然后，若还有富余工夫，再把那时代的历史瞧上一瞧。我劝你也照此办理。」

===Original===
“Oh, don’t be a goat,” said Pillingshot.

And he retired, brooding, as before.

===Chinese===
「噢，别犯傻，」皮林肖特说。

于是他一如方才，满怀心事地退场了。

===Original===
That afternoon he spent industriously, copying out the fourth book of *The Aeneid*. At the beginning of the week he had had a slight disagreement with M. Gerard, the French master.

Pillingshot’s views on behaviour and deportment during French lessons did not coincide with those of M. Gerard. Pillingshot’s idea of a French lesson was something between a pantomime rally and a scrum at football. To him there was something wonderfully entertaining in the process of “barging” the end man off the edge of the form into space, and upsetting his books over him. M. Gerard, however, had a very undeveloped sense of humour. He warned the humorist twice, and on the thing happening a third time, suggested that he should go into extra lesson on the ensuing Wednesday.

So Pillingshot went, and copied out Virgil.

===Chinese===
那天下午他过得颇为勤勉——抄写《埃涅阿斯纪》（*The Aeneid*）第四卷。本星期初，他曾与法语教员热拉尔先生（M. Gerard）发生过一场小小的龃龉。

关于法语课上应有的品行与仪态，皮林肖特的见解与热拉尔先生殊难合拍。皮林肖特心目中的法语课，是介于哑剧混战与橄榄球争球之间的某种东西。在他看来，「拱」掉长凳排尾那位、叫他连人带书跌进空档里去，这一过程自有妙不可言的娱乐性。怎奈热拉尔先生的幽默感发育得极不完全。他将这位幽默家警告了两次，待到同样的事变发生第三回，便提议他于下星期三莅临罚课。

于是皮林肖特去了，抄他的维吉尔（Virgil）。

===Original===
He emerged from the room of detention at a quarter past four. As he came out into the grounds he espied in the middle distance somebody being carried on a stretcher in the direction of the School House. At the same moment Parker loomed in sight, walking swiftly towards the School shop, his mobile features shining with the rapt expression of one who sees much ginger-beer in the near future.

“Hullo, Parker,” said Pillingshot, “who’s the corpse?”

===Chinese===
四点一刻，他从罚课教室里脱身出来。刚走进校园，便望见中景处有人被担架抬着，朝学舍（School House）方向去了。同一时刻帕克赫然入目，正快步赶往学校小卖部，一张生动的脸容光焕发，洋溢着一种眼见不远处有大宗姜汁啤酒之人所独有的出神神情。

「哈啰，帕克，」皮林肖特说，「担架上那具『尸体』是谁？」

===Original===
“What, haven’t you heard?” said Parker. “Oh, no, of course, you were in extra. It’s young Brown. He’s stunned or something.”

“How did it happen?”

“That rotter, Babington, in Dacre’s. Simply slamming about, you know, getting his eye in before going in, and Brown walked slap into one of his drives. Got him on the side of the head.”

“Much hurt?”

===Chinese===
「怎么，你没听说？」帕克说。「噢，没有，当然，你在罚课呢。是小布朗（Brown）。他叫人打昏了还是怎么的。」

「怎么出的事？」

「戴克家（Dacre's）那个混球巴宾顿（Babington）干的。就是抡圆了乱抽一气呗，上场之前先找球感，结果布朗不偏不倚，一头撞上他一记抽击，正中脑壳侧面。」

「伤得重么？」

===Original===
“Oh, no, I don’t think so. Keep him out of school for about a week.”

“Lucky beast. Wish somebody would come and hit me on the head. Come and hit me on the head, Parker.”

“Come and have an ice,” said Parker.

===Chinese===
「噢，不重，我看不重。大约得歇学一个星期。」

「幸运的畜生。真巴不得谁来照我脑袋上也来这么一下。来呀帕克，来照我脑袋上这么一下。」

「走，吃冰淇淋去，」帕克说。

===Original===
“Right-ho,” said Pillingshot. It was one of his peculiarities, that whatever the hour or the state of the weather, he was always equal to consuming an ice. This was probably due to genius. He had an infinite capacity for taking pains. Scarcely was he outside the promised ice when another misfortune came upon him. Scott, of the First Eleven, entered the shop. Pillingshot liked Scott, but he was not blind to certain flaws in the latter’s character. For one thing, he was too energetic. For another, he could not keep his energy to himself. He was always making Pillingshot do things. And Pillingshot’s notion of the ideal life was complete *dolce far niente*.

===Chinese===
「好嘞，」皮林肖特说。无论何时何刻、也不论天气处于何种状况，吃冰淇淋这件事他永远胜任愉快——这是他的特异禀赋之一。这多半要归功于天才：他具有一种无穷无尽的吃苦能耐。那份应许的冰淇淋还没在他肚里安顿妥当，另一桩不幸便又临头了。一队（First Eleven）的斯科特（Scott）走进了小卖部。皮林肖特喜欢斯科特，但对后者品格中的若干瑕疵并非视而不见。其一，此人精力太旺。其二，他又不肯叫精力专供自用。他老是支使皮林肖特做事。而皮林肖特对理想生活的构想，则是十足的「甜美闲怠」（*dolce far niente*）。

===Original===
“Ginger-beer, please,” said Scott, with parched lips. He had been bowling at the nets, and the day was hot. “Hullo! Pillingshot, you young slacker, why aren’t you changed? Been bunking half-holiday games? You’d better reform, young man.”

“I’ve been in extra,” said Pillingshot, with dignity.

“How many times does that make this term? You’re going for the record, aren’t you? Jolly sporting of you. Bit slow in there, wasn’t it? ’Nother ginger-beer, please.”

“Just a bit,” said Pillingshot.

===Chinese===
「姜汁啤酒，劳驾。」斯科特说，唇干口燥。他方才在球网那边投了一阵球，这天又热。「哈啰！皮林肖特，你这小懒骨头，怎么还不换装？想旷掉半日假的比赛么？你最好改过自新，年轻人。」

「我一直在罚课，」皮林肖特庄严地说。

「这学期这是第几遭了？你这是奔着纪录去的吧？可真够运动家的。那里头有点儿闷吧？再来一杯姜汁啤酒，劳驾。」

「闷也闷了那么一点，」皮林肖特说。

===Original===
“I thought so. And now you’re dying for some excitement. Of course you are. Well, cut over to the House and change, and then come back and field at the nets. The man Yorke is going to bowl me some of his celebrated slow tosh, and I’m going to show him exactly how Jessop does it when he’s in form.”

===Chinese===
「我就知道。这会儿你正馋着来点儿刺激呢。当然啦。这样吧，赶紧蹿回宿舍换装，然后回来在球网那边守球。那位约克先生（Mr. Yorke）要给我投几记他久负盛名的慢速水货球，我呢，正好给他原样示范一番，杰索普（Jessop）状态好的时候是怎么打的。」

===Original===
Scott was the biggest hitter in the School. Mr. Yorke was one of the masters. He bowled slow leg-breaks, mostly half-volleys and long hops. Pillingshot had a sort of instinctive idea that fielding out in the deep with Mr. Yorke bowling and Scott batting would not contribute largely to the gaiety of his afternoon. Fielding deep at the nets meant that you stood in the middle of the football field, where there was no telling what a ball would do if it came at you along the ground. If you were lucky you escaped without injury. Generally, however, the ball bumped and deprived you of wind or teeth, according to the height to which it rose. He began politely, but firmly, to excuse himself.

===Chinese===
斯科特是全校头一号重炮手。约克先生是教员之一。他投慢速腿侧旋球，且大半是半凌空球与长跳球之流。皮林肖特隐隐生出一种本能的预感：约克先生司投球、斯科特司击球、而他却远在深区守外野，这不会给他下午的欢愉增添多大进项。在球网那边守深区，意味着你站在橄榄球场正当中，一颗贴地朝你滚来的球会耍出什么花招，谁也没法预料。运气好，你毫发无伤全身而退。然而大抵而言，球总要跳将起来，并视其腾空之高度，或收走你的气息，或收走你的牙齿。于是他开始客气而坚定地告罪推辞。

===Original===
“Don’t talk rot,” said Scott, complainingly, “you must have some exercise or you’ll go getting fat. Think what a blow it would be to your family, Pillingshot, if you lost your figure. Buck up. If you’re back here in a quarter of an hour you shall have another ice. A large ice, Pillingshot, price sixpence. Think of it.”

The word ice, as has been remarked before, touched chords in Pillingshot’s nature to which he never turned a deaf ear. Within the prescribed quarter of an hour he was back again, changed.

===Chinese===
「别说混话，」斯科特委屈地抱怨道，「你必须运动运动，不然可要发胖了。想想看，皮林肖特，你若是把身段丢了，对你全家将是何等沉重的一击。振作起来。一刻钟之内回到此地，就再赏你一只冰淇淋。特大号的冰淇淋，皮林肖特，价目六便士。想想吧。」

如前文所述，「冰淇淋」这个词，总能在皮林肖特的天性里拨响他从不充耳不闻的那些琴弦。规定的一刻钟尚未用尽，他便换了装回来了。

===Original===
“Here’s the ice,” said Scott, “I’ve been keeping it warm for you. Shovel it down. I want to be starting for the nets. Quicker, man, quicker! Don’t roll it round your tongue as if it was port. Go for it. Finished? That’s right. Come on.”

Pillingshot had not finished, but Scott so evidently believed that he had, that it would have been unkind to have mentioned the fact. He followed the smiter to the nets.

If Pillingshot had passed the earlier part of the afternoon in a sedentary fashion, he made up for it now. Scott was in rare form, and Pillingshot noticed with no small interest that, while he invariably hit Mr. Yorke’s deliveries a quarter of a mile or so, he never hit two balls in succession in the same direction. As soon as the panting fieldsman had sprinted to one side of the football ground and returned the ball, there was a beautiful, musical *plonk*, and the ball soared to the very opposite quarter of the field. It was a fine exhibition of hitting, but Pillingshot felt that he would have enjoyed it more if he could have watched it from a deck-chair.

===Chinese===
「冰淇淋在这儿，」斯科特说，「我一直替你把它焐着呢。快刨进肚去。我这就该动身去球网了。快些，伙计，快些！别把它当波尔图陈酒似的在舌头上滚来滚去。放开腮帮干。完了？这就对了。走吧。」

皮林肖特并没有完，但斯科特显然笃信他完了，再要指出这一事实便未免残忍。他跟着这位重击手往球网去了。

倘若这天下午的前半程，皮林肖特是以久坐的方式打发的，如今他可全数补偿了回来。斯科特状态好得出奇，皮林肖特饶有兴味地注意到：他对约克先生的投球回回轰出四分之一英里开外，却绝不朝同一方向连轰两球。气喘吁吁的外野手刚冲刺到橄榄球场一侧、将球传回，那边厢便响起悦耳的一声「*梆*」，球已扶摇直上，飞向球场恰恰相反的一隅。这是一场精彩的击球表演，但皮林肖特觉得，若能从一张帆布躺椅上观礼，他本会欣赏得更透。

===Original===
“You’re coming on as a deep field, young Pillingshot,” said Scott, as he took off his pads. “You’ve got a knack of stopping them with your stomach, which the best first-class fields never have. You ought to give lessons at it. Now we’ll go and have some tea.”

If Pillingshot had had a more intimate acquaintance with the classics, he would have observed at this point, “*Timeo Danaos*,” and made a last dash for liberty in the direction of the shop. But he was deceived by the specious nature of Scott’s remark. Visions rose before his eyes of sitting back in one of Scott’s armchairs, watching a fag toasting muffins, which he would eventually dispatch with languid enjoyment. So he followed Scott to his study. The classical parallel to his situation is the well-known case of the oysters. They, too, were eager for the treat.

===Chinese===
「你当深区外野手，长进可观啊，小皮林肖特，」斯科特一面卸护腿一面说。「你有一手用肚皮拦球的绝活儿，连第一流外野手里的顶尖人物都不曾练成。你该开馆授徒才是。走，咱们吃茶点去。」

倘若皮林肖特与古典作品交情更深些，此刻便会吐出一句「我怕达那奥斯人啊」（*Timeo Danaos*），并朝小卖部的方向做最后一搏，冲刺争自由。无奈他被斯科特那句话华而不实的表象骗过了。眼前冉冉升起幻景：他深深陷进斯科特的一张扶手椅里，看着一名学仆（fag，伺候高年级学生的低年级生）烤松饼，而那些松饼终将由他本人懒洋洋地、陶然地消灭干净。于是他跟着斯科特进了书房。与他处境堪称经典对仗的，是那桩尽人皆知的牡蛎公案（典出卡罗尔《海象与木匠》）。那些牡蛎，也是热切巴望着那顿款待的。

===Original===
They had reached the study, and Pillingshot was about to fling himself, with a sigh of relief, into the most comfortable chair, when Scott unmasked his batteries.

“Oh, by the way,” he said, with a coolness which to Pillingshot appeared simply brazen, “I’m afraid my fag won’t be here today. The young crock’s gone and got mumps, or the plague, or something. So would you mind just lighting that stove? It’ll be rather warm, but that won’t matter. There are some muffins in the cupboard. You might weigh in with them. You’ll find the toasting-fork on the wall somewhere. It’s hanging up. Got it? Good man. Fire away.”

And Scott collected five cushions, two chairs, and a tin of mixed biscuits, and made himself comfortable. Pillingshot, with feelings too deep for words (in the then limited state of his vocabulary), did as he was requested. There was something remarkable about the way Scott could always get people to do things for him. He seemed to take everything for granted. If he had had occasion to hire an assassin to make away with the German Emperor, he would have said, “Oh, I say, you might run over to Germany and kill the Kaiser, will you, there’s a good chap? Don’t be long.” And he would have taken a seat and waited, without the least doubt in his mind that the thing would be carried through as desired.

===Chinese===
两人到了书房，皮林肖特正待如释重负地把自己掼进最舒服的那把椅子，斯科特却揭开炮衣、亮出了排炮。

「噢，对了，」他说，那份安详，在皮林肖特看来简直厚颜无耻到了极点，「恐怕我的学仆今天来不了了。那小病秧子偏偏害了腮腺炎，要不就是鼠疫，反正总归一路货。那么可否劳驾，把那只炉子点上？屋里会相当热，不过无关紧要。碗柜里有几只松饼。你不妨就此下手。烤叉就在墙上什么地方挂着。挂得好好的。明白了吗？好伙计。动手吧。」

于是斯科特收拢了五只靠垫、两把椅子和一听什锦饼干，把自己安顿得舒舒服服。皮林肖特则怀着言语无从承载的深情（鉴于他当时词汇量之有限），如嘱照办了。斯科特总有本事支得动人替他做事，这里头确乎有一种了不起的成分。他似乎把一概事项都视作理所当然。倘若他有需要雇一名刺客去结果德国皇帝，他会这么说：「噢，我说，你跑一趟德国、把德皇宰了，好不好，好伙计？去去就回。」然后他会拣个座位坐下静候，心里对事情必将如愿办成，不存一丝一毫的疑虑。

===Original===
Pillingshot had just finished toasting the muffins, when the door opened, and Venables, of Merevale’s, came in.

“I thought I heard you say something about tea this afternoon, Scott,” said Venables. “I just looked in on the chance. Good Heavens, man! Fancy muffins at this time of year! Do you happen to know what the thermometer is in the shade?”

“Take a seat,” said Scott. “I attribute my entire success in life to the fact that I never find it too hot to eat muffins. Do you know Pillingshot? One of the hottest fieldsmen in the School. At least, he was just now. He’s probably cooled off since then. Venables—Pillingshot, and vice versa. Buck up with the tea, Pillingshot. What, ready? Good man. Now we might almost begin.”

===Chinese===
皮林肖特刚把松饼烤好，门开了，米尔维尔家（Merevale's）的维纳布尔斯（Venables）踱了进来。

「我想我听见你提过今天下午吃茶点来着，斯科特，」维纳布尔斯说。「我抱着撞撞运气的念头进来瞧瞧。我的老天，老兄！居然在这个时节吃松饼！你可知道阴凉处温度计标着几度？」

「请坐，」斯科特说。「我把此生全部的成功，归功于这样一个事实：我从来不觉得热得吃不下松饼。你认识皮林肖特吗？全校最『火热』的外野手之一。至少方才是。这会儿多半已经凉透了。维纳布尔斯——皮林肖特，反之亦然。茶抓紧，皮林肖特。什么，这就备齐了？好伙计。现在咱们差不多可以开场了。」

===Original===
“Beastly thing that accident of young Brown’s, wasn’t it?” said Scott. “Chaps oughtn’t to go slamming about like that with the field full of fellows. I suppose he won’t be right by next Saturday?”

“Not a chance. Why? Oh, yes, I forgot. He was to have scored for the team at Windybury, wasn’t he?”

“Who are you going to get now?”

Venables was captain of the St. Austin’s team. The match next Saturday was at Windybury, on the latter’s ground.

===Chinese===
「小布朗那档子事故，真够呛，是不是？」斯科特说。「外场上满是人，就不该那样抡圆了乱抽。我料他下星期六总归好不利索了吧？」

「毫无指望。问这个做什么？噢，对了，我忘了。他本来该在温迪伯里（Windybury）给球队记分的，是不是？」

「如今你打算找谁？」

维纳布尔斯是圣奥斯汀（St. Austin's）队的队长。下周六的比赛在温迪伯里，用对方的场地。

===Original===
“I haven’t settled,” said Venables. “But it’s easy to get somebody. Scoring isn’t one of those things which only one chap in a hundred understands.”

Then Pillingshot had an idea—a great, luminous idea.

“May I score?” he asked, and waited trembling with apprehension lest the request be refused.

===Chinese===
「还没定下来，」维纳布尔斯说。「不过找人容易。记分不属于一百个人里只有一个人才通晓的那类学问。」

这时皮林肖特生出一个主意——一个伟大而澄明的主意。

「我能去记分么？」他问，随即战战兢兢地候着发落，唯恐这项请求遭到驳回。

===Original===
“All right,” said Venables, “I don’t see any reason why you shouldn’t. We have to catch the 8:14 at the station. Don’t you go missing it or anything.”

“Rather *not*,” said Pillingshot. “Not much.”

===Chinese===
「行啊，」维纳布尔斯说，「我看不出你有什么去不得的道理。我们得赶车站 8 点 14 分那班火车。你可别误了车什么的。」

「万万*不*会，」皮林肖特说。「半点儿也不会。」

---

===Original===
On Saturday morning, at exactly 9:15, Mr. Mellish distributed the Livy papers. When he arrived at Pillingshot’s seat and found it empty, an expression passed over his face like unto that of the baffled villain in transpontine melodrama.

“Where is Pillingshot?” he demanded tragically. “Where is he?”

“He’s gone with the team to Windybury, sir,” said Parker, struggling to conceal a large size in grins. “He’s going to score.”

“No,” said Mr. Mellish sadly to himself, “he *has* scored.”

===Chinese===
星期六上午 9 点 15 分整，梅利什先生分发李维试卷。行至皮林肖特的座位，发现座上无人，一种表情掠过他的脸，活脱是泰晤士河南岸通俗情节剧里那位棋败的反派所有的那一种。

「皮林肖特人在何处？」他悲怆地质问道。「他在何处？」

「他随球队去温迪伯里了，先生，」帕克说，一面苦苦按捺一副特大号的嬉笑。「他去记分。」

「不，」梅利什先生凄然自语道，「他可是*已经*得分了。」

## Notes / 笔记

===Original===

> Of all forms of lettered effusiveness, that which exploits the original work of others and professes to supply us with right opinions thereanent is the least wanted.
> — Kenneth Grahame

===Chinese===

> 在一切舞文弄墨的滔滔倾泻之中，最不受人待见的，莫过于那种剥削他人原著、还自称要就此供给我们正确见解的货色。
> ——肯尼斯·格雷厄姆（Kenneth Grahame）

===Original===

It has always seemed to me one of the worst flaws in our mistaken social system, that absolutely no distinction is made between the master who forces the human boy to take down notes from dictation and the rest of mankind. I mean that, if in a moment of righteous indignation you rend such a one limb from limb, you will almost certainly be subjected to the utmost rigour of the law, and you will be lucky if you escape a heavy fine of five or ten shillings, exclusive of the costs of the case. Now, this is not right on the face of it. It is even wrong. The law should take into account the extreme provocation which led to the action. Punish if you will the man who travels second-class with a third-class ticket, or who borrows a pencil and forgets to return it; but there are occasions when justice should be tempered with mercy, and this murdering of pedagogues is undoubtedly such an occasion.

It should be remembered, however, that there are two varieties of notes. The printed notes at the end of your Thucydides or Homer are distinctly useful when they aim at acting up to their true vocation, namely, the translating of difficult passages or words. Sometimes, however, the author will insist on airing his scholarship, and instead of translations he supplies parallel passages, which neither interest, elevate, nor amuse the reader. This, of course, is mere vanity. The author, sitting in his comfortable chair with something short within easy reach, recks nothing of the misery he is inflicting on hundreds of people who have done him no harm at all. He turns over the pages of his book of *Familiar Quotations* with brutal callousness, and for every tricky passage in the work which he is editing, finds and makes a note of three or four even trickier ones from other works. Who has not in his time been brought face to face with a word which defies translation? There are two courses open to you on such an occasion, to look the word up in the lexicon, or in the notes. You, of course, turn up the notes, and find: "See line 80." You look up line 80, hoping to see a translation, and there you are told that a rather similar construction occurs in Xenophades' *Lyrics from a Padded Cell*. On this, the craven of spirit will resort to the lexicon, but the man of mettle will close his book with an emphatic bang, and refuse to have anything more to do with it. Of a different sort are the notes which simply translate the difficulty and subside. These are a boon to the scholar. Without them it would be almost impossible to prepare one's work during school, and we should be reduced to the prosaic expedient of working in prep time. What we want is the commentator who translates *mensa* as "a table" without giving a page and a half of notes on the uses of the table in ancient Greece, with an excursus on the habit common in those times of retiring underneath it after dinner, and a list of the passages in Apollonius Rhodius where the word "table" is mentioned.

===Chinese===

一直以来我都觉得，我们这套谬误百出的社会制度有个大毛病：对强迫人类少年听写笔记的老师，与对其余芸芸众生，竟不加任何区分。我的意思是，倘使你在义愤填膺之际，把这样一位先生生生肢解，你几乎必定要领受法律最严酷的制裁，若能逃过五至十先令的重额罚金——讼费另计——便算你走运。这明摆着就不对。简直是错。法律理应考量那促使你动手的极端挑衅。拿三等车票坐二等车厢的人、借了铅笔忘记归还的人，你尽可以惩办；但世上自有正义应掺几分慈悲的时机，而这种戕害教书匠的行径，无疑正是此种时机。

然而应当记住，笔记有两路。印在你那册修昔底德（Thucydides）或荷马（Homer）卷尾的印刷体笔记，只要立志恪尽本职——即翻译难段难词——便分明大有用处。可有时候，编注者偏要炫耀学问，不给你译文，改供「类似段落」，既不能令读者生趣，不能使之升华，也不能博其一哂。这，当然，纯属虚荣。这位先生安坐舒适的圈椅里，伸手便可够着一杯烈性短饮，对强加给几百个从未得罪过他的人的苦难，全然无动于衷。他冷酷无情地翻动他那册《名言熟语大全》（Familiar Quotations），为他编注的著作里每一处刁钻的段落，都从旁的著作里寻出三四处理加刁钻的，郑重记上一笔。谁此生不曾与一个拒绝就范的词狭路相逢？此情此景，你有两条路：查词典，或查注释。你自然去翻注释，只见：「见第 80 行。」你翻到第 80 行，指望见着译文，那里却告诉你：色诺法德斯（Xenophades）的《软壁病房抒情诗集》（Lyrics from a Padded Cell）中有一个颇相仿佛的构造。见此情形，胆小鬼会转投词典，有血性的则砰的一声毅然合上书，从此与之恩断义绝。另有一路笔记，单单把难处译出，随即功成身退。这才是学子之福。没有它们，想在上课时段把功课预备停当几乎不可能，我们便只好沦落到用自习时间做功课这种寡淡的权宜之计。我们要的是这样的注家：他把 mensa 译作「桌子」，绝不添上一页半关于古希腊桌子功用的注释，不附论彼时饭后钻到桌子底下去的风俗，更不罗列阿波罗尼俄斯·罗狄乌斯（Apollonius Rhodius）著作中凡提及「桌子」一词的段落清单。

===Original===

These voluminous notes are apt to prove a nuisance in more ways than one. Your average master is generally inordinately fond of them, and will frequently ask some member of the form to read his note on so-and-so out to his fellows. This sometimes leads to curious results, as it is hardly to be expected that the youth called upon will be attending, even if he is awake, which is unlikely. On one occasion an acquaintance of mine, "whose name I am not at liberty to divulge," was suddenly aware that he was being addressed, and, on giving the matter his attention, found that it was the form-master asking him to read out his note on *Balbus murum aedificavit*. My friend is a kindhearted youth and of an obliging disposition, and would willingly have done what was asked of him, but there were obstacles, first and foremost of which ranked the fact that, taking advantage of his position on the back desk (whither he thought the basilisk eye of Authority could not reach), he had substituted *Bab Ballads* for the words of Virgil, and was engrossed in the contents of that modern classic. The subsequent explanations lasted several hours. In fact, it is probable that the master does not understand the facts of the case thoroughly even now. It is true that he called him a "loathsome, slimy, repulsive toad," but even this seems to fall short of the grandeur of the situation.

Those notes, also, which are, alas! only too common nowadays, that deal with peculiarities of grammar, how supremely repulsive they are! It is impossible to glean any sense from them, as the Editor mixes up Nipperwick's view with Sidgeley's reasoning and Spreckendzedeutscheim's surmise with Donnerundblitzendorf's conjecture in a way that seems to argue a thorough unsoundness of mind and morals, a cynical insanity combined with a blatant indecency. He occasionally starts in a reasonable manner by giving one view as (1) and the next as (2). So far everyone is happy and satisfied. The trouble commences when he has occasion to refer back to some former view, when he will say: "Thus we see (1) and (14) that," etc. The unlucky student puts a finger on the page to keep the place, and hunts up view one. Having found this, and marked the spot with another finger, he proceeds to look up view fourteen. He places another finger on this, and reads on, as follows: "Zmpe, however, maintains that Schrumpff (see 3) is practically insane, that Spleckzh (see 34) is only a little better, and that Rswkg (see 97 a (b) c3) is so far from being right that his views may be dismissed as readily as those of Xkryt (see 5x)." At this point brain-fever sets in, the victim's last coherent thought being a passionate wish for more fingers. A friend of mine who was the wonder of all who knew him, in that he was known to have scored ten percent in one of these papers on questions like the above, once divulged to an interviewer the fact that he owed his success to his methods of learning rather than to his ability. On the night before an exam, he would retire to some secret, solitary place, such as the boot room, and commence learning these notes by heart. This, though a formidable task, was not so bad as the other alternative. The result was that, although in the majority of cases he would put down for one question an answer that would have been right for another, yet occasionally, luck being with him, he would hit the mark. Hence his ten percent.

===Chinese===

这些鸿篇注释为祸之途，不止一端。你那平庸老师多半嗜之成癖，动辄点名班里某位同学，把他关于某某的注释当众念给同窗听。这有时会引出奇妙的结果，因为被点到的少年纵然醒着——这本身就不大可能——也断难指望他正在听讲。某回，我的一位相识——「其大名，我不便奉告」——蓦然察觉有人在对自己说话，凝神一听，原来是班主任要他念出他注的 *Balbus murum aedificavit*（「巴尔布斯筑了一道墙」，拉丁文入门名句）。我这位朋友心地善良、乐于助人，本很愿意照办，无奈障碍重重，其中高居榜首的是：他倚仗后排课桌的地理位置（他以为「权威」那条蛇怪之眼鞭长莫及），早已用《巴布歌谣》（Bab Ballads）掉包换下了维吉尔（Virgil）的诗文，正潜心咀嚼那部现代经典的精华。随后的解释工作持续了好几个钟头。事实上，那位老师很可能至今也没把本案案情彻底弄清。他确实骂了他一声「讨嫌、黏滑、叫人作呕的癞蛤蟆」，但即便是这句，似乎也不足以匹配当时场面的宏大。

还有那些讲语法特性的笔记——唉！今日何其普遍——它们是何等登峰造极地可憎！想从里头拾掇出半点意思，绝无可能：编者把尼珀威克（Nipperwick）的见解与西奇利（Sidgeley）的推论搅作一团，把斯普雷肯德泽多伊奇海姆（Spreckendzedeutscheim）的臆测与多纳温德布利岑多夫（Donnerundblitzendorf）的猜想炖作一锅，那手法似乎足证其心智与道德败坏到了家，一种愤世的癫狂兼露骨的猥亵。他偶尔也会通情达理地起个头，把一种观点列为（1），第二种列为（2）。到此为止，人人幸福，个个满足。麻烦始于他需要回提前文某条观点之时，那时他会写道：「由（1）与（14）我们可见」云云。倒霉的学生伸出一根手指按住页面记下位置，去寻观点一。找到之后，再用另一根手指标记此处，继而去找观点十四。他又往那儿按上一根手指，接着往下读，读到如下一段：「然而 Zmpe 坚称，Schrumpff（见3）简直就是疯子，Spleckzh（见34）不过略好一丁点，而 Rswkg（见97 a (b) c3）则错得离谱，其观点大可与 Xkryt（见5x）的观点一样随手打发。」读至此处，脑热症便告发作，受害者最后一点连贯的念头，是热切巴望自己能再多长几根手指。我有一位朋友，素为识者共推奇才，因为他曾在一份上述题型的卷子上博得百分之十，此事有口皆碑。他一度向一位记者披露，自己的成就当归功于学习方法，而非天资。每逢考试前夜，他便退隐到某个隐秘清静的所在——比如靴子间——着手把这些笔记背诵于心。这虽是桩艰巨的工程，总比另一条路强得多。其结果是：多数时候，他会把本该答另一道题的答案写在这一道底下，可偶尔鸿运当头，也能蒙个正着。于是便有了他那百分之十。

===Original===

Another fruitful source of discomfort is provided by the type of master who lectures on a subject for half an hour, and then, with a bland smile, invites, or rather challenges, his form to write a "good, long note" on the quintessence of his discourse. For the inexperienced this is an awful moment. They must write something—but what? For the last half hour they have been trying to impress the master with the fact that they belong to the class of people who can always listen best with their eyes closed. Nor poppy, nor mandragora, nor all the drowsy syrups of the world can ever medicine them to that sweet sleep which they have just been enjoying. And now they must write a "good, long note." It is in such extremities that your veteran shows up well. He does not betray any discomfort. Not he. He rather enjoys the prospect, in fact, of being permitted to place the master's golden eloquence on paper. So he takes up his pen with alacrity. No need to think what to write. He embarks on an essay concerning the master, showing up all his flaws in a pitiless light, and analysing his thorough worthlessness of character. On so congenial a subject he can, of course, write reams, and as the master seldom, if ever, desires to read the "good, long note," he acquires a well-earned reputation for attending in school and being able to express himself readily with his pen. *Vivat floreatque.*

But all these forms of notes are as nothing compared with the notes that youths even in this our boasted land of freedom are forced to take down from dictation. Of the "good, long note" your French scholar might well remark: "*C'est terrible*," but justice would compel him to add, as he thought of the dictation note: "*mais ce n'est pas le diable.*" For these notes from dictation are, especially on a warm day, indubitably *le diable*.

===Chinese===

还有一种丰沛的苦痛之源，出自那一型老师：他就某题目滔滔讲授半个钟头，随即和颜悦色地一笑，请——毋宁说激——全班就其宏论的精华写一篇「好而长的笔记」。对新手而言，这是个可怕的关口。他们必须写点什么——可写什么呢？方才那半个钟头，他们一直在力图让老师深信：自己属于那一类闭目反而听得最真切的人。罂粟治不好，曼德拉草治不好，普天之下一切催眠的糖浆，也再医不回他们方才享受的那种酣眠了。而眼下，他们必须写一篇「好而长的笔记」。正是在这般绝境里，你们的老手方显本色。他绝不流露半点窘迫。才不呢。说实话，想到获准把老师金声玉振的雄辩落到纸面，他简直求之不得。于是他欣然提笔。犯不着想写什么。他动笔撰一篇关于老师本人的宏文，将此公种种短处置于无情的光天化日之下，并细析其人品之彻底一文不值。在如此相宜的题目上，他当然下笔千言，而老师难得会想读那「好而长的笔记」，于是他挣得一份实至名归的名声：在校听讲专心，且运笔畅达。*Vivat floreatque*（愿他长荣昌盛）。

然而比起口授笔记来——即便在我们这片自诩自由的乐土上，少年人也难逃此劫——上述一切笔记都算不得什么。谈起那「好而长的笔记」，你的法国学者尽可以叹一句：「C'est terrible（这真可怕）」；可一想到口授笔记，公道之心也会迫使他补上一句：「mais ce n'est pas le diable（但这还算不上魔鬼）」。因为口授笔记，尤其在暖和的日子里，确凿无疑地就是 le diable（魔鬼）本身。

===Original===

Such notes are always dictated so rapidly that it is impossible to do anything towards understanding them as you go. You have to write your hardest to keep up. The beauty of this, from one point of view, is that, if you miss a sentence, you have lost the thread of the whole thing, and it is useless to attempt to take it up again at once. The only plan is to wait for some perceptible break in the flow of words, and dash in like lightning. It is much the same sort of thing as boarding a bus when in motion. And so you can take a long rest, provided you are in an obscure part of the room. In passing, I might add that a very pleasing indoor game can be played by asking the master, "what came after so-and-so?" mentioning a point of the oration some half-hour back. This always provides a respite of a few minutes while he is thinking of some bitter repartee worthy of the occasion, and if repeated several times during an afternoon may cause much innocent merriment.

===Chinese===

这种笔记口授起来永远迅捷无比，教你根本无从边听边懂。你只能拼尽全力地写，勉力不掉队。从一个角度看，妙处正在于此：漏掉一句，全局的线索便告失落，想立刻接续也是枉然。唯一的对策，是等待语流中出现一个明显可辨的间歇，然后如闪电般杀入。这与跳上一辆行进中的公共汽车颇有异曲同工之妙。如此一来，只要你坐在教室里某个僻暗的角落，便能好生休憩一场。顺带一提，还有一种极为惬意的室内游戏可玩：问老师一句「某某的后面是什么来着？」，所指乃是约莫半个钟头前那篇宏论中的某一点。趁他搜肠刮肚，酝酿一句配得上此情此景的刻薄妙答，你总能赢得几分钟的喘息；一个下午里若连来数回，更能平添许多无辜的欢愉。

===Original===

Of course, the real venom that lurks hid within notes from dictation does not appear until the time for examination arrives. Then you find yourself face to face with sixty or seventy closely and badly written pages of a notebook, all of which must be learnt by heart if you would aspire to the dizzy heights of half-marks. It is useless to tell your examiner that you had no chance of getting up the subject. "Why," he will reply, "I gave you notes on that very thing myself." "You did, sir," you say, as you advance stealthily upon him, "but as you dictated those notes at the rate of two hundred words a minute, and as my brain, though large, is not capable of absorbing sixty pages of a notebook in one night, how the suggestively asterisked aposiopesis do you expect me to know them? Ah-h-h!" The last word is a war cry, as you fling yourself bodily on him, and tear him courteously, but firmly, into minute fragments. Experience, which, as we all know, teaches, will in time lead you into adopting some method by which you may evade this taking of notes. A good plan is to occupy yourself with the composition of a journal, an unofficial magazine not intended for the eyes of the profane, but confined rigidly to your own circle of acquaintances. The chief advantage of such a work is that you will continue to write while the notes are being dictated. To throw your pen down with an air of finality and begin reading some congenial work of fiction would be a gallant action, but impolitic. No, writing of some sort is essential, and as it is out of the question to take down the notes, what better substitute than an unofficial journal could be found? To one whose contributions to the School magazine are constantly being cut down to mere skeletons by the hands of censors, there is a rapture otherwise unattainable in a page of really scurrilous items about those in authority. Try it yourselves, my beamish lads. Think of something really bad about somebody. Write it down and gloat over it. Sometimes, indeed, it is of the utmost use in determining your future career. You will probably remember those Titanic articles that appeared at the beginning of the war in *The Weekly Luggage-Train*, dealing with all the crimes of the War Office—the generals, the soldiers, the enemy—of everybody, in fact, except the editor, staff and office boy of *The W.L.T.* Well, the writer of those epoch-making articles confesses that he owes all his skill to his early training, when, a happy lad at his little desk in school, he used to write trenchantly in his notebook on the subject of the authorities. There is an example for you. Of course we can never be like him, but let, oh! let us be as like him as we're able to be. A final word to those lost ones who dictate the notes. Why are our ears so constantly assailed with unnecessary explanations of, and opinions on, English literature? Prey upon the Classics if you will. It is a revolting habit, but too common to excite overmuch horror. But surely anybody, presupposing a certain bias towards sanity, can understand the Classics of our own language, with the exception, of course, of Browning. Take Tennyson, for example. How often have we been forced to take down from dictation the miserable maunderings of some commentator on the subject of *Maud*. A person reads *Maud*, and either likes it or dislikes it. In any case his opinion is not likely to be influenced by writing down at express speed the opinions of somebody else concerning the methods or objectivity and subjectivity of the author when he produced the work.

===Chinese===

当然，口授笔记里潜藏的真正毒性，要到考试时节方才显形。那时你发现自己与一本笔记本上密密麻麻、字迹潦草的六七十页狭路相逢，你若想企及「总分之半」那令人晕眩的高峰，就必须把它们尽数背熟。跟考官说你根本无暇温习此科，纯属徒劳。「怎么，」他会答道，「这桩事我可是亲手给你授过笔记的。」「您是授过，先生，」你一边蹑手蹑脚地向他逼近，一边说，「可是您口授那些笔记的速度是每分钟两百词，而我的脑子虽然不小，却也没有本事在一夜之间吸收六十页笔记本，你这星号打得意味深长的『跳脱格』（aposiopesis）啊，究竟指望我怎么把它们统统记住？啊——哈！」末一个词是冲锋的战号，紧接着你整个人扑将上去，礼数周全而意志坚定地把他撕成细碎的残片。众所周知，经验这位教员迟早会引导你想出某种法子，规避这门记笔记的苦役。一条妙计是专心致志编一份期刊——一份非官方杂志，不供俗眼过目，严格限于你自己的熟人圈内流通。此举首要的妙处在于：口授笔记的当口，你手中始终有字可写。把笔一撂、摆出就此收工的架势，转而捧读某部合口味的小说，固然算得壮举，惜乎失之于不智。不行，写点什么总是必需的；既然记笔记绝无可能，还有什么比一份非官方期刊更理想的替身呢？对一个投稿屡被审查官斫得只剩骨架的校刊作者而言，一版真正泼辣刻毒、专攻当权者的小新闻，能带来别处无从企及的极乐。亲手试试吧，我神采焕发的少年们。想出一件关于某人的真正够坏的事。写下来，反复咂摸。有时，它当真能对你择定终身职业起到至大的作用。你们多半还记得大战之初《每周行李列车》（The Weekly Luggage-Train）上那批泰坦式的宏文，历数陆军部的滔天罪行——将军们、士兵们、敌人们——事实上除了《行李列车》的主编、职员和办公室小听差之外，无一漏网。喏，那批划时代宏文的作者亲口招认：他的一身绝技全亏早年的训练——当年他是个安坐学校小课桌前的快乐少年，惯于在笔记本里就当权者诸公挥毫痛陈。这便是给你的榜样。我们固然永远成不了他，可是让我们——哦！让我们竭尽全力，像他像到几分是几分。最后奉劝那些口授笔记的迷途羔羊几句。我们的耳朵，为何要没完没了地遭受关于英国文学的多余解释与意见的轰击？你们尽可以去糟蹋古典作品。那习惯固然可憎，但早已司空见惯，引不起太大的惊骇。可是只要心智稍稍偏向健全一端，任何人想必都能读懂我们自己语言的经典——当然，布朗宁（Browning）除外。且拿丁尼生（Tennyson）来说。多少回，我们被迫记下某位评论家就《莫德》（Maud）一诗发表的凄凄惶惶的絮叨。一个人读了《莫德》，或者喜欢，或者不喜欢。无论哪种，他的见解都不见得会因为以特快车速抄录旁人关于作者手法、或者客观性与主观性的高论，而有分毫改变。

===Original===

Somebody told me a short time ago that Shelley was an example of supreme, divine, superhuman genius. It is the sort of thing Mr. Gilbert's "rapturous maidens" might have said: "How Botticellian! How Fra Angelican! How perceptively intense and consummately utter!" There is really no material difference.

===Chinese===

前些日子有人告诉我，雪莱（Shelley）乃是至高无上、神圣非凡、超越人寰的天才典范。这话，吉尔伯特（Gilbert）先生笔下那些「如痴如狂的少女」想必也说得出口：「多么波提切利！多么弗拉·安杰利科！多么洞察入微的炽烈，多么登峰造极的彻底！」两者之间，实在没有实质性的差别。

## Now, Talking About Cricket / 闲话板球

===Original===
In the days of yore, when these white hairs were brown﻿—or was it black? At any rate, they were not white﻿—and I was at school, it was always my custom, when Fate obliged me to walk to school with a casual acquaintance, to whom I could not unburden my soul of those profound thoughts which even then occupied my mind, to turn the struggling conversation to the relative merits of cricket and football.

“Do you like cricket better than footer?” was my formula. Now, though at the time, in order to save fruitless argument, I always agreed with my companion, and praised the game he praised, in the innermost depths of my subconsciousness, cricket ranked a long way in front of all other forms of sport. I may be wrong. More than once in my career it has been represented to me that I couldn’t play cricket for nuts. My captain said as much when I ran him out in *the* match of the season after he had made forty-nine and looked like stopping. A bowling acquaintance heartily endorsed his opinion on the occasion of my missing three catches off him in one over. This, however, I attribute to prejudice, for the man I missed ultimately reached his century, mainly off the deliveries of my bowling acquaintance. I pointed out to him that, had I accepted any one of the three chances, we should have missed seeing the prettiest century made on the ground that season; but he was one of those bowlers who sacrifice all that is beautiful in the game to mere wickets. A sordid practice.

Later on, the persistence with which my county ignored my claims to inclusion in the team, convinced me that I must leave cricket fame to others. True, I did figure, rather prominently, too, in one county match. It was at the Oval, Surrey *v.* Middlesex. How well I remember that occasion! Albert Trott was bowling (Bertie we used to call him); I forget who was batting. Suddenly the ball came soaring in my direction. I was not nervous. I put down the sandwich I was eating, rose from my seat, picked the ball up neatly, and returned it with unerring aim to a fieldsman who was waiting for it with becoming deference. Thunders of applause went up from the crowded ring.

===Chinese===
遥想当年，这几根白发还是棕色——抑或是黑色？反正不是白——那时我还在学堂读书。每当命运捉弄，安排我与某位泛泛之交结伴上学——此等交情，尚不足以让我倾吐胸中那些即便彼时也已盘踞脑海的深邃思想——我素来的惯例，便是把这场奄奄一息的谈话，引向板球与足球孰优孰劣这个题目。

「板球和足球，你更爱哪个？」这便是我的开场套话。当年，为省却徒劳的争辩，我总顺着同伴附和，他夸哪样我便夸哪样；然而在我潜意识的最深处，板球遥遥领先于其他一切运动形式。我也许错了。在我的运动生涯中，不止一次有人向我指出：玩板球我一窍不通。我们队长便说过这样的话——那季最要紧的一场比赛里，他已得四十九分、正自站稳脚跟，我却害得他被跑传出局。一位投球的相识对此热烈附议，起因是一轮投球之内，我接连漏接了他手下的三个球。不过我把这话归之于偏见：我漏掉的那位，最终得了一百分，分数主要便是从这位投球朋友的投球里取来的。我向他指出，那三个机会但凡接住一个，我们便要错失那季在这座球场上打出的最漂亮的一记百分了；怎奈他是那种投球手——为了区区出局数，不惜牺牲这项运动中一切美的东西。陋习一桩。

后来，本郡始终如一地无视我入队的申请，终于使我确信：板球盛名，还是留给旁人去挣罢。诚然，我确曾在一场郡级比赛里露过脸，而且露得颇为显眼。那是在椭圆球场（the Oval），萨里（Surrey）对米德尔塞克斯（Middlesex）。那场面我记得多真切啊！投球的是阿尔伯特·特罗特（Albert Trott）——我们惯称他伯蒂；击球的是谁，我忘了。忽地那球腾空朝我飞来。我并不慌张。放下正吃着的三明治，从座位上起身，干净利落地拾起球，毫厘不爽地掷还给一位守场员——他正怀着恰如其分的敬意候着此球。四下里拥挤的看台掌声雷动。

===Original===
That was the highest point I ever reached in practical cricket. But, as the historian says of Mr. Winkle, a man may be an excellent sportsman in theory, even if he fail in practice. That’s me. Reader (if any), have you ever played cricket in the passage outside your study with a walking-stick and a ball of paper? That’s the game, my boy, for testing your skill of wrist and eye. A century *v.* the M.C.C. is well enough in its way, but give me the man who can watch ’em in a narrow passage, lit only by a flickering gas-jet﻿—one for every hit, four if it reaches the end, and six if it goes downstairs full-pitch, any pace bowling allowed. To make double figures in such a match is to taste life. Only you had better do your tasting when the Housemaster is out for the evening.

===Chinese===
这便是我板球实践生涯所抵达的最高峰了。不过，正如那位史家评论文克尔先生（Mr. Winkle，《匹克威克外传》人物）时所言：一个人尽可以在理论上是个极好的运动员，哪怕实践上一败涂地。说的就是我。读者诸君（倘若有），你可曾拿一根手杖、一团纸球，在书房外的过道里打过板球？这才是好游戏呢，我的孩子——检验你手腕与眼力的功夫，全在乎此。对玛丽勒本板球俱乐部（M.C.C.）打出一百分，固然自有其妙处；但我更推崇这等好汉——能在一条仅靠一盏摇曳煤气灯照明的窄过道里盯稳来球：每击中一次得一分，球打到过道尽头得四分，若球不落地一路滚下楼梯则得六分，投球快慢悉听尊便。在这样一场比赛里打到两位数，才算尝到了人生的滋味。只是这滋味，你最好趁舍监晚间外出时再行品尝。

---

===Original===
I like to watch the young cricket idea shooting. I refer to the lower games, where “next man in” umpires with his pads on, his loins girt, and a bat in his hand. Many people have wondered why it is that no budding umpire can officiate unless he holds a bat. For my part, I think there is little foundation for the theory that it is part of a semi-religious rite, on the analogy of the Freemasons’ special handshake and the like. Nor do I altogether agree with the authorities who allege that man, when standing up, needs something as a prop or support. There is a shadow of reason, I grant, in this supposition, but after years of keen observation I am inclined to think that the umpire keeps his bat by him, firstly, in order that no unlicensed hand shall commandeer it unbeknownst, and secondly, so that he shall be ready to go in directly his predecessor is out. There is an ill-concealed restiveness about his movements, as he watches the batsmen getting set, that betrays an overwrought spirit. Then of a sudden one of them plays a ball on to his pad. “*’s that?*” asks the bowler, with an overdone carelessness. “Clean out. Now *I’m* in,” and already he is rushing up the middle of the pitch to take possession. When he gets to the wicket a short argument ensues. “Look here, you idiot, I hit it hard.” “Rot, man, out of the way.” “!!??!” “Look here, Smith, *are* you going to dispute the umpire’s decision?” Chorus of fieldsmen: “Get out, Smith, you ass. You’ve been given out years ago.” Overwhelmed by popular execration, Smith reluctantly departs, registering in the black depths of his soul a resolution to take on the umpireship at once, with a view to gaining an artistic revenge by giving his enemy run out on the earliest possible occasion. There is a primeval insouciance about this sort of thing which is as refreshing to a mind jaded with the stiff formality of professional umpires as a cold shower-bath.

===Chinese===
我爱看板球的幼芽拔节抽枝。我指的是低年级组的比赛——那厢「下一位上场」的击球手系着护腿、束紧腰身、手提球棒，正在执哨当裁判。许多人纳闷，为何任何裁判界的新芽，不手执球棒便无法司职。依我看，有一种理论说这是半宗教仪式的一部分，可与共济会（Freemasons）的专属握手礼之类相类比——实在没什么根据。我也不敢完全苟同那些权威的说法，他们声称人一旦站起来，便需要点什么东西作扶手作支撑。我承认这一假设也有一丝道理；但经过多年的热心观察，我倾向于认为：裁判把球棒留在身边，其一，是为了不让哪只未经许可的手趁人不备将棒征用；其二，是为了前任一出局，他即刻便能上阵。眼看击球手们渐渐进入状态，他那一举一动间透着一股掩藏不住的躁动，泄露出一颗焦灼难耐的心。蓦地，其中一位把球打上了自家护腿。「怎么样？」投球手问道，那副漫不经心装得过了头。「干干净净，出局。这下该我上了。」话音未落，他已冲上球道中央，前去接管阵地。待他赶到三柱门，一场短促的争论随即爆发。「你听着，你这白痴，我是结结实实打中球的。」「胡扯，老兄，让开。」「！！？！」「我说，史密斯（Smith），你莫非还想质疑裁判的判决？」众守场员齐声合唱：「下去吧，史密斯，你这蠢驴。你出局都出了好几年啦。」在众口一词的唾骂声中，史密斯悻悻退场，同时在灵魂的黑渊里登记下一项决议：立即接过裁判的职位，以便瞅准最早的时机，判他的仇家跑传出局，成就一场艺术的复仇。这类事情里有一种太古的无忧无虑；对于被职业裁判的死板客套磨得疲惫的心智，它如一场冷水浴般涤荡提神。

===Original===
I have made a special study of last-wicket men; they are divided into two classes, the deplorably nervous, or the outrageously confident. The nervous largely outnumber the confident. The launching of a last-wicket man, when there are ten to make to win, or five minutes left to make a draw of a losing game, is fully as impressive a ceremony as the launching of the latest battleship. An interested crowd harasses the poor victim as he is putting on his pads. “Feel in a funk?” asks some tactless friend. “N-n-no, norrabit.” “That’s right,” says the captain encouragingly, “bowling’s as easy as anything.”

This cheers the wretch up a little, until he remembers suddenly that the captain himself was distinctly at sea with the despised trundling, and succumbed to his second ball, about which he obviously had no idea whatever. At this he breaks down utterly, and, if emotional, will sob into his batting glove. He is assisted down the Pavilion steps, and reaches the wickets in a state of collapse. Here, very probably, a reaction will set in. The sight of the crease often comes as a positive relief after the vague terrors experienced in the Pavilion.

The confident last-wicket man, on the other hand, goes forth to battle with a light quip upon his lips. The lot of a last-wicket batsman, with a good eye and a sense of humour, is a very enviable one. The incredulous disgust of the fast bowler, who thinks that at last he may safely try that slow head-ball of his, and finds it lifted genially over the leg-boundary, is well worth seeing. I remember in one school match, the last man, unfortunately on the opposite side, did this three times in one over, ultimately retiring to a fluky catch in the slips with forty-one to his name. Nervousness at cricket is a curious thing. As the author of *Willow the King*, himself a county cricketer, has said, it is not the fear of getting out that causes funk. It is a sort of intangible *je ne sais quoi*. I trust I make myself clear. Some batsmen are nervous all through a long innings. With others the feeling disappears with the first boundary.

===Chinese===
我对末柱击球手（last-wicket man）做过专门研究。他们可分为两类：一类紧张得可叹，一类自信得离谱，而紧张者的人数远压自信者。当还差十分方能取胜，或败局已定、只剩五分钟去争取一和之时，一位末柱击球手的登场礼，其庄严隆重不亚于最新式战舰的下水典礼。热心的围观者围着那位正系护腿的可怜牺牲品百般骚扰。「心里发毛吗？」某位不知轻重的朋友问道。「不——不——不发毛，一点也——也不发毛。」「这就对了，」队长温言鼓励道，「这投球软和得很哪。」

这话让那可怜虫稍稍振作，直到他猛然记起：队长本人先前面对那备受轻视的慢投时也分明方寸大乱，第二个球便遭了毒手，对那球他显然全无半点办法。想到这里他彻底垮了；若是感情丰富之辈，还要对着击球手套抽噎一场。他由人搀下看亭（Pavilion）的台阶，半瘫半软地挨到三柱门前。而到了此地，十之八九会发生反弹。尝过看亭里那种种无名的恐怖之后，再见到击球线的景象，反倒往往成了一种实实在在的宽慰。

自信的末柱击球手则不然，他唇边挂着一句轻俏的妙语，昂然赴战。眼力既佳、幽默感又不缺的末柱击球手，其命运着实令人艳羡。快球投手心想，这下总算可以放心一试他那记慢速高球了，却发现球被和和气气地挑过了腿侧边界——他脸上那副难以置信的嫌恶，实在值得一看。我记得有一场校际比赛，最后一位击球手——不巧在对方阵中——一轮之内连演了三回这般好戏，最终顶着四十一分的名头，被截杀区里一次侥幸的接杀送下了场。板球场上的紧张是桩奇事。诚如《柳木为王》（Willow the King）的作者——他本人便是位郡级球手——所言：作祟致怯的，并非对出局的恐惧。它是一种无从捉摸的、说不清道不明的东西（je ne sais quoi）。但愿我把话说清楚了。有的击球手整场长局都紧张不已；换了另一些人，第一记边界球一出，那感觉便烟消云散。

---

===Original===
A young lady﻿—it is, of course, not polite to mention her age to the minute, but it ranged somewhere between eight and ten﻿—was taken to see a cricket match once. After watching the game with interest for some time, she gave out this profound truth: “They all attend specially to one man.” It would be difficult to sum up the causes of funk more lucidly and concisely. To be an object of interest is sometimes pleasant, but when ten fieldsmen, a bowler, two umpires, and countless spectators are eagerly watching your every movement, the thing becomes embarrassing.

That is why it is, on the whole, preferable to be a cricket spectator rather than a cricket player. No game affords the spectator such unique opportunities of exerting his critical talents. You may have noticed that it is always the reporter who knows most about the game. Everyone, moreover, is at heart a critic, whether he represent the majesty of the Press or not. From the lady of Hoxton, who crushes her friend’s latest confection with the words, “My, wot an ’at!” down to that lowest class of all, the persons who call your attention (in print) to the sinister meaning of everything Clytemnestra says in *The Agamemnon*, the whole world enjoys expressing an opinion of its own about something.

In football you are vouchsafed fewer chances. Practically all you can do is to shout “offside” whenever an opponent scores, which affords but meagre employment for a really critical mind. In cricket, however, nothing can escape you. Everything must be done in full sight of everybody. There the players stand, without refuge, simply inviting criticism.

===Chinese===
有一位小姑娘——把她的年纪精确到分地说出来自然不礼貌，不过大致在八岁到十岁之间——有一回被人带去看板球赛。她兴致勃勃地观战片刻之后，发布了这条深刻的真理：「他们都专门盯着一个人。」要为怯场的缘由找一个比这更明白畅晓、更言简意赅的总结，恐怕难乎其难。做众目所瞩的对象，有时是桩乐事；可当十名守场员、一名投球手、两名裁判，外加数不清的看客，全都热切注视着你的一举一动时，这事可就尴尬了。

所以总的说来，做板球看客，实在比做板球球员可取。再没有哪项运动，能赐予看客如此独一无二的机会，一展其批评的才具。你或许早已留意到：最通晓这项运动的，永远是写报道的那一位。况且，人人心中都住着一位批评家，管他是否代表着新闻界的威严。上自霍克斯顿（Hoxton）的太太——她一句「乖乖，好一顶帽子哟！」便碾碎了女友最新的帽子新作——下至最低等的一类：专在印刷品上提请你留神克吕泰涅斯特拉（Clytemnestra）在《阿伽门农》（The Agamemnon）里每一句台词的险恶用意——全世界都热爱对点什么发表一己之见。

足球赐予你的机会则少得多。你所能做的，实际上不过是每逢对方进球便高喊一声「越位」——对一个真正具有批评头脑的人而言，这点用武之地实在菲薄。板球却不然，什么也逃不过你的眼睛。一切动作都必须在众目睽睽之下完成。球员们就站在那里，无处藏身，简直是明摆着邀请批评。

===Original===
It is best, however, not to make one’s remarks too loud. If you do, you call down upon yourself the attention of others, and are yourself criticized. I remember once, when I was of tender years, watching a school match, and one of the batsmen lifted a ball clean over the Pavilion. This was too much for my sensitive and critical young mind. “On the carpet, sir,” I shouted sternly, well up in the treble clef, “keep ’em on the carpet.” I will draw a veil. Suffice it to say that I became a sport and derision, and was careful for the future to criticize in a whisper. But the reverse by no means crushed me. Even now I take a melancholy pleasure in watching school matches, and saying So-and-So will make quite a fair *schoolboy* bat in time, but he must get rid of that stroke of his on the off, and that shocking leg-hit, and a few of those *awful* strokes in the slips, but that on the whole, he is by no means lacking in promise. I find it refreshing. If, however, you feel compelled not merely to look on, but to play, as one often does at schools where cricket is compulsory, it is impossible to exaggerate the importance of white boots. The game you play before you get white boots is not cricket, but a weak imitation. The process of initiation is generally this. One plays in shoes for a few years with the most dire result, running away to square leg from fast balls, and so on, till despair seizes the soul. Then an angel in human form, in the very effective disguise of the man at the school boot-shop, hints that, for an absurdly small sum in cash, you may become the sole managing director of a pair of *white buckskin* boots with real spikes. You try them on. They fit, and the initiation is complete. You no longer run away from fast balls. You turn them neatly off to the boundary. In a word, you begin for the first time to play the game, the whole game, and nothing but the game.

===Chinese===
不过，发言还是以不太响亮为妙。一旦太响，你便招来旁人的注意，自己反倒成了被批评的对象。记得我年少之时，有一回看校赛，一名击球手把球干干净净地挑过了看亭顶。这对吾辈敏感而富于批评精神的少年心智，实在是可忍孰不可忍。「球要贴地，先生，」我严词喝道，音调高高扬起、直入高音谱号，「把球都给我贴着地打。」余下情节，此处且拉上一层帷幕。只消说一句：我沦为众人的笑柄，从此小心翼翼，改用耳语进行批评。但那次挫折绝没有压垮我。直到如今，我仍怀着一种悒郁的乐趣观看校赛，点评道：某君假以时日，未尝不能成为一员尚可的学童击球手，只是他务必去掉外侧那记击球、那记吓人的腿侧猛挥，以及截杀区那几手糟糕透顶的招式——不过总的说来，此子前途未可限量。我发觉此举令人神清气爽。然而，倘若你不甘于只看不练、还非得下场不可——在那些板球列为必修的学校里，这是常有的事——那么白靴之重要，任你怎么夸张都不为过。得白靴之前你打的那玩意儿不是板球，只是它一份绵软无力的仿制品。入门的历程大致如下：先穿着便鞋打上几年，下场惨不忍睹——见了快球便逃往侧外野，诸如此类——直到绝望攫住灵魂。随后一位人形天使，化装成学校靴店的伙计——这化装实在高明——暗示你说：只需一笔少得荒唐的现金，你便可成为一双带真正鞋钉的白色鹿皮靴（white buckskin）的唯一常务董事。你试穿了一下。正合脚，于是入门礼成。你再不逃避快球，而是把它们利落地拨向边界。一言以蔽之：你生平头一遭，打起了这场比赛，整场的比赛，而且除了比赛还是比赛。

===Original===
There are misguided people who complain that cricket is becoming a business more than a game, as if that were not the most fortunate thing that could happen. When it ceases to be a mere business and becomes a religious ceremony, it will be a sign that the millennium is at hand. The person who regards cricket as anything less than a business is no fit companion, gentle reader, for the likes of you and me. As long as the game goes in his favour the cloven hoof may not show itself. But give him a good steady spell of leather-hunting, and you will know him for what he is, a mere dilettante, a dabbler, in a word, a worm, who ought never to be allowed to play at all. The worst of this species will sometimes take advantage of the fact that the game in which they happen to be playing is only a scratch game, upon the result of which no very great issues hang, to pollute the air they breathe with verbal, and the ground they stand on with physical, buffooneries. Many a time have I, and many a time have you, if you are what I take you for, shed tears of blood, at the sight of such. Careless returns, overthrows﻿—but enough of a painful subject. Let us pass on.

I have always thought it a better fate for a man to be born a bowler than a bat. A batsman certainly gets a considerable amount of innocent fun by snicking good fast balls just off his wicket to the ropes, and standing stolidly in front against slow leg-breaks. These things are good, and help one to sleep peacefully o’ nights, and enjoy one’s meals. But no batsman can experience that supreme emotion of “something attempted, something done,” which comes to a bowler when a ball pitches in a hole near point’s feet, and whips into the leg stump. It is one crowded second of glorious life. Again, the words “retired hurt” on the score-sheet are far more pleasant to the bowler than the batsman. The groan of a batsman when a loose ball hits him full pitch in the ribs is genuine. But the “Awfully-sorry-old-chap-it-slipped” of the bowler is not. Half a loaf is better than no bread, as Mr. Chamberlain might say, and if he cannot hit the wicket, he is perfectly contented with hitting the man. In my opinion, therefore, the bowler’s lot, in spite of billiard table wickets, red marl, and suchlike inventions of a degenerate age, is the happier one.

And here, glowing with pride of originality at the thought that I have written of cricket without mentioning Alfred Mynn or Fuller Pilch, I heave a reminiscent sigh, blot my MS., and thrust my pen back into its sheath.

===Chinese===
有些误入歧途的人抱怨，板球正日益成为一桩生意，而非一场游戏——仿佛这不是天下头一桩幸事似的。待到它不再止于生意、进而升格为宗教典礼之日，便是千禧年将至之兆。谁若把板球看得比生意还轻，温柔的读者，此人便配不上与你我这等人同席。只要比赛顺着他，那只偶蹄兴许还藏而不露。可只要让他结结实实追上一阵球皮（leather-hunting，指长时间在外野追球），你便认得他的真面目了：不过是个半吊子，一个浅尝辄止之徒，一句话，一条虫——压根儿就不该放他上场。此类中最恶劣者，有时还会钻这样一个空子：他们碰巧参加的不过是场临时凑合的野球赛，胜负无关宏旨——于是乎，用口头的插科打诨污染他们呼吸的空气，用肢体的胡闹把戏玷污他们脚下的地面。目睹这般光景，我曾多少次黯然泣血——你若如我所料，也曾多少次如此。随随便便的回传，一次次的掷偏——罢了，伤心事不必多提。我们且往下讲。

我一向认为，生为投球手，实比生为击球手命好。击球手固然也能捞到不少清白的乐趣：将好端端的快球贴着三柱门轻轻一蹭、送去边线围绳，或面对慢速腿转球，稳稳当当杵在门前不动。这些都是好事，能助人夜里安眠，吃饭香甜。但击球手永远无缘体验那种至高无上的情绪——「有所尝试，有所成」——当一颗球落在外侧守场员脚边的小坑里，随即一拐旋进腿侧柱门，这情绪便涌上投球手的心头。那是浓缩于一秒之内的辉煌人生。再者，记分册上「因伤退场」几个字，看在投球手眼里，远比看在击球手眼里受用。松垮的坏球直不隆咚砸中击球手的肋骨时，后者的呻吟出自肺腑；投球手那句「实在抱歉老伙计球脱手了」却不然。半个面包总比没有面包强——张伯伦先生（Mr. Chamberlain）大概会这么说——既然打不中柱门，打中了人，他也就心满意足。因此依我之见，投球手的命运——纵有台球桌般平整的球道、红泥灰土，以及这颓废时代的诸般发明作梗——终究是更幸福的那一份。

写到这里，一想到我通篇谈板球，竟未提及阿尔弗雷德·明恩（Alfred Mynn）或富勒·皮尔奇（Fuller Pilch），我不禁为首创精神灼灼生光，喟发一声怀旧之叹，吸干手稿上的墨迹，把笔插回鞘中。

## Pillingshot, Detective / 侦探皮林肖特

===Original===
Life at St. Austin’s was rendered somewhat hollow and burdensome for Pillingshot by the fact that he fagged for Scott. Not that Scott was the Beetle-Browed Bully in any way. Far from it. He showed a kindly interest in Pillingshot’s welfare, and sometimes even did his Latin verses for him. But the noblest natures have flaws, and Scott’s was no exception. He was by way of being a humorist, and Pillingshot, with his rather serious outlook on life, was puzzled and inconvenienced by this.

It was through this defect in Scott’s character that Pillingshot first became a detective.

He was toasting muffins at the study fire one evening, while Scott, seated on two chairs and five cushions, read *Sherlock Holmes*, when the Prefect laid down his book and fixed him with an earnest eye.

“Do you know, Pillingshot,” he said, “you’ve got a bright, intelligent face. I shouldn’t wonder if you weren’t rather clever. Why do you hide your light under a bushel?”

===Chinese===
皮林肖特（Pillingshot）给斯科特（Scott）当学仆（fag，伺候高年级学生的低年级生），这件事使他在圣奥斯汀（St. Austin's）的日子过得未免有些空虚而沉重。倒不是说斯科特在哪一点上算得上「浓眉恶霸」式的人物，远远谈不上。他对皮林肖特的福祉还表现出慈爱的关怀，有时甚至替他做拉丁韵文。然而最高贵的品性也有瑕疵，斯科特亦非例外。他算得上是一位幽默家，而皮林肖特对人生的看法相当严肃，为此既困惑又受累。

皮林肖特头一遭当上侦探，正是拜斯科特品性中的这一缺陷所赐。

一天晚上，他正在书房壁炉前烤松饼，斯科特高踞于两把椅子、五只靠垫之上，读着《福尔摩斯探案》（*Sherlock Holmes*）。忽而这位级长放下书，用恳切的目光盯住了他。

「你可知道，皮林肖特，」他说，「你长了一张机灵聪慧的脸。说你相当有头脑，我也不奇怪。你为什么把光藏在斗底下呢？」

===Original===
Pillingshot grunted.

“We must find some way of advertising you. Why don’t you go in for a Junior Scholarship?”

“Too old,” said Pillingshot with satisfaction.

“Senior, then?”

“Too young.”

===Chinese===
皮林肖特哼了一声。

「咱们得想个法子把你宣传出去。你怎么不去争取一下初级奖学金？」

「超龄了，」皮林肖特心满意足地说。

「那就争取高级的？」

「还没到岁数。」

===Original===
“I believe by sitting up all night and swotting—”

“Here, I say!” said Pillingshot, alarmed.

“You’ve got no enterprise,” said Scott sadly. “What are those? Muffins? Well, well, I suppose I had better try and peck a bit.”

He ate four in rapid succession, and resumed his scrutiny of Pillingshot’s countenance.

“The great thing,” he said, “is to find out your special line. Till then we are working in the dark. Perhaps it’s music? Singing? Sing me a bar or two.”

===Chinese===
「我相信只要通宵达旦、悬梁刺股地苦读——」

「喂，我说！」皮林肖特惊慌地说。

「你毫无进取精神，」斯科特悲哀地说。「那是什么？松饼？好吧好吧，我想我还是尝上一口的好。」

他风卷残云般接连吞下四只，随即重新端详起皮林肖特的脸来。

「头等大事，」他说，「是找出你的专门行当。在那之前，咱们全是暗中摸黑。也许是音乐？唱歌？给我来上一两小节。」

===Original===
Pillingshot wriggled uncomfortably.

“Left your music at home?” said Scott. “Never mind, then. Perhaps it’s all for the best. What are those? Still muffins? Hand me another. After all, one must keep one’s strength up. You can have one if you like.”

Pillingshot’s face brightened. He became more affable. He chatted.

“There’s rather a row on downstairs,” he said. “In the junior day-room.”

===Chinese===
皮林肖特不自在地扭动起来。

「乐谱忘在家里了？」斯科特说。「那就算了。没准儿这样反倒最好。那是什么？还是松饼？再递我一只。毕竟，人总得保住体力。你要是乐意，也可以吃一只。」

皮林肖特的脸明朗起来。他变得和气了，还攀谈起来。

「楼下正闹得挺凶，」他说。「在低年级活动室里。」

===Original===
“There always is,” said Scott. “If it grows too loud, I shall get in amongst them with a swagger-stick. I attribute half my success at bringing off late-cuts to the practice I have had in the junior day-room. It keeps the wrist supple.”

“I don’t mean that sort of row. It’s about Evans.”

“What about Evans?”

“He’s lost a sovereign.”

“Silly young ass.”

===Chinese===
「那儿从来都在闹，」斯科特说。「若是闹得过了分，我就提着短手杖到他们中间去走一遭。我打迟切球能得手，一半功劳得记在低年级活动室的操练上。那能叫手腕保持柔韧。」

「我不是说那种闹法。是埃文斯（Evans）的事。」

「埃文斯怎么了？」

「他丢了一枚沙弗林金币（sovereign，一镑金币）。」

「蠢驴一头。」

===Original===
Pillingshot furtively helped himself to another muffin.

“He thinks someone’s taken it,” he said.

“What! Stolen it?”

Pillingshot nodded.

“What makes him think that?”

“He doesn’t see how else it could have gone.”

===Chinese===
皮林肖特偷偷又摸了一只松饼。

「他疑心是有人拿了，」他说。

「什么！给人偷了？」

皮林肖特点点头。

「他凭什么这么想？」

「他想不出它还能怎么没的。」

===Original===
“Oh, I don’t—By Jove!”

Scott sat up with some excitement.

“I’ve got it,” he said. “I knew we should hit on it sooner or later. Here’s a field for your genius. You shall be a detective. Pillingshot, I hand this case over to you. I employ you.”

Pillingshot gaped.

===Chinese===
「噢，我可不——哎呀！」

斯科特带着几分兴奋坐直了身子。

「有了，」他说。「我早知道咱们迟早会撞上它。这正是你天才的用武之地。你就当一名侦探吧。皮林肖特，本案移交给你。本人雇用你。」

皮林肖特目瞪口呆。

===Original===
“I feel certain that’s your line. I’ve often noticed you walking over to school, looking exactly like a bloodhound. Get to work. As a start you’d better fetch Evans up here and question him.”

“But, look here—”

“Buck up, man, buck up. Don’t you know that every moment is precious?”

Evans, a small, stout youth, was not disposed to be reticent. The gist of his rambling statement was as follows. Rich uncle. Impecunious nephew. Visit of former to latter. Handsome tip, one sovereign. Impecunious nephew pouches sovereign, and it vanishes.

“And I call it beastly rot,” concluded Evans volubly. “And if I could find the cad who’s pinched it, I’d jolly well—”

===Chinese===
「我敢肯定那就是你的本行。我时常注意到你往学校走去的模样，活脱一条寻血猎犬。开工吧。头一步，你最好把埃文斯带到这儿来，盘问盘问。」

「可是，喂——」

「麻利点儿，伙计，麻利点儿。你难道不知道寸阴寸金么？」

埃文斯是个矮胖的小子，并无守口如瓶之意。他那东拉西扯的陈述，要点如下：阔叔叔；穷侄儿；前者驾临后者处；赏钱丰厚，计金币一枚；穷侄儿把金币收入囊中，金币随即失踪。

「而且我要说，这简直混账透顶，」埃文斯滔滔不绝地作结，「要是叫我逮着那个昧下金币的下流胚，我非好好地——」

===Original===
“Less of it,” said Scott. “Now, then, Pillingshot, I’ll begin this thing, just to start you off. What makes you think the quid has been stolen, Evans?”

“Because I jolly well know it has.”

“What you jolly well know isn’t evidence. We must thresh this thing out. To begin with, where did you last see it?”

“When I put it in my pocket.”

“Good. Make a note of that, Pillingshot. Where’s your notebook? Not got one? Here you are then. You can tear out the first few pages, the ones I’ve written on. Ready? Carry on, Evans. When?”

“When what?”

===Chinese===
「少来这套，」斯科特说。「那么，皮林肖特，这案子我来起个头，好引你上路。埃文斯，你凭什么认为那一镑钱是被人偷了？」

「因为我明明白白知道就是被偷了。」

「你明明白白知道的可算不得证据。这案子咱们必须彻底筛个水落石出。首先，你最后一次见到它是在哪儿？」

「我把它放进裤袋里的时候。」

「好。记下这一条，皮林肖特。你的记事本呢？没有？那就给你这本。头几页你可以撕掉，就是我写过字的那几页。就绪？继续，埃文斯。什么时候？」

「什么时候什么？」

===Original===
“When did you put it in your pocket?”

“Yesterday afternoon.”

“What time?”

“About five.”

“Same pair of bags you’re wearing now?”

“No, my cricket bags. I was playing at the nets when my uncle came.”

===Chinese===
「你什么时候把它放进口袋的？」

「昨天下午。」

「几点钟？」

「五点来钟。」

「跟你现在身上这条裤子是同一条么？」

「不是，是我那条板球裤。我叔叔来的时候，我正在球网那边打球。」

===Original===
“Ah! Cricket bags? Put it down, Pillingshot. That’s a clue. Work on it. Where are they?”

“They’ve gone to the wash.”

“About time, too. I noticed them. How do you know the quid didn’t go to the wash as well?”

“I turned both the pockets inside out.”

“Any hole in the pocket?”

“No.”

===Chinese===
「啊！板球裤？记下来，皮林肖特。这是条线索。顺着查下去。裤子如今在哪儿？」

「送去洗了。」

「也该洗了，我见过那条裤子。你怎么知道那一镑钱没一并进了洗衣房？」

「我把两只口袋都翻了个里朝外。」

「口袋上可有窟窿？」

「没有。」

===Original===
“Well, when did you take off the bags? Did you sleep in them?”

“I wore ’em till bedtime, and then shoved them on a chair by the side of the bed. It wasn’t till next morning that I remembered the quid was in them—”

“But it wasn’t,” objected Scott.

“I thought it was. It ought to have been.”

===Chinese===
「那么，你几时脱的裤子？莫不是穿着睡的？」

「我一直穿到就寝，然后把它们往床边一张椅子上一撂。直到第二天早上，我才想起那一镑钱还在里头——」

「可它并不在，」斯科特提出异议。

「我以为在。它本该在的。」

===Original===
“He thought it was. That’s a clue, young Pillingshot. Work on it. Well?”

“Well, when I went to take the quid out of my cricket bags, it wasn’t there.”

“What time was that?”

“Half-past seven this morning.”

“What time did you go to bed?”

“Ten.”

“Then the theft occurred between the hours of ten and seven-thirty. Mind you, I’m giving you a jolly good leg-up, young Pillingshot. But as it’s your first case I don’t mind. That’ll be all from you, Evans. Pop off.”

===Chinese===
「他以为在。这是一条线索，小皮林肖特。顺着查下去。然后呢？」

「然后，等我去板球裤里取那一镑钱的时候，它已经不在了。」

「那是几点的事？」

「今天早上七点半。」

「你几点上的床？」

「十点。」

「那么窃案便发生在十点至七点半之间。听好了，小皮林肖特，我可是大大地扶了你一把。不过念在你初办大案，不与你计较。你的供词录完了，埃文斯。去吧。」

===Original===
Evans disappeared. Scott turned to the detective.

“Well, young Pillingshot,” he said, “what do you make of it?”

“I don’t know.”

“What steps do you propose to take?”

“I don’t know.”

“You’re a lot of use, aren’t you? As a start, you’d better examine the scene of the robbery, I should say.”

Pillingshot reluctantly left the room.

===Chinese===
埃文斯退场。斯科特转向侦探。

「怎么样，小皮林肖特，」他说，「你对此有何见地？」

「我不知道。」

「你打算采取什么步骤？」

「我不知道。」

「你可真顶用，是不是？依我看，开局你最好先勘察一下失窃现场。」

皮林肖特老大不情愿地出了房门。

===Original===
“Well?” said Scott, when he returned. “Any clues?”

“No.”

“You thoroughly examined the scene of the robbery?”

“I looked under the bed.”
